18. Clostridioides difficile

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: hospital-acquired watery diarrhea after antibiotics (clindamycin, fluoroquinolones, cephalosporins, penicillins) disrupting normal flora.
  • Gram-positive, spore-forming, obligate anaerobic rod; spores resist heat, alcohol-based sanitizers, and drying.
  • Toxin A (enterotoxin) and Toxin B (cytotoxin; depolymerizes actin) cause pseudomembranous colitis with yellow-grey plaques.
  • Diagnosis: stool NAAT/PCR for toxin genes or EIA for toxins A/B (often GDH + toxin algorithm).
  • Treatment: oral vancomycin or fidaxomicin first-line; IV metronidazole only adjunctive in fulminant disease (with PO/PR vancomycin).
  • Prevention: soap-and-water handwashing (not alcohol gel), contact precautions, stop the offending antibiotic.
  1. 1Walnuts: Spores are metabolically inactive and highly resistant to heat, radiation, and chemical agents (including alcohol-based hand sanitizers). They persist on hospital surfaces and drive transmission.⭐ Alcohol gel does not kill spores; use soap and water.
  2. 2"Clean" sign with washing hands over chocolate machine: C. difficile is primarily nosocomial; spores spread patient to patient via contaminated hands and surfaces (fecal-oral). Antibiotics, especially clindamycin, fluoroquinolones, cephalosporins, and penicillins, disrupt normal gut flora, letting C. difficile overgrow and produce toxins that cause diarrhea.
  3. 3Workstation A and workstation B: C. difficile produces two major exotoxins, Toxin A (TcdA) and Toxin B (TcdB), which are responsible for disease. Both are glucosyltransferases that inactivate Rho GTPases in colonocytes.
  4. 4"A - Apples" sign: Toxin A is the enterotoxin: it binds the brush border of intestinal epithelial cells, causing fluid secretion, inflammation, and mucosal damage with watery diarrhea.
  5. 5Employee picking up the apple and brushing chocolate on them: Toxin A acts on the intestinal lining (apples brushed with chocolate): epithelial cell damage and inflammation lead to cell death and secretory watery diarrhea.
  6. 6Chocolate filled machine: Toxin A is mainly enterotoxic: it drives fluid secretion and mucosal injury, producing the watery diarrhea of early disease.
  7. 7"B - Black Licorice" sign: Toxin B is the cytotoxin: it depolymerizes actin filaments in enterocytes, causing cell rounding and death. Mucosal necrosis forms a yellowish-grey pseudomembrane of fibrin, mucus, and dead cells, characteristic of pseudomembranous colitis.⭐ Pseudomembranes seen on colonoscopy are the classic finding.
  8. 8Black Licorice: Toxin B (black licorice) disrupts the enterocyte actin cytoskeleton by depolymerizing actin, causing cell rounding and death.
  9. 9Little kid chewing them apart: Toxin B damage to the cytoskeleton (kid chewing licorice apart) causes enterocyte rounding, loss of the cell layer, and mucosal necrosis.
  10. 10Worker packing the licorice in sheets of yellowish plastic: Mucosal necrosis from toxin B yields the yellowish-grey pseudomembrane (yellow plastic sheets) of fibrin, mucus, and dead cells that defines pseudomembranous colitis. Severe disease can progress to toxic megacolon.
  11. 11Worker holding up chocolate covered shoe: Diagnose by stool testing for toxigenic strains: NAAT/PCR (detects toxin-encoding genes) or EIA for toxins A and B. Test only symptomatic patients with unformed stools.
  12. 12Gas mask: C. difficile is an obligate anaerobe (cannot grow in oxygen-rich environments), consistent with infection in the anaerobic colon.
  13. 13Violet: C. difficile is a gram-positive bacillus (violet on Gram stain) and a spore former.
  14. 14Van with the mouth like opening: Oral vancomycin is first-line therapy (fidaxomicin is an equally accepted first-line option). It stays in the gut lumen with minimal systemic absorption, directly reaching bacteria and toxins.⭐ IV vancomycin does not reach the colonic lumen and is useless for C. difficile.
  15. 15Train: IV metronidazole is used for severe or fulminant infection alongside vancomycin, as it achieves systemic levels that reach inflamed colonic tissue, complementing local gut-directed therapy.
Show the original legend picture

19. Clostridium botulinum

Exam snapshot
  • Classic clue: descending symmetric flaccid paralysis with ptosis, diplopia, dysphagia and dilated pupils after home-canned food or honey (infant).
  • Micro: gram-positive, spore-forming obligate anaerobe; heat-resistant spores; toxin is heat labile.
  • Diagnosis: clinical; toxin detection in serum, stool or food (mouse bioassay/PCR).
  • Treatment/prevention: antitoxin (BabyBIG for infants), supportive care and ventilation; no honey under 12 months.
  • Buzzwords: floppy baby, descending paralysis, SNARE cleavage, canned food, honey, Botox.
  1. 1Robots: Clostridium botulinum (robots = botulinum, 'bot') is a gram-positive bacillus.
  2. 2Purple hues: Purple marks gram-positive staining: C. botulinum is a gram-positive rod.
  3. 3Tin can: Improperly canned or preserved food provides anaerobic conditions that allow C. botulinum spores to germinate and grow and produce toxin (home-canned vegetables, fish).⭐ Home-canned food = botulism.
  4. 4Nuts: C. botulinum forms metabolically inactive spores (nuts = hard shells) that resist heat, radiation and chemicals. The spores survive; the preformed toxin is heat labile and destroyed by boiling.⭐ Spores survive boiling, toxin does not.
  5. 5Anaerobic gas mask: C. botulinum is an obligate anaerobe, so it grows in low-oxygen environments (sealed cans, deep wounds).
  6. 6Limp neck: Botulism starts with cranial nerve (bulbar) dysfunction and progresses to symmetric descending weakness of trunk, limbs and respiratory muscles, ending in flaccid paralysis; respiratory failure can occur.⭐ Descending paralysis (Guillain-Barre is ascending).
  7. 7Droopy eyelids: Early bulbar signs include ptosis and diplopia, also dysphagia, dysarthria and dilated, poorly reactive pupils; there is no sensory loss or fever.
  8. 8Cutting ACh current: Botulinum toxin is a protease that cleaves SNARE proteins, blocking presynaptic release of acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction, causing flaccid paralysis (this is the basis of Botox).⭐ Botulism blocks ACh release (flaccid); tetanus blocks inhibitory release (spastic).
  9. 9Floppy baby robot: Infant botulism ('floppy baby syndrome') results from ingesting spores that colonize the immature gut and make toxin in vivo: constipation, poor feeding, weak cry, hypotonia. Adults usually get botulism from ingesting preformed toxin in food (wound botulism also occurs, e.g., injection drug use).⭐ Infant botulism = colonization by spores; adult foodborne = preformed toxin.
  10. 10Nuts in honey: Honey can contain C. botulinum spores, so it should not be given to infants younger than 12 months.⭐ No honey before age 1 year.
Show the original legend picture

20. Clostridium perfringens

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: dirty wound (trauma, war injury) with severe pain, crepitus, and rapidly spreading myonecrosis (gas gangrene)
  • Large gram-positive rod, spore-forming, anaerobic; box-car shaped; alpha toxin (lecithinase/phospholipase C) is the key virulence factor
  • Blood agar shows a double zone of hemolysis (inner theta toxin, outer alpha toxin)
  • Treatment: surgical debridement plus penicillin G (often with clindamycin)
  • Also causes food poisoning (meat/gravy; watery diarrhea, self-limited)
  1. 1Private Ringen: Clostridium perfringens is a large gram-positive rod, spore-forming and non-motile.
  2. 2Purple hues: C. perfringens is gram-positive (purple on Gram stain); it is a bacillus.
  3. 3Wounded soldier: C. perfringens infection occurs in wounds contaminated with soil or fecal matter, historically in battlefield injuries, and also with trauma, surgical wounds, or ischemic limbs.
  4. 4Spored walnuts: C. perfringens forms endospores, which are metabolically inactive and resistant to heat, radiation, and chemicals, allowing survival in harsh environments and in poorly cooked food.
  5. 5Soil: Spores persist in soil (and in the human and animal GI tract), resisting heat, radiation, desiccation, and disinfectants, which is how wounds and food get contaminated.
  6. 6Anaerobic gas mask: C. perfringens is an obligate (aerotolerant) anaerobe, so it grows in devitalized, poorly oxygenated tissue such as necrotic wounds.
  7. 7Gas vapor: C. perfringens causes necrotizing soft tissue infection with myonecrosis and gas formation (gas gangrene): severe pain, crepitus on palpation, foul discharge, and rapid spread with systemic toxicity.⭐ Crepitus + dirty wound = gas gangrene.
  8. 8Alpha flag + phospholipid clothespins: Alpha toxin is a lecithinase (phospholipase C) that cleaves lecithin (phosphatidylcholine) in cell membranes, causing myonecrosis and red cell hemolysis. Hemolysis and tissue destruction impair oxygen delivery.
  9. 9Lysed tomatoes: Alpha toxin (lecithinase) lyses red cells, causing intravascular hemolysis that impairs oxygen delivery to tissues; this contributes to anemia, jaundice, and renal failure in severe cases.
  10. 10"DOUBLE fine zone": On blood agar, C. perfringens produces a characteristic double zone of hemolysis: complete hemolysis in the inner zone (theta toxin) and incomplete hemolysis in the outer zone (alpha toxin).⭐ Double zone of hemolysis = C. perfringens.
  11. 11Purple pencil: Treatment is urgent surgical debridement (amputation if needed) plus antibiotics; penicillin G is preferred, usually with clindamycin (to suppress toxin production).
  12. 12"Slow" Sign in Muddy water: C. perfringens enterotoxin causes food poisoning from improperly cooked or stored foods (meats, gravy), with watery diarrhea and cramps and little vomiting or fever. Sketchy says onset is slow, within 2-12 h (classically 8-16 h); it is self-limited.⭐ Spores survive cooking, and enterotoxin is released in the gut during sporulation.
Show the original legend picture

21. Clostridium tetani

Exam snapshot
  • Classic clue: trismus (lockjaw), risus sardonicus, opisthotonos and painful spasms after a puncture wound in an unvaccinated or under-vaccinated person.
  • Micro: gram-positive, spore-forming obligate anaerobe (terminal spores); tetanospasmin blocks inhibitory neurotransmitter release.
  • Diagnosis: clinical.
  • Treatment/prevention: tetanus immune globulin, wound debridement, metronidazole, benzodiazepines/supportive care; tetanus toxoid vaccine (DTaP/Tdap).
  • Buzzwords: lockjaw, risus sardonicus, opisthotonos, rusty nail, Renshaw cells, GABA and glycine.
  1. 1Monkey testing lab: Clostridium tetani (tetanus; 'monkey'/test lab) is a gram-positive bacillus.
  2. 2Purple hues: Purple marks gram-positive staining: C. tetani is a gram-positive rod, often with a terminal spore ('tennis racket' or 'drumstick').
  3. 3Anaerobic gas mask: C. tetani is an obligate anaerobe; it grows in deep, poorly oxygenated tissue (e.g., necrotic puncture wounds).
  4. 4Spored walnuts: It forms metabolically inactive spores (walnut shells) that resist heat, radiation and chemicals and persist in the environment.⭐ Spores survive; vaccination, not sterilization of soil, is the protection.
  5. 5Barbed wire: Infection is classically associated with puncture wounds from barbed wire (deep wounds that contaminate tissue with spores and create low oxygen).
  6. 6Rusty nails: Rusty nails are another classic source: puncture wounds from contaminated metal. (Rust itself does not cause tetanus; the soil spores on the nail do.)
  7. 7Soil: C. tetani spores are found throughout soil (and animal feces), so wounds contaminated with dirt are at risk.⭐ Neonatal tetanus: contaminated umbilical stump.
  8. 8Evil grin: Classic signs: trismus (lockjaw, spasm of the masseters) and risus sardonicus, a sustained contraction of facial muscles producing a fixed 'evil smile'.
  9. 9Arched back: Spasms of back and neck muscles cause opisthotonos: backward arching of head, neck and spine.
  10. 10Retrograde pulley: Tetanospasmin enters the peripheral nerve at the wound and is carried by retrograde axonal transport to the spinal cord and brainstem.
  11. 11Cutting SNARE trap: Tetanospasmin is a protease that cleaves SNARE proteins (e.g., synaptobrevin), which are needed for synaptic vesicles to fuse with the neuronal membrane and release neurotransmitter.
  12. 12Trapped "G&G" researcher: In inhibitory interneurons (Renshaw cells) of the spinal cord, cleaving SNAREs blocks release of GABA and glycine (the inhibitory neurotransmitters; 'G and G' trapped).⭐ Tetanus: no inhibition, so spastic paralysis.
  13. 13Wrench and saw: Loss of inhibition means unopposed motor neuron firing: muscle spasms, spastic (rigid) paralysis and hyperreflexia (wrench and saw = tight, rigid muscles). This is the opposite of botulism's flaccid paralysis.⭐ Tetanus = spastic; botulism = flaccid.
  14. 14Orange toxoid syringe: Tetanus is prevented with the tetanus toxoid vaccine (inactivated toxin), given as DTaP in infants and children and Tdap in older children and adults, with Td boosters every 10 years. Unvaccinated patients with dirty wounds also get tetanus immune globulin.⭐ Vaccine = toxoid; tetanus immune globulin for unvaccinated patients with wounds.
Show the original legend picture

22. Coccidioides immitis

Exam snapshot
  • Classic clue: pneumonia with arthralgias and erythema nodosum ('desert bumps') after dust exposure in the Southwest US or northern Mexico.
  • Micro: thermally dimorphic fungus; mold with arthroconidia in soil, large spherules with endospores in tissue.
  • Diagnosis: spherules on KOH/histology, culture (lab hazard), serology (IgM acute, IgG/complement fixation).
  • Treatment: azoles (fluconazole or itraconazole) for mild-moderate disease; amphotericin B for severe or disseminated disease.
  • Buzzwords: San Joaquin Valley fever, desert rheumatism, spherules, erythema nodosum, meningitis, arthroconidia.
  1. 1"Presidio San Joaquin": Coccidioides immitis is a dimorphic fungus that causes coccidioidomycosis, also called San Joaquin Valley fever (the Presidio/San Joaquin icon). (C. posadasii is the related species in Arizona and Texas.)
  2. 2Southwest US map: It is endemic to the arid southwestern United States (California's San Joaquin Valley, Arizona) and northern Mexico, where it lives in desert soil.⭐ Southwest US desert = Coccidioides.
  3. 3Breathing in dust: Infection follows inhalation of airborne arthroconidia (mold spores).
  4. 4Cracks in earth: Arthroconidia become aerosolized when soil is disturbed by construction, farming or natural events such as earthquakes and dust storms (cracks in the earth).
  5. 5Dimorphic butterfly: Coccidioides is thermally dimorphic: mold (with arthroconidia) in the cooler environment (25-30 C), and a tissue form at body temperature (35-37 C). Unlike other dimorphic fungi, the tissue form is a spherule full of endospores rather than a budding yeast.
  6. 6Tumbleweeds: In tissue, Coccidioides appears as large spherules (tumbleweeds) filled with endospores. Spherules are much larger than red blood cells.⭐ Spherules with endospores = Coccidioides.
  7. 7Small red hat next to tumbleweed: The small red hat represents an RBC beside the tumbleweed: spherules are significantly larger than RBCs (about 20-80 micrometers).
  8. 8Coughing + sweating: Presentation ranges from asymptomatic to acute pneumonia with cough, fever and pleuritic chest pain. Diagnosis: KOH stain or histology (spherules), culture, and serology (IgM indicates acute infection; IgG and complement fixation for more chronic/disseminated disease).
  9. 9Knee pain: Arthralgia is a common feature of primary infection ('desert rheumatism'), along with fever, cough and chest pain.
  10. 10Holes in lung wall: Chest X-ray in primary disease may show cavities or nodules (holes in the wall).
  11. 11Protruding bricks in lung wall: Chest X-ray may also show unilateral infiltrates and hilar lymphadenopathy (bricks protruding from the wall); imaging is normal in many cases.
  12. 12Spotted leg wall: Erythema nodosum (painful red nodules on the shins from panniculitis) reflects a strong immune response to fungal antigens and is a favorable sign. Called 'desert bumps'.⭐ Erythema nodosum + arthralgia + cough in the Southwest = valley fever.
  13. 13Immunocompromised cane: Extrapulmonary (disseminated) disease (skin, bone, joint, CNS) mainly occurs in immunocompromised patients; also increased risk in pregnancy, diabetes and people of Filipino or African descent.
  14. 14Statue erosion over chest: Dissemination from the lungs can involve skin, bone and meninges; the chest icon marks that the infection begins in the chest (lungs) and spreads from there.
  15. 15Statue erosion over skin: Skin dissemination causes papules, pustules, ulcers or nodules.
  16. 16Exposed bone: Bone dissemination causes osteolytic lesions with localized pain, swelling and tenderness over the affected bones (spine, skull and long bones).
  17. 17Neck brace: CNS dissemination causes coccidioidal meningitis: headache, fever, neck stiffness, photophobia and altered mental status. It is chronic and often needs lifelong azole therapy.⭐ Coccidioides meningitis requires lifelong fluconazole.
  18. 18Pinecones: Azoles (itraconazole or fluconazole) are recommended for mild to moderate disease.
  19. 19Amphibians: Amphotericin B is the treatment of choice for severe or disseminated disease.

23. Coronavirus

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: cough, fever, dyspnea, myalgias, anosmia/ageusia; severe cases progress to viral pneumonia and ARDS.
  • Micro: enveloped, helical, positive-sense ssRNA virus (Coronaviridae) with S, M, E and N proteins; zoonotic (bats).
  • Diagnosis: RT-PCR (NAAT) on nasopharyngeal swab; antigen tests detect N protein; serology detects IgM/IgG/IgA.
  • Treatment/prevention: supportive care; N95 for aerosol-generating procedures; vaccination.
  • Buzzwords: crown (corona) spikes, ACE2 receptor, type II pneumocytes, cytokine storm, ARDS, MERS camels.
  1. 1Crown: Coronaviruses (family Coronaviridae) are named for the crown-like ring of spikes seen on electron microscopy ('corona' = crown).
  2. 2Warm colors: Warm colors encode RNA: coronaviruses have an RNA genome (single-stranded).
  3. 3Positive sun: The sun is a positive symbol: the RNA genome is positive-sense, so it can be translated directly like mRNA.⭐ Positive-sense RNA genome is infectious by itself and acts directly as mRNA.
  4. 4Sniffly commoner: Four low-pathogenicity human coronaviruses (229E, NL63, OC43, HKU1) cause the common cold and are endemic in humans.
  5. 5Evil advisor + bat: The high-pathogenicity coronaviruses (SARS-CoV, MERS-CoV, SARS-CoV-2) are zoonotic; bats are the primary reservoir host.
  6. 6Stars: Star icon = SARS-CoV (severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus), the cause of SARS.
  7. 7Mars: Mars icon = MERS-CoV (Middle East respiratory syndrome coronavirus), the cause of MERS.⭐ MERS is linked to camels in the Arabian Peninsula.
  8. 82 stars + 19: SARS-CoV-2 (two stars) is the cause of COVID-19 (coronavirus disease 2019).
  9. 9Helical N tower: Coronaviruses have helical nucleocapsids made of the nucleocapsid (N) protein bound to the RNA genome.⭐ Coronaviruses are the only positive-sense RNA viruses with a helical nucleocapsid.
  10. 10Enveloped by robe: Coronaviruses are enveloped: the capsid is surrounded by a lipid bilayer studded with viral glycoproteins.
  11. 11ME crown + S sceptre: The membrane (M), envelope (E) and spike (S) proteins are embedded in the coronavirus envelope.
  12. 12S sceptre decorated with glycans: The coronavirus S (spike) glycoprotein is heavily glycosylated, which helps shield it from antibody recognition.
  13. 13Ace and 2 (ace of cards): At the host cell surface, SARS-CoV-2 binds angiotensin-converting enzyme 2 (ACE2) to gain entry (ace = ACE).
  14. 142 of clubs: The '2' of the card pair: ACE2 is highly expressed on type II alveolar cells (type II pneumocytes), explaining lung tropism.⭐ Type II pneumocytes (surfactant producers) express ACE2.
  15. 15Hitting cards with S sceptre: The S protein binds ACE2, causing a conformational change that allows fusion and viral entry (host proteases such as TMPRSS2 prime S).
  16. 16Genomic sun necklace + two large meats: The replicase gene (ORF1a/1b) of the positive-sense genomic RNA is translated directly by host ribosomes into two long polyproteins.
  17. 17Cleaver in meat: Viral proteases (e.g., main protease and papain-like protease) autoproteolytically cleave the two polyproteins into individual functional proteins, including RdRp.⭐ Main protease (3CLpro/Mpro) is the target of nirmatrelvir (Paxlovid).
  18. 18Sun necklace + necklace molds + new necklaces: RNA-dependent RNA polymerase (RdRp) copies positive-sense genomic RNA into full-length negative-sense RNA, which then serves as the template for new positive-sense genomes of progeny virions.⭐ Remdesivir inhibits RdRp.
  19. 19SMEN bracelets: The viral genome also yields subgenomic RNAs (nested set) from which the S, M, E and N proteins are translated.
  20. 20S sceptre + ME crown + Golgi goldfish: The S, M and E proteins are inserted into the ER and transported to the membrane of the ER-Golgi intermediate compartment/Golgi.
  21. 21N tower in fishbowl: The nucleocapsid forms in the cytoplasm as N protein subunits associate with new genomic RNA.
  22. 22Goldfish blowing bubbles: The nucleocapsid joins the assembling virus, and a new enveloped virion buds into the ER-Golgi intermediate compartment (ERGIC) lumen.
  23. 23Budding bubbles: Progeny virions are carried to the cell membrane in membranous vesicles and released by exocytosis.
  24. 24Cough droplets: SARS-CoV-2 spreads via respiratory droplets and, in some settings, airborne (aerosol) transmission.
  25. 25kNight 95 + mask: Because of possible airborne spread, an N95 respirator (or equivalent) is recommended for healthcare workers during aerosol-generating procedures; surgical masks suffice for most routine care.⭐ N95 for aerosol-generating procedures.
  26. 26Commoner's tissue: The low-pathogenicity coronaviruses cause mild upper respiratory illness (common cold).
  27. 27Tapestry with Stars, Mars, and 2 stars + 19: The high-pathogenicity coronaviruses (SARS-CoV, MERS-CoV, SARS-CoV-2) can cause potentially fatal pneumonia.
  28. 28Happy painting: Some people infected with SARS-CoV-2 have no symptoms (asymptomatic carriers) but can still transmit the virus.
  29. 29Coughing: COVID-19 commonly presents with cough and fever; other symptoms include dyspnea, fatigue, myalgias, headache, sore throat, nasal congestion, nausea, vomiting and diarrhea.
  30. 30Flame feather: The second icon for the same symptom list highlights the loss of taste (ageusia) and smell (anosmia), a characteristic feature of COVID-19 (along with the fever, fatigue and GI symptoms listed).⭐ Sudden anosmia/ageusia is a clue for COVID-19.check original
  31. 31Dirty lung chestplate: Severe COVID-19 causes viral pneumonia (bilateral ground-glass opacities); in some patients a cytokine storm drives progression to ARDS and respiratory failure requiring mechanical ventilation.check original
  32. 32ARDS shark: The shark icon emphasizes the progression to acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS): diffuse alveolar damage with hypoxemia and respiratory failure, often needing ventilation.⭐ ARDS: bilateral infiltrates, PaO2/FiO2 <= 300 with PEEP, normal wedge pressure.
  33. 33Gnaats + NP swab spear: Nucleic acid amplification tests (NAATs, e.g., RT-PCR) on nasopharyngeal swabs diagnose active infection by detecting viral RNA.⭐ RT-PCR on nasopharyngeal swab is the gold-standard diagnostic.
  34. 34N tower shaped helmet: Antigen tests detect viral protein (most often the nucleocapsid N protein) rather than RNA; they are rapid but less sensitive than NAAT.
  35. 35Antibody arrows: Serologic assays (ELISA, CLIA, lateral-flow rapid antibody tests) detect IgM, IgG or IgA antibodies to viral proteins such as spike or nucleocapsid, indicating past infection or vaccination, not active infection.

24. Corynebacterium diphtheriae

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: pharyngitis with gray pseudomembrane, bull neck, myocarditis, later palatal/cranial neuropathy.
  • Micro: gram-positive club-shaped rod in V/Y 'Chinese letter' arrangements; metachromatic (volutin) granules; lysogenic phage carries tox gene.
  • Diagnosis: Loeffler or tellurite (gray-black colonies) culture; Elek test for toxin production.
  • Treatment/prevention: antitoxin plus penicillin or erythromycin; DTaP/Tdap toxoid vaccine.
  • Buzzwords: pseudomembrane, bull neck, ADP-ribosylation of EF-2, Albert stain, Elek test.
  1. 1Bull: Corynebacterium diphtheriae (bull = 'bull neck'), a gram-positive bacillus.
  2. 2Purple hues: Purple = gram-positive staining: C. diphtheriae is a gram-positive bacillus.
  3. 3V + Y design on maracas: C. diphtheriae forms club-shaped rods arranged in V and Y shapes ('Chinese letters') because of snapping division after cell replication.
  4. 4Red and blue maracas: The cytoplasm contains metachromatic (volutin) granules, polyphosphate stores that stain red/dark with aniline dyes (e.g., Albert or methylene blue stain) while the rest of the bacillus stains blue.⭐ Albert stain highlights metachromatic granules.
  5. 5Bowtie + elongated accordion: Diphtheria toxin (an A-B exotoxin encoded by a lysogenic beta phage) ADP-ribosylates elongation factor-2 (EF-2), inhibiting protein synthesis; Pseudomonas exotoxin A works by the same mechanism.⭐ Diphtheria toxin and Pseudomonas exotoxin A both inactivate EF-2.
  6. 6Grey cotton candy + plastic wrap: Toxin causes epithelial cell necrosis; fibrin, dead cells and bacteria form a dense gray pseudomembrane over the tonsils and pharynx that bleeds if scraped.⭐ Gray pseudomembrane that bleeds when scraped.
  7. 7Bull's neck: Severe disease with massive cervical lymphadenopathy and soft-tissue edema produces the 'bull neck'; airway obstruction can occur from laryngeal/pharyngeal membrane.
  8. 8Water droplets: C. diphtheriae is transmitted mainly by respiratory droplets.
  9. 9Heart cape: Systemic absorption of toxin causes cardiotoxicity, most often myocarditis and arrhythmias, a major cause of death.⭐ Myocarditis is the leading cause of death.
  10. 10Myelin sausages + back of throat: Toxin can cause demyelinating neuropathy, classically beginning with palatal and posterior pharyngeal paralysis, followed later by other cranial and peripheral nerve involvement.
  11. 11Black "TELE": Tellurite agar (cystine-tellurite) is a selective medium; C. diphtheriae reduces tellurite to form gray/black colonies.
  12. 12Laughing: Loeffler's medium (Loeffler's serum slant) promotes growth and enhances the bacterium's morphology (metachromatic granules), easing identification.
  13. 13Tongue lick: The Elek test (immunodiffusion) distinguishes toxigenic from non-toxigenic C. diphtheriae strains by detecting toxin precipitin lines.⭐ Elek test = toxin production.
  14. 14Toxoid syringes: The vaccine is an inactivated toxin (toxoid), usually combined with tetanus toxoid and acellular pertussis (DTaP in children, Tdap in adolescents/adults; DT and Td without pertussis).⭐ Toxoid vaccine induces antitoxin antibodies; treat active disease with antitoxin plus an antibiotic (penicillin or erythromycin).

25. Coxiella burnetii

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: acute Q fever - flu-like illness with high fever, headache, dry cough, atypical pneumonia or hepatitis; NO rash.
  • Micro: obligate intracellular, gram-negative (poorly staining) coccobacillus; spore-like small cell variant; aerosol transmission from livestock birth products.
  • Diagnosis: serology (phase I/II antibodies) or PCR; Gram stain not helpful.
  • Treatment/prevention: doxycycline; chronic disease needs doxycycline plus hydroxychloroquine; vaccine for high-risk workers; pasteurize milk.
  • Buzzwords: Q fever, no arthropod vector, sheep/cattle/goats, culture-negative endocarditis, granulomatous hepatitis.
  1. 1Curly Q the ram: Coxiella burnetii, the cause of Q fever (the 'Q' ram); it is a gram-negative (or gram-variable) bacterium.
  2. 2Red barn: Red = gram-negative: C. burnetii stains poorly and variably because of its unusual cell wall, but is classified as gram-negative.
  3. 3White fur: White fur = no rash: unlike other zoonotic obligate intracellular bacteria (Rickettsia, Ehrlichia, Orientia), Q fever typically has no rash.⭐ Q fever = no rash and no arthropod vector (unlike rickettsiae).
  4. 4"Q" horn: C. burnetii causes Q fever, typically mild flu-like illness (fever, headache, myalgia, malaise) that can progress to atypical pneumonia or hepatitis.
  5. 5Locked indoors: C. burnetii is an obligate intracellular organism that survives and multiplies within host cells, notably macrophages (inside acidic phagolysosomes).
  6. 6Walnuts: C. burnetii forms spore-like small-cell variants that resist heat, drying and disinfectants and persist in the environment.⭐ Spore-like forms are very hardy in the environment.
  7. 7Animal droppings: The hardy organism persists in animal waste (birth products, urine, feces, milk); the birth products of infected livestock are particularly infectious.
  8. 8Inhaling dust clouds: Contaminated material becomes aerosolized dust; inhalation is the primary route of human infection (no tick vector needed).⭐ Inhalation of aerosols from livestock = classic route.
  9. 9Hitting head: Q fever commonly presents with nonspecific symptoms; this icon represents the severe headache.
  10. 10Coughing: The same nonspecific symptom set includes a dry cough, reflecting atypical pneumonia.
  11. 11Sweating: Fever is a consistent, defining feature of Q fever (often high with sweats).
  12. 12Cow: Cattle, sheep and goats are major reservoirs. Prevention: avoid birth products and waste, use protective equipment, pasteurize milk and vaccinate high-risk individuals (e.g., veterinarians).
  13. 13Liver-shaped spot: Q fever can cause granulomatous hepatitis (granulomas often with a fibrin ring, 'doughnut granulomas') with mildly elevated liver enzymes and nonspecific symptoms.⭐ Doughnut (fibrin-ring) granulomas in liver.
  14. 14Red fingers: Chronic Q fever can cause culture-negative endocarditis, especially in patients with valvular disease; peripheral signs include splinter hemorrhages.⭐ Culture-negative endocarditis in a patient exposed to livestock suggests Coxiella.

26. Coxsackievirus A & B

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: A - hand-foot-mouth disease, herpangina; B - myopericarditis, Bornholm disease (pleurodynia); both - aseptic meningitis.
  • Micro: naked, icosahedral, positive-sense ssRNA virus (Picornaviridae, enterovirus); fecal-oral.
  • Diagnosis: clinical; RT-PCR of stool, throat or CSF for enterovirus.
  • Treatment/prevention: supportive care; hand hygiene.
  • Buzzwords: hand-foot-mouth, herpangina, devil's grip, dilated cardiomyopathy, summer-fall children.
  1. 1Cockatoos: Coxsackievirus (cockatoo) A and B are positive-sense RNA viruses in the family Picornaviridae (enteroviruses).
  2. 2Warm colors: Warm colors encode RNA: coxsackieviruses have an RNA genome.
  3. 3Positive sun: Positive sun = positive-sense RNA genome, directly translated by host ribosomes.
  4. 4Baby cockatoo: 'Pico' (small) RNA virus: coxsackieviruses are members of the Picornaviridae family.
  5. 5Statue of David: The nude statue means a naked virus: coxsackieviruses lack an envelope, making them acid-stable and resistant to detergents, so they survive the stomach and spread fecal-orally.
  6. 6"A" cage: Cage A represents Coxsackievirus A, a positive-sense RNA picornavirus.
  7. 7Hand, foot, mouth red birdseed: Coxsackievirus A causes hand-foot-and-mouth disease (small red vesicles/macules on mouth, palms and soles, non-itchy) and herpangina (small vesicles or ulcers on soft palate, tonsillar pillars and uvula with sudden fever and sore throat); both are self-limited and treated supportively.⭐ Hand-foot-mouth and herpangina = Coxsackie A.
  8. 8Meningitis helmet: Coxsackievirus A (and less commonly B) can cause aseptic (viral) meningitis, especially in children, by crossing the blood-brain or blood-CSF barrier.⭐ Enteroviruses are the most common cause of aseptic meningitis.
  9. 9Kid + bathing suit: Coxsackieviruses mainly affect children, with a seasonal peak in summer and early fall.
  10. 10"B" cage: Cage B represents Coxsackievirus B, a positive-sense RNA picornavirus.
  11. 11Floppy heart bags: Coxsackievirus B causes myopericarditis by directly infecting cardiomyocytes and triggering an immune response, causing chest pain, fatigue, dyspnea or arrhythmias; chronic cases can progress to dilated cardiomyopathy and heart failure.⭐ Coxsackie B is the most common viral cause of myocarditis.
  12. 12Tight grip around chest: Coxsackievirus B causes Bornholm disease (epidemic pleurodynia, 'devil's grip'): sudden, severe pleuritic chest or upper abdominal pain, sometimes with fever and muscle tenderness; self-limited and treated supportively.⭐ Devil's grip = pleurodynia.

27. Cryptococcus neoformans

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: subacute meningoencephalitis (headache, fever, neck stiffness, high opening pressure) in HIV patients with CD4 < 100; also pulmonary infection.
  • Micro: encapsulated yeast (narrow-based budding), urease positive; found in pigeon droppings and soil.
  • Diagnosis: India ink (halo), mucicarmine stain, latex agglutination/cryptococcal antigen in CSF or serum.
  • Treatment/prevention: amphotericin B + flucytosine induction, then fluconazole.
  • Buzzwords: soap bubbles on MRI, pigeon droppings, India ink, mucicarmine, polysaccharide capsule.
  1. 1Crypt: Cryptococcus neoformans (crypt = 'crypto'), an opportunistic encapsulated yeast.
  2. 2Thick-walled sarcophagus: The sarcophagus represents the thick polysaccharide capsule, which inhibits phagocytosis, enhances virulence and is the main diagnostic target.⭐ Capsule = main virulence factor.
  3. 3Hexagon design: The repeating pattern on the sarcophagus represents the repeating polysaccharide capsular antigen (glucuronoxylomannan), which is antiphagocytic and detectable by antigen tests.
  4. 4Pigeon poop + archeologist coughing: C. neoformans is associated with pigeon droppings and contaminated soil; inhaling the dust leads to a primary pulmonary infection that may disseminate, especially to the CNS.⭐ Pigeon droppings are the classic exposure.
  5. 5Ammonia bottle: C. neoformans is urease positive, hydrolyzing urea to CO2 and ammonia.
  6. 6Immunocompromised cane: The cane marks an immunocompromised patient: cryptococcosis is an opportunistic infection, classically in HIV/AIDS (CD4 < 100) and with transplant or steroid therapy.⭐ Most common fungal meningitis in AIDS.
  7. 7Archeologist coughing: Pulmonary cryptococcosis may be asymptomatic, but about half of patients have cough or chest pain; dyspnea can occur and disease is more severe in immunocompromised hosts.
  8. 8Neck brace + sweat: Cryptococcus can disseminate to the CNS, causing meningoencephalitis (fever, malaise, headache, neck stiffness, nausea, vomiting, photophobia); CSF shows elevated opening pressure, lymphocytic pleocytosis, high protein and low-to-normal glucose.⭐ Markedly elevated opening pressure is characteristic.
  9. 9Soap bubbles on chest: The 'soap bubbles' on the chest mark respiratory samples: bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid from bronchoscopy can be stained to detect Cryptococcus in the lungs (the soap-bubble look refers to the yeast in tissue).check original
  10. 10Red and silver: On BAL specimens, mucicarmine (red) stains the polysaccharide capsule and Gomori methenamine silver (GMS) stains the yeast cell wall, confirming pulmonary cryptococcosis.⭐ Mucicarmine stains the capsule red.
  11. 11Light circles in black tar: India ink stain of CSF (from lumbar puncture) is a negative stain: the capsule shows as a clear halo around the yeast against a dark background, diagnosing cryptococcal meningitis.⭐ India ink = clear halo around yeast.
  12. 12Latex gloves: The latex agglutination (cryptococcal antigen) test detects capsular polysaccharide antigen in serum and CSF using antibody-coated latex beads that clump when antigen is present; it is highly sensitive.
  13. 13Soap bubbles on head: Cryptococcal meningoencephalitis can show 'soap bubble' lesions on MRI: multiple small cystic non-enhancing lesions in the basal ganglia and periventricular gray matter, representing yeast-filled dilated perivascular (Virchow-Robin) spaces.⭐ Soap-bubble lesions in basal ganglia.
  14. 14Frogs and flute: Treatment of cryptococcal meningitis begins with induction therapy: amphotericin B (frogs, 'ribbit' for -ampho) plus flucytosine (flute, 'flu-cy').⭐ Induction: amphotericin B + flucytosine.
  15. 15Pinecones: After induction, long-term consolidation and maintenance therapy uses fluconazole (the pinecones stand for the following 'cones'-azole step).⭐ Then fluconazole consolidation/maintenance.check original
Show the original legend picture

28. Cryptosporidium spp

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: watery, non-bloody diarrhea; self-limited in normal hosts but severe and chronic in AIDS (CD4 <100).
  • Diagnosis: oocysts in stool, acid-fast (modified Ziehl-Neelsen) pink-red on blue; antigen or PCR also used.
  • Transmission: fecal-oral; contaminated water (pools, lakes, municipal supply) or animal contact; oocysts resist chlorine.
  • Treatment: nitazoxanide for immunocompetent patients; in HIV, restore immunity with ART plus supportive care.
  • Classic association: outbreaks at swimming pools/daycares and in HIV patients with chronic watery diarrhea.
  1. 1Flooded crypt: Cryptosporidium spp. are intestinal protozoa that infect the small-bowel epithelium and cause watery diarrhea. Mnemonic: 'crypt' for Cryptosporidium, flooded because of the profuse watery diarrhea.
  2. 2Bubbles: Infectious oocysts are shed in stool and spread fecal-orally, typically by oocyst-contaminated water or food. They are environmentally hardy and survive for long periods.
  3. 3Multifaceted amethyst gems scattered in water: Oocysts contain four sporozoites and are small (about 4-6 micrometers). They are acid-fast or partially acid-fast on modified Ziehl-Neelsen stain (pink-red on a blue background), which allows stool microscopy diagnosis.
  4. 4Pink acid-fast poncho: Modified acid-fast staining of stool is the classic diagnostic test: pink-red oocysts stand out against the blue counterstain. Antigen detection or PCR can also be used.⭐ Acid-fast oocyst in stool + watery diarrhea = Cryptosporidium.
  5. 5Cowboy hat + wading through dirty water: Risk factors include drinking or swimming in contaminated water (pools, lakes, untreated water) and occupational or recreational contact with animals, especially cattle and calves. Daycare and swimming pool outbreaks are classic.
  6. 6Brown water: Cryptosporidium causes watery, non-bloody diarrhea. In immunocompetent hosts it is self-limited, usually resolving in 1-2 weeks.
  7. 7Immunocompromised cane: In immunocompromised patients, particularly advanced HIV (CD4 below about 100), it causes severe, chronic, profuse watery diarrhea with dehydration and weight loss, and may involve the biliary tract.
  8. 8Broken pipe + leaking brown water: After ingestion, sporozoites invade the epithelial cells of the GI tract (the apical enterocyte, within the brush border), leading to diarrhea through direct cellular damage and malabsorption/secretory changes. The infection is non-invasive beyond the epithelium, so stool is not bloody.
  9. 9Knitted sock: Nitazoxanide is approved for cryptosporidiosis in immunocompetent patients and offers variable benefit in immunocompromised patients.
  10. 10Sock filter: Prevention depends on water filtration, because oocysts are resistant to chlorine and standard chemical disinfection. Hand hygiene and avoiding swimming while having diarrhea also reduce spread.⭐ Chlorine-resistant oocysts explain pool and municipal water outbreaks.
  11. 11Spirit crows: Spiramycin (a macrolide) may relieve diarrhea in some immunocompromised patients, but the key to resolution is immune restoration (ART in HIV) plus supportive care and rehydration. Paromomycin is another agent sometimes used.check original
Show the original legend picture

29. Cytomegalovirus (CMV)

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: mono-like illness (monospot negative); in transplant/AIDS - retinitis, esophagitis, colitis, pneumonitis; congenital - hearing loss, periventricular calcifications.
  • Micro: enveloped dsDNA herpesvirus (HHV-5); latent in monocytes/macrophages; owl's-eye intranuclear inclusions.
  • Diagnosis: PCR/serology; biopsy shows owl's-eye inclusions; dilated eye exam for retinitis.
  • Treatment/prevention: ganciclovir/valganciclovir; foscarnet if UL97 mutation resistance.
  • Buzzwords: owl's eye, pizza-pie retinitis, linear esophageal ulcers, periventricular calcifications, #1 congenital infection.
  1. 1"Mega-Lo prices!": Cytomegalovirus (CMV), a DNA virus in Herpesviridae; spread by blood, sex, breast milk, saliva, urine and vertically (mother to fetus).
  2. 2Cool colors: Cool colors encode DNA: CMV is a DNA virus (double-stranded, enveloped).
  3. 3Hermes toys: Hermes = herpes: CMV is a member of the Herpesviridae family (HHV-5).
  4. 4Sleepings: Sleeping figures represent latency: CMV establishes latency in mononuclear myeloid cells (monocytes, macrophages, dendritic cells).
  5. 5Macro-cages: The 'macro' cages reinforce that latent CMV resides in monocytes and macrophages.
  6. 6Torches: CMV belongs to the TORCHeS group of congenital infections: Toxoplasma, Other (VZV, parvovirus B19, Zika, HIV), Rubella, CMV, HSV, Syphilis; all cross the placenta.⭐ TORCHeS infections cross the placenta.
  7. 7Epstein's Beer + back of throat: CMV can cause a mononucleosis-like illness similar to EBV: fever, sore throat, lymphadenopathy and fatigue.
  8. 8"No Mo' Spot" detergent: The monospot (heterophile) test is typically negative in CMV mononucleosis but positive in EBV; PCR and serology are used to diagnose either.⭐ Mono-like illness with negative monospot suggests CMV.
  9. 9Waking man with immunocompromised cane: In immunocompromised patients, latent CMV can reactivate.
  10. 10Solid organs: Transplant recipients (solid organ, bone marrow, stem cell) are at high risk of active CMV infection (reactivation or from donor organ).
  11. 11Coughing + lung stains: Transplant patients are at risk of CMV pneumonitis, and also hepatitis, gastritis, colitis and encephalitis.⭐ CMV pneumonitis is a leading infectious cause of death after bone marrow transplant.
  12. 12Immunocompromised cane: AIDS patients with low CD4 counts (below 50 cells/mm3) are especially vulnerable to CMV disease.
  13. 13"Charity drive 50 cents": The 50 cents is the CD4 threshold: CMV disease occurs when CD4+ count falls below 50.⭐ CMV disease at CD4 < 50.
  14. 14AIDS ribbons: The AIDS ribbons mark AIDS patients with advanced HIV (CD4 < 50 cells/mm3) at risk of CMV disease.
  15. 15Retina pizza: CMV retinitis in advanced AIDS (CD4 < 50): painless progressive vision loss; fundoscopy shows hemorrhages with fluffy yellow-white exudates ('pizza pie' or 'cheese and ketchup' retinopathy); untreated it leads to retinal necrosis and blindness.⭐ Most common cause of blindness in AIDS.
  16. 16Linear tears in conveyor belt: CMV esophagitis in HIV patients causes odynophagia with a few large, deep, linear ulcers (HSV causes multiple shallow punched-out ulcers; Candida causes white plaques).⭐ CMV = linear ulcers; HSV = punched-out ulcers.
  17. 17Torn mucosal bags: CMV colitis causes ulcerative colonic lesions with abdominal pain, hematochezia and diarrhea.
  18. 18Owl-O Cereal: Histology shows 'owl's eye' inclusions: enlarged cells with large nuclei containing a basophilic intranuclear inclusion surrounded by a clear halo.⭐ Owl's-eye intranuclear inclusions.
  19. 19Blueberry muffins: Congenital CMV can cause 'blueberry muffin' rash: purpuric, non-blanching lesions from extramedullary hematopoiesis (due to anemia or marrow suppression); thrombocytopenic petechiae may also occur; not specific to CMV and seen in other TORCHeS infections (e.g., rubella).
  20. 20Yellow cow + spleen and liver spots: Congenital CMV causes jaundice (hepatitis with mixed hyperbilirubinemia) and hepatosplenomegaly from viral replication, inflammation and extramedullary hematopoiesis.
  21. 21Covering ears: Congenital CMV causes sensorineural hearing loss because the virus damages the developing cochlea and auditory nerve; deficits are permanent and may be progressive.
  22. 22Large helmet vents: Congenital CMV can cause ventriculomegaly: brain infection and loss of parenchyma and impaired CSF circulation enlarge the ventricles.
  23. 23Milk on head: Congenital CMV causes periventricular calcifications (death of neural progenitor cells with dystrophic calcification); congenital toxoplasmosis instead tends to cause scattered, diffuse calcifications.⭐ CMV = periventricular; Toxoplasma = diffuse.
  24. 24"Expect DELAYS": Structural brain damage (calcifications, ventriculomegaly, cortical malformations such as microcephaly) disrupts neuronal connectivity, causing developmental delay and intellectual disability.
  25. 25Shake lines: The shaking lines represent seizures, another consequence of CMV structural brain damage.
  26. 26#1 shirt: Congenital CMV is the #1 infectious cause of sensorineural hearing loss and a leading infectious cause of intellectual disability.⭐ Most common congenital infection.
  27. 27Balloon bear in water: Congenital CMV can cause hydrops fetalis: fluid in two or more fetal compartments due to severe anemia, liver dysfunction and low plasma proteins (low oncotic pressure).
  28. 28"80-90% off!": 80-90% of newborns with congenital CMV are asymptomatic at birth (though they may develop hearing loss later).
  29. 29Green recycling bin: Ganciclovir (and oral valganciclovir) are first-line antivirals for treatment and prevention of CMV; they inhibit viral DNA polymerase after phosphorylation by the viral UL97 kinase.⭐ Ganciclovir toxicity: bone marrow suppression.
  30. 30"UL97" sticker + fast car net: UL97 gene mutation (the viral kinase that activates ganciclovir) causes ganciclovir resistance; foscarnet, which needs no activation, is used.⭐ Ganciclovir-resistant CMV: foscarnet.

30. Dermatophytes (Epidermophyton, Trichophyton, Microsporum)

Exam snapshot
  • Tinea (ringworm): red, scaly, itchy, annular lesions with raised edges and central clearing on skin, scalp, groin, feet, or nails.
  • Filamentous fungi that infect keratinized tissue (skin, hair, nails): Epidermophyton, Trichophyton, Microsporum; not normal flora.
  • KOH microscopy shows septate hyphae; Microsporum fluoresces under Wood's lamp; culture on Sabouraud agar.
  • Topical azoles or terbinafine for skin; oral terbinafine, griseofulvin, or azoles for nails, scalp, or severe disease.
  • Ringworm without worms, tinea capitis/corporis/cruris/pedis/unguium, Wood's lamp, griseofulvin binds keratin.
  1. 1Tin man: Dermatophytes are fungi that infect keratinized tissues (skin, hair, nails), causing tinea, or ringworm, so named for the red circular lesions, although no worm is involved.
  2. 2Rings of rust on tin man: Same point: tinea lesions are red, annular (ring-shaped) plaques, hence 'ringworm'.
  3. 3"E," "T," and "M" munchkins: The main dermatophyte genera are Epidermophyton, Trichophyton, and Microsporum.⭐ Trichophyton is the most common cause of tinea.
  4. 4Naked munchkins: Dermatophytes are pathogens, not part of the normal skin flora.
  5. 5Dog: Transmission is by direct contact with infected animals or humans, or indirectly through shared environments and fomites.⭐ Microsporum canis is acquired from pets.
  6. 6Rings on hat: Tinea capitis: infection of the scalp and hair, mainly in children, with patchy hair loss, scaling, broken hairs, and sometimes inflammation.⭐ Treat with oral therapy (griseofulvin or terbinafine); topicals are not enough.
  7. 7Rings on groin: Tinea cruris (jock itch): red, itchy, often ring-shaped rash of the groin and inner thighs; more common with warmth, moisture, sweating, or tight clothing.
  8. 8Rings on feet: Tinea pedis (athlete's foot): scaling, redness, itching, and sometimes cracking or blisters, especially between the toes; common in locker rooms, showers, and tight shoes.
  9. 9Rings on body: Tinea corporis: red, scaly, ring-shaped patches with raised edges and central clearing, on any part of the body; seen in wrestlers (tinea gladiatorum).
  10. 10Athletic head band: Athletes are at higher risk because of close contact, shared equipment, and warm, moist environments (swimmers, wrestlers); common infections include tinea corporis, pedis, and cruris.
  11. 11Scratching: Dermatophyte infections typically cause a red, itchy, scaly rash, often with raised edges and central clearing.
  12. 12KOH salt: KOH microscopy of scrapings reveals septate hyphae and confirms the diagnosis.⭐ KOH dissolves keratinocytes so hyphae are seen.
  13. 13Lamps + "M" munchkin: A Wood's (UV) lamp can detect Microsporum species, which fluoresce.⭐ Microsporum fluoresces green; Trichophyton generally does not (except T. schoenleinii).
  14. 14Pinecones: Topical azoles are commonly used to treat dermatophyte skin infections.⭐ Topical terbinafine is also effective.
  15. 15Biting nails: Onychomycosis (tinea unguium) is a fungal nail infection causing thickening, discoloration, brittleness, and separation from the nail bed; it can affect fingernails or toenails.
  16. 16Turban + biting nails: Oral antifungals (terbinafine, griseofulvin, or azoles) are used for onychomycosis, tinea capitis (turban), and severe or resistant skin infections.⭐ Terbinafine is first-line for onychomycosis; monitor liver enzymes.
  17. 17Grease + biting nails: Oral griseofulvin is used for more severe dermatophyte infections; it deposits in keratin-rich tissues (nails, hair, skin) but is often poorly tolerated due to GI side effects.⭐ Griseofulvin: microtubule inhibitor; hepatotoxic, teratogenic, CYP450 inducer.

31. Ebolavirus & Marburgvirus (Filoviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: abrupt fever, headache, myalgias after exposure to bats or an outbreak region, then GI symptoms, rash and hemorrhage.
  • Micro: enveloped, helical, nonsegmented negative-sense ssRNA virus (filamentous); Ebola and Marburg.
  • Diagnosis: RT-PCR on blood; handle under BSL-4 precautions.
  • Treatment/prevention: supportive care; Ebola Zaire has monoclonal antibodies (e.g. ansuvimab, atoltivimab/maftivimab/odesivimab) and a vaccine; strict PPE and isolation.
  • Buzzwords: hemorrhagic fever, DIC, shock, fruit bats, health care workers, body fluids, funeral transmission.
  1. 1Soccer field: Filoviruses (Ebola, Marburg) are RNA viruses.
  2. 2Warm colors: Filoviruses (Ebola, Marburg) are RNA viruses. (warm colors = RNA virus in the Sketchy color code).
  3. 3Moon with negative sign-shaped clouds: Filoviruses are negative-sense RNA viruses (the genome must be transcribed by a viral RNA-dependent RNA polymerase carried in the virion).⭐ Negative-sense RNA viruses carry their own RdRp in the virion.
  4. 4Orange spiral: The nucleocapsids of filoviruses have a helical structure (long filamentous virions).
  5. 5Soccer jerseys: Filoviruses are enveloped viruses.
  6. 6"Marburg" jersey: Marburg virus is a negative-sense RNA virus in the Filoviridae family.
  7. 7Gooooal!: Ebola virus is a negative-sense RNA virus in the Filoviridae family.
  8. 8Spotted jersey + red face: Marburg and Ebola infections begin abruptly with fever, headache, malaise and myalgias (flu-like), followed by nausea, vomiting, diarrhea and a maculopapular or petechial rash around day 5 as the disease progresses toward hemorrhagic features.
  9. 9Pool of blood: Filoviruses may progress to severe viral hemorrhagic fever with bleeding due to vascular leakage, DIC, mucosal and GI bleeding, multiorgan failure and shock; without supportive care mortality is extremely high.⭐ DIC with hemorrhagic fever is the classic cause of death.
  10. 10Kidney + liver blood spots: Same severe hemorrhagic fever picture: vascular leakage, DIC, bleeding, multiorgan (kidney and liver) failure and shock; mortality is extremely high without supportive care.
  11. 11Lightning bolt: Filoviruses may present with shock, typically mixed hypovolemic (fluid loss from vomiting, diarrhea, vascular leakage) and distributive (cytokine-mediated vasodilation, endothelial dysfunction), causing hypotension, tachycardia, oliguria and end-organ hypoperfusion.
  12. 12Monkeys: Fruit bats (Rousettus species for Marburg; several species for Ebola) are believed to be the natural reservoir; humans are infected through contact with infected bats, primates or other animals, followed by human-to-human spread via direct contact with blood or body fluids.⭐ Spread by direct contact with body fluids, not by aerosol.
  13. 13Bats: Fruit bats are the presumed natural reservoir for both viruses (Rousettus for Marburg), the source of spillover to humans and primates.
  14. 14Medics: Health care workers are at high risk without adequate PPE because the viruses are highly transmissible through direct contact with blood or body fluids.⭐ Strict isolation and full PPE prevent nosocomial spread.

32. Entamoeba histolytica

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: bloody diarrhea (dysentery) after travel or fecal-oral exposure; RUQ pain/fever from liver abscess.
  • Diagnosis: stool O&P with trophozoites containing ingested RBCs; serology/antigen/PCR help, and serology is key for liver abscess.
  • Histology: flask-shaped colonic ulcers; liver abscess aspirate looks like anchovy paste (usually right lobe, solitary).
  • Treatment: metronidazole or tinidazole for invasive disease, THEN a luminal agent (paromomycin or iodoquinol) to clear cysts.
  • Transmission: fecal-oral via cyst-contaminated food/water; also sexual (oral-anal) contact. Cysts have 4 nuclei, trophozoites eat RBCs.
  1. 1"Do not ENTer - HiSTorical site": Entamoeba histolytica is an intestinal protozoan that causes amebiasis (amebic dysentery and liver abscess). Mnemonic: the 'histolytica' name means tissue-lysing, so it invades tissue.
  2. 2Bubbles in water: The trophozoite is the motile, invasive, feeding stage; the cyst is the infectious, environmentally resistant form passed in stool. Cysts survive in water, which is why contaminated water is the main source.
  3. 3Drinking bubbly water: Amebiasis is acquired mainly by ingesting food or water contaminated with E. histolytica cysts (fecal-oral route). It is most common in areas with poor sanitation and in travelers returning from endemic regions.
  4. 4Holding hands: Transmission can also be sexual through oral-anal contact, since spread is fecal-oral. This makes it a consideration in men who have sex with men.
  5. 5Right lobe dig site: Hepatic amebiasis, a liver abscess, is the most common extraintestinal manifestation. Trophozoites reach the liver via the portal vein, and the abscess is typically solitary and in the right lobe.
  6. 6Clutching right side: Liver abscess presents with RUQ pain, fever, hepatomegaly, malaise and nausea; labs may show leukocytosis and elevated liver enzymes. Diarrhea is often absent at the time the abscess presents.
  7. 7Anchovy paste truck: Abscess aspirate is thick, reddish-brown 'anchovy paste' from necrotic hepatocytes (liquefactive necrosis). Trophozoites are usually absent from the aspirate and sit at the abscess periphery.⭐ Anchovy paste = amebic liver abscess. Treat with metronidazole; drainage is rarely needed.
  8. 8Colon drainage pipe with rust spots: Colonic infection produces ulcers with a narrow neck at the mucosal surface and a broad base in the submucosa (flask shape), most often in the cecum and ascending colon but anywhere in the large bowel.
  9. 9Flask water bottles: Flask-shaped ulcers on histology are the hallmark of invasive intestinal amebiasis. The trophozoites lyse the mucosa and undermine the submucosa.
  10. 10Flask in colon pipe: Colon biopsy showing flask-shaped ulcers with trophozoites confirms intestinal amebiasis when stool studies are unrevealing. Colonoscopy/biopsy is used when the diagnosis is uncertain.
  11. 11Red stools: Bloody diarrhea (dysentery) results from trophozoite invasion of the mucosa and submucosa with tissue necrosis and ulceration. Stool is typically bloody and mucoid with relatively little fever, unlike bacterial dysentery.
  12. 12"O&P" pipe: Diagnosis uses stool O&P: cysts (round, up to 4 nuclei, mostly in formed stool) or trophozoites (motile, mostly in liquid stool of acute infection). Multiple samples may be needed; antigen tests or PCR can distinguish E. histolytica from non-pathogenic E. dispar.
  13. 13Trophozoite puddle + red cups: Trophozoites containing ingested RBCs (erythrophagocytosis) in stool or tissue are diagnostic of invasive amebiasis, and this separates E. histolytica from non-pathogenic amebae.⭐ Ingested RBCs inside the trophozoite = invasive E. histolytica.
  14. 14Metro: Metronidazole (or tinidazole) is first-line for invasive disease and liver abscess because it kills tissue-invading trophozoites. It does not reliably clear luminal cysts, so a luminal agent must follow.
  15. 15Pair of mice: A luminal agent (paromomycin or iodoquinol) is given after metronidazole/tinidazole to eradicate residual cysts in the gut lumen and prevent relapse and spread. Paromomycin is a poorly absorbed aminoglycoside.
  16. 16"Queen Iodo's Tomb": Iodoquinol is the other luminal amebicide used to clear cysts after tissue-active therapy. Either paromomycin or iodoquinol completes the treatment course.

33. Enterococcus faecium & faecalis

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: UTI, endocarditis (after GU/GI procedures), biliary tree infection, often hospital-acquired
  • Gram-positive cocci in pairs/short chains; group D; non-hemolytic (gamma) usually; PYR positive; catalase negative
  • Grows in 6.5% NaCl and in bile (hydrolyzes esculin: bile esculin agar turns black)
  • Treatment: ampicillin (+/- gentamicin for endocarditis); VRE: linezolid or daptomycin
  • Buzzwords: 6.5% NaCl, bile-esculin, VRE, E. faecalis most common
  1. 1"Caucus": Enterococcus species are gram-positive cocci (in pairs and short chains; group D by Lancefield).
  2. 2Purple hues: Enterococcus faecium and Enterococcus faecalis are gram-positive.
  3. 3"Resist the 6.5% N.Ca": E. faecium and E. faecalis grow in 6.5% NaCl (hypertonic saline), distinguishing them from most other gram-positive cocci such as nonenterococcal group D strep (S. gallolyticus/bovis).⭐ Enterococci survive 6.5% NaCl and bile; group D Streptococcus bovis survives bile only.
  4. 4Green bile boots: E. faecium and E. faecalis hydrolyze esculin in the presence of bile (bile-esculin positive, i.e. bile-resistant), turning the medium black.
  5. 5"Stop the FEES" + less people: Enterococcus faecium accounts for the minority of enterococcal infections but is more often vancomycin-resistant.⭐ E. faecium = fewer infections but more resistance.
  6. 6"CALIfornia caucus" + more people: Enterococcus faecalis is responsible for the majority of enterococcal infections.
  7. 7"Do U ¥ trees?": Enterococcal infections include UTIs (cystitis, pyelonephritis), endocarditis and biliary tree infections (also intra-abdominal and wound infections).⭐ Enterococcus is a classic cause of subacute endocarditis after GU/GI procedures.
  8. 8Resisting entering van: Some E. faecium and E. faecalis strains are resistant to many antibiotics; vancomycin-resistant enterococci (VRE) are particularly important.⭐ VRE: vanA gene alters D-Ala-D-Ala to D-Ala-D-Lac.
  9. 9Police line: For VRE infections, linezolid is an effective treatment.⭐ Daptomycin is another option for VRE.
  10. 10Tiger stripes: Tigecycline has activity against some VRE strains and may be considered in specific clinical scenarios.
