34. Epstein-Barr Virus

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: fever, exudative pharyngitis, posterior cervical lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly in a teen/young adult; amoxicillin rash.
  • Micro: enveloped dsDNA herpesvirus (HHV-4); infects B cells via gp350 binding CD21; latent in B cells.
  • Diagnosis: atypical lymphocytes (CD8+ T cells), positive monospot (heterophile IgM); EBV-specific serology (VCA IgM) is more accurate.
  • Treatment/prevention: supportive; avoid contact sports for weeks (splenic rupture).
  • Buzzwords: kissing disease, Downey cells, CD21, Burkitt lymphoma t(8;14), nasopharyngeal carcinoma, oral hairy leukoplakia.
  1. 1"Epstein's Bar": Epstein-Barr virus (EBV, HHV-4), a DNA virus in Herpesviridae.
  2. 2Cool colors: Cool colors encode DNA: EBV is a (double-stranded, enveloped) DNA virus.
  3. 3Saliva drips + trying to kiss: EBV spreads mainly via saliva, so close contact transmits it, especially among adolescents and college students ('kissing disease').⭐ Kissing disease.
  4. 4Sweat drops: Fever is a hallmark of infectious mononucleosis; with lymphadenopathy and pharyngitis it forms the classic triad.⭐ Classic triad: fever, pharyngitis, lymphadenopathy.
  5. 5Grabbing back of shirt: Tender lymphadenopathy, especially posterior cervical nodes, though it can be generalized.⭐ Posterior cervical nodes are classic for EBV.
  6. 6Reactive "8" T-knight + atypical stains: EBV causes lymphocytosis with atypical lymphocytes (Downey cells): large, reactive CD8+ T cells with abundant basophilic cytoplasm responding to EBV-infected B cells, seen on peripheral smear.⭐ Atypical lymphocytes are CD8+ T cells, not B cells.
  7. 7Enlarged spleen spot + liver spot: Proliferating mononuclear cells accumulate in lymphoid tissue including the spleen, causing splenomegaly (hepatomegaly less commonly).
  8. 8Sleeping B-cell archer: EBV establishes lifelong latency in B cells and can reactivate with certain triggers (e.g., immunosuppression).
  9. 9Entrance + "Must B 21": EBV gp350 binds CD21 (CR2, complement receptor 2) on B cells to enter them.⭐ EBV enters B cells through CD21.
  10. 10White drool: EBV pharyngitis is severe with copious gray-white tonsillar exudate and marked tonsillar enlargement, typically in late teens and young adults (streptococcal pharyngitis is more common in children).
  11. 11Red pencil spots: In active EBV infection, giving ampicillin or amoxicillin causes a diffuse maculopapular rash; this is an immune-mediated, T-cell driven (delayed type IV) reaction, not true IgE-mediated penicillin allergy.⭐ Amoxicillin/ampicillin rash in mono.
  12. 12Crab meals: The crab (cancer) icon: EBV increases risk of some cancers, including Hodgkin and non-Hodgkin lymphomas (notably Burkitt) and nasopharyngeal carcinoma.
  13. 13Stern owl's eyes + sitting in reeds: Hodgkin lymphoma (associated with EBV) shows Reed-Sternberg cells: large B cells with bilobed nuclei and prominent nucleoli ('owl's eyes'); they are CD15+ and CD30+.⭐ Reed-Sternberg cells: CD15+, CD30+.
  14. 14Bar kid: EBV is associated with Burkitt lymphoma, a highly aggressive B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma with t(8;14) translocation (c-myc and IgH) and a 'starry sky' histology.⭐ t(8;14) c-myc translocation; starry-sky appearance.
  15. 15Jaw lump: Endemic (African) Burkitt lymphoma occurs in children in equatorial Africa and classically presents as a rapidly growing jaw or facial bone mass.
  16. 16Crab pinching nose: Nasopharyngeal carcinoma is strongly associated with EBV (especially in Southeast Asia and Southern China); it presents with a painless neck mass, nasal obstruction or bleeding, ear symptoms or cranial nerve palsies.
  17. 17Bearded man + immunocompromised cane: Oral hairy leukoplakia: painless white corrugated ('hairy') lesions on the lateral tongue that cannot be scraped off (unlike thrush), caused by EBV in immunocompromised patients (HIV/AIDS).⭐ Cannot be scraped off, unlike candidal thrush.
  18. 18Bullseye spot + star darts: The monospot test detects heterophile IgM antibodies that agglutinate horse or sheep red cells for quick EBV diagnosis; it has limited sensitivity/specificity and the CDC no longer recommends it - EBV-specific serology is preferred.⭐ Monospot = heterophile antibodies; false negatives early in disease.
  19. 19"No contact jousting": Patients with infectious mononucleosis must avoid contact sports for several weeks (until cleared) because of the risk of splenic rupture.⭐ Splenic rupture risk: no contact sports.
Show the original legend picture

35. Eq. Encephalitis / Chikungunya virus

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: equine encephalitides - fever then meningoencephalitis (EEE worst); chikungunya - abrupt fever, severe polyarthralgia, rash.
  • Micro: enveloped, icosahedral, positive-sense ssRNA alphaviruses (Togaviridae); arboviruses.
  • Diagnosis: serology/PCR (IgM in CSF or serum).
  • Treatment/prevention: supportive care; mosquito control; no specific antiviral.
  • Buzzwords: togas, horse + bird + mosquito cycle, EEE east coast, chikungunya 'bendy' joints, Aedes aegypti.
  1. 1Togas: Togaviridae (toga = 'tog-a' virus), a family containing the Alphavirus genus.
  2. 2acme: The 'acme' icon represents the alphavirus genus (A = alpha, 'a-cme') within Togaviridae.check original
  3. 3Warm colors: Warm colors encode RNA: Togaviridae have RNA genomes (single-stranded).
  4. 4Positive sun: Positive sun = positive-sense RNA genome (translated directly).
  5. 5Toga dress: The toga wraps the virus: Togaviridae are enveloped viruses.
  6. 6Icosahedral lantern: Alphaviruses have icosahedral capsids (and a surrounding envelope).
  7. 7Horse: The equine encephalitides - Eastern (EEEV), Western (WEEV) and Venezuelan (VEEV) - are alphaviruses in Togaviridae.
  8. 8Chicken gunny sack: Chikungunya virus (CHIKV) is also an alphavirus (Togaviridae).
  9. 9Mosquitoes: Alphaviruses are arboviruses (arthropod-borne), transmitted to humans by mosquito vectors.
  10. 10Equine bird: The equine encephalitis viruses are maintained in animal reservoirs, mainly birds, which are amplifying hosts that infect mosquitoes.⭐ Reservoir = birds; horses and humans are dead-end hosts.
  11. 11Horse bothered by mosquitos: Equine encephalitis alphaviruses are zoonotic: mosquitoes transmit to horses (which develop disease) and to humans.
  12. 12Due east: Eastern equine encephalitis virus (EEEV) occurs in the eastern US along the Atlantic and Gulf coasts.⭐ EEE: east coast, most severe.
  13. 13Summer sun: EEEV infections peak in summer; most are mild flu-like (fever, headache, myalgia, malaise, vomiting), but a minority progress to neuroinvasive disease.
  14. 14Brain in jar + red skull: Neuroinvasive EEE causes meningitis (fever, headache, neck stiffness, photophobia) and encephalitis (altered mental status, seizures, focal deficits, movement disorders), with high mortality or long-term morbidity (the red skull).⭐ EEE has the highest fatality among the equine encephalitides.
  15. 15Westward WEE: Western equine encephalitis virus (WEEV) occurs in the western and southwestern US.
  16. 16Warning-Baby on Board (WEEV): Infants are at highest risk for severe WEEV encephalitis, although most infections are benign.check original
  17. 17Due south: Venezuelan equine encephalitis virus (VEEV) occurs in Central and South America (Venezuela, Colombia, parts of Mexico) and occasionally the southern US.
  18. 18Warning-Baby on Board (VEEV): Neuroinvasive VEEV is more likely in children than adults, though most VEEV infections are benign (flu-like).check original
  19. 19Global chicken trade label on chicken gunny sack: Chikungunya is found worldwide (Asia, Pacific, Africa, Americas, Caribbean, Florida), spread by travel and global trade.
  20. 2080's night egyptian tablet + mosquitos: The vector is the Aedes aegypti mosquito (Egyptian tablet; Aedes albopictus also transmits).⭐ Aedes aegypti (also dengue, Zika, yellow fever).
  21. 21Bendy chicken: Chikungunya ('that which bends up') presents with abrupt fever then polyarthralgia from viral replication in joints (synovial fibroblasts, macrophages, muscle) causing cytokine-mediated inflammation.⭐ Chikungunya = stooped posture from severe joint pain.
  22. 22Chicken handling gloves: Chikungunya arthralgias are bilateral, symmetric and distal-predominant, mainly affecting small joints of the hands and wrists.
  23. 23Grandfather sundial: Chikungunya can cause chronic arthralgia and arthritis lasting months to years, sometimes debilitating.⭐ Chronic arthritis can last months to years.
  24. 24Chicken handler rash: Chikungunya often has a diffuse maculopapular erythematous rash 2-5 days after fever onset (trunk, limbs, face); bullous rash is uncommon.
Show the original legend picture

36. Escherichia coli (ETEC & EHEC)

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: UTI (cystitis/pyelonephritis), neonatal meningitis (K1), gram-negative sepsis; EHEC = bloody diarrhea + HUS; ETEC = traveler's watery diarrhea.
  • Lactose fermenter: pink on MacConkey, metallic green sheen on EMB; oxidase negative, indole positive.
  • EHEC O157:H7: sorbitol-negative (colorless on sorbitol-MacConkey), Shiga-like toxin (inactivates 60S ribosome), undercooked beef.
  • HUS triad: thrombocytopenia, MAHA with schistocytes, acute kidney injury; do NOT give antibiotics for EHEC.
  • ETEC: LT (like cholera, increases cAMP) and ST (increases cGMP) cause watery diarrhea; treat with fluids.
  1. 1E. cola: Escherichia coli is a gram-negative bacillus (pink rod on Gram stain) and a normal member of gut flora, but it is also the leading cause of several infections. It is a facultative anaerobe in the Enterobacteriaceae family.
  2. 2Red hues: Red/pink coloring reminds you that E. coli is gram-negative: it has a thin peptidoglycan layer with an outer membrane containing LPS (endotoxin), which stains pink with safranin counterstain.
  3. 3Pink milk carton: E. coli ferments lactose, forming pink colonies on MacConkey agar (acid from lactose fermentation turns the pH indicator pink). Other lactose fermenters include Klebsiella and Enterobacter.
  4. 4Glass capsule: Many strains of E. coli are surrounded by a polysaccharide capsule, which helps them evade phagocytosis.
  5. 5K cake + glass capsule: Some strains express K capsule (K antigen), a key virulence factor in extraintestinal infections (e.g., sepsis, meningitis, UTI) because it resists phagocytosis.
  6. 6Green coaster: E. coli typically forms distinctive metallic green colonies on eosin methylene blue (EMB) agar; the metallic sheen is a classic buzzword for strong lactose fermentation.
  7. 7Catalase cat: E. coli is catalase positive: catalase converts hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen, protecting the bacterium from oxidative killing. Catalase-positive organisms are particularly a problem in chronic granulomatous disease.
  8. 8Fimbriae ribbons: Fimbriae (pili) of E. coli mediate adhesion to host cells, an important virulence factor. P fimbriae bind uroepithelium and are linked to pyelonephritis, preventing the bacteria from being washed out by urine flow.
  9. 9"1" bladder cup: E. coli is the most common cause of urinary tract infections (cystitis and pyelonephritis), usually by ascending spread from the gut flora. It is the most common cause of uncomplicated UTI.
  10. 10Blood red shake: E. coli is a major cause of gram-negative sepsis. LPS (endotoxin, lipid A) triggers release of TNF-α and IL-1, leading to fever, hypotension and septic shock; urinary tract and GI/biliary sources are common.
  11. 11K cake + baby + meningitis helmet: Strains with the K1 capsular antigen cause neonatal meningitis, acquired from the maternal birth canal.⭐ Neonatal meningitis: think group B Strep, E. coli (K1), and Listeria.
  12. 12Hamburger: Enterohemorrhagic E. coli (EHEC) is classically transmitted through undercooked ground beef (also unpasteurized juice and raw produce). The infectious dose is very low.⭐ Undercooked burger + bloody diarrhea = EHEC (O157:H7).
  13. 13Red stool: EHEC causes bloody diarrhea (hemorrhagic colitis) by damaging the intestinal lining through Shiga-like toxin production. Fever is typically absent or low-grade, and fecal leukocytes are scant.⭐ Antibiotics and antimotility agents are avoided in EHEC because they can raise the risk of HUS.
  14. 14Sorbitol free soda: O157:H7 does not ferment sorbitol, so it forms colorless colonies on sorbitol-MacConkey agar, distinguishing it from most other E. coli strains, which are sorbitol fermenters.
  15. 15She-gorilla toy: EHEC produces Shiga-like toxin (Shiga toxin 1/2, phage-encoded), nearly identical to the Shiga toxin of Shigella dysenteriae. It inactivates the 60S ribosomal subunit (cleaves 28S rRNA) in host cells, disrupting protein synthesis and killing the cell.
  16. 16Glomerular straw: Shiga-like toxin damages endothelial cells of glomerular capillaries and causes hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS). This endothelial injury leads to acute kidney injury; HUS usually follows the diarrheal illness by about a week, mainly in children.
  17. 17Plate aggregation: Endothelial damage exposes the surface to platelets, triggering platelet aggregation and microthrombi. Platelet consumption causes thrombocytopenia (the classic HUS triad is thrombocytopenia, MAHA and acute kidney injury).⭐ HUS: low platelets but normal PT/PTT (unlike DIC); schistocytes on smear.
  18. 18Popped red balloon: In microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA), red cells are mechanically sheared into schistocytes as they pass through small vessels filled with fibrin/platelet clots, lowering the red cell count. Labs show elevated LDH and indirect bilirubin, low haptoglobin, and a negative Coombs test.
  19. 19"Only $1.57": E. coli O157:H7 is the classic enterohemorrhagic serotype: Shiga-like toxin causes bloody diarrhea and HUS. The "$1.57" in the picture is a mnemonic for O157. Management is supportive (fluids); avoid antibiotics.
  20. 20Water truck: Enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC) is a common cause of traveler's diarrhea, acquired by ingesting contaminated water or food. It causes watery diarrhea, abdominal cramping, nausea and vomiting; it colonizes the small bowel via fimbriae (colonization factors) and does not invade.
  21. 21eL Agua: Heat-labile toxin (LT, mnemonic: eL = Labile) ADP-ribosylates Gs, increasing adenylyl cyclase activity, so cAMP rises, inhibiting sodium/chloride absorption and promoting chloride and water secretion, causing secretory (watery) diarrhea. It works like cholera toxin.
  22. 22San Gabriel: Heat-stable toxin (ST, mnemonic: San Gabriel = Stable, Guanylate) activates guanylate cyclase, raising cGMP, increasing chloride secretion and reducing sodium absorption, causing secretory (watery) diarrhea.⭐ LT = cAMP (like cholera); ST = cGMP. Remember: LT is Labile (heat-inactivated), ST is Stable.
  23. 23Spilling water: ETEC causes traveler's diarrhea: watery diarrhea, abdominal cramping, nausea and vomiting, with no blood and no significant inflammation. It is usually self-limited and treated with oral rehydration; antibiotics (e.g., rifaximin, azithromycin, fluoroquinolone) are used in more severe cases.
Show the original legend picture

37. Flaviviridae/Yellow Fever

Exam snapshot
  • Flaviviridae: enveloped, (+)ssRNA, non-segmented viruses; flaviviruses (dengue, yellow fever, Zika, West Nile) are arboviruses; HCV (Hepacivirus) is bloodborne.
  • Dengue: Aedes; 'breakbone fever', retro-orbital pain, thrombocytopenia; severe dengue with plasma leakage on secondary infection by a different serotype (antibody-dependent enhancement).
  • Yellow fever: Aedes; fever, jaundice, hemorrhage, black vomit; Councilman bodies and Faget sign; live attenuated 17D vaccine for travelers to Africa/South America.
  • Zika: Aedes; mild febrile illness; congenital microcephaly, sexual transmission, and Guillain-Barre syndrome.
  • West Nile: Culex mosquito, birds are reservoir; mostly asymptomatic; can cause meningitis, encephalitis, and acute flaccid paralysis; all treatment is supportive.
  1. 1Warm colors: Flaviviridae are RNA viruses (the warm color theme represents 'flavi' = yellow, from yellow fever).
  2. 2Positive sun: Flaviviridae are positive-sense RNA viruses, so the genome can serve directly as mRNA.
  3. 35 Flavors!: The Flaviviridae family includes the genera Flavivirus and Hepacivirus; clinically important members are dengue, yellow fever, Zika, West Nile, and hepatitis C. Most flaviviruses are arboviruses (arthropod-borne), spread by mosquitoes (Aedes or Culex), whereas hepatitis C is bloodborne and not vector-transmitted.
  4. 4Single straw: Flaviviridae have a non-segmented, single-stranded RNA genome, so they cannot undergo reassortment.
  5. 5Hep C Hippo: Hepatitis C virus is a member of Flaviviridae (genus Hepacivirus). It is the only one commonly spread by blood and it causes chronic liver disease.
  6. 6Robe: Flaviviridae are enveloped viruses, so they are inactivated by detergents and disinfectants.
  7. 7Dinghy with a donkey: Dengue virus (Flavivirus) is transmitted by the Aedes aegypti mosquito and causes dengue fever, or 'breakbone fever'. It presents with high fever, severe myalgias and arthralgias, headache, retro-orbital pain, and rash.
  8. 8Eighties clothes on donkey: Dengue fever (the colorful 80s clothes represent the rash) presents with high fever, severe myalgias and arthralgias, headache, and a macular or maculopapular rash. Treatment is supportive (fluids, acetaminophen); avoid NSAIDs and aspirin because of bleeding risk.
  9. 9Broken plates: Dengue causes bone marrow suppression and immune-mediated platelet destruction, leading to thrombocytopenia and increased bleeding risk (petechiae, positive tourniquet test).⭐ Thrombocytopenia is a lab hallmark of dengue.
  10. 10Spilled strawberry milkshake: Dengue hemorrhagic fever is dengue with thrombocytopenia, plasma leakage, and hemorrhagic manifestations. It often occurs on secondary infection with a different serotype, when non-neutralizing antibodies cause antibody-dependent enhancement.⭐ Second infection with a different serotype = worse disease (ADE).
  11. 11Deflated kidney: Severe dengue can cause acute renal failure and hypovolemic shock (dengue shock syndrome) because of plasma leakage, dehydration, and multiorgan dysfunction.
  12. 12Syringe-shaped oar: A tetravalent live attenuated dengue vaccine is available, but it is given only to people with confirmed prior dengue infection. In seronegative people it can predispose to antibody-dependent enhancement and severe disease upon natural infection.
  13. 13Yellow water buffalo: Yellow fever virus (a positive-sense RNA flavivirus) is transmitted by Aedes aegypti and causes fever, jaundice, myalgia, and hemorrhagic manifestations. Treatment is supportive. Its reservoirs are monkeys (sylvatic cycle) and humans (urban cycle).
  14. 14Eighties clothes on buffalo: Yellow fever (the 80s clothes mark the Aedes-borne flavivirus) causes fever, jaundice, myalgia, and hemorrhage. Classically, a patient may improve briefly and then relapse with the toxic phase of jaundice and bleeding. The Faget sign is a high fever with a relatively slow pulse.⭐ Faget sign = fever with relative bradycardia.
  15. 15Liver spot + yellow buffalo: Yellow fever damages hepatocytes and Kupffer cells, causing hepatocellular necrosis and inflammation that lead to hepatomegaly and impaired bilirubin metabolism, hence jaundice. Histology shows midzonal necrosis with eosinophilic Councilman bodies (apoptotic hepatocytes).⭐ Councilman bodies are classic for yellow fever.
  16. 16Back hump: Yellow fever causes severe myalgia, especially in the lumbosacral region, from viral-induced myositis and cytokine-mediated muscle inflammation.
  17. 17Red stool: Yellow fever is a hemorrhagic fever with bloody diarrhea, melena, hematemesis ('black vomit'), epistaxis, and petechiae. It results from impaired clotting factor synthesis by the injured liver, thrombocytopenia, and endothelial damage, giving elevated PT and PTT and a low platelet count.
  18. 18Syringe-shaped horns: A live attenuated vaccine (17D strain) prevents yellow fever and is recommended for travelers to endemic regions of Africa and South America. It is contraindicated in immunocompromised patients and used with caution in egg allergy.
  19. 19Zebra: Zika virus (Flavivirus) is transmitted by Aedes aegypti and usually causes mild fever, rash, arthralgia, and conjunctivitis (red, irritated eyes). Most infections are asymptomatic or mild and treatment is supportive.
  20. 20Eighties clothes on zebra: Zika (the 80s clothes mark the Aedes-borne flavivirus) causes mild fever, rash, arthralgia, and nonpurulent conjunctivitis. Treatment is supportive.
  21. 21Pregnant zebra: Zika is shed in blood, semen, and other body fluids, so it spreads through Aedes bites and also vertically (mother to fetus), sexually, and via blood transfusion or organ transplant.⭐ Zika is notable among flaviviruses for sexual transmission.
  22. 22Tiny hat: Congenital Zika syndrome is marked by microcephaly with deficient brain tissue (e.g., calcifications, ventriculomegaly) and neurologic dysfunction.⭐ Zika = microcephaly.
  23. 23Guillain the bear: Zika infection is associated with Guillain-Barre syndrome, an ascending symmetric weakness with areflexia, usually 1-2 weeks after the acute infection. It is an autoimmune demyelinating polyneuropathy.
  24. 24Nile bird: West Nile virus is transmitted by Culex mosquitoes, with birds as the reservoir host. It is mostly asymptomatic, but can cause West Nile fever (fever, headache, myalgia, rash) or progress to neuroinvasive disease. Treatment is supportive.
  25. 25Cool-X + mosquito: Culex mosquitoes (the 'X' in Culex) spread West Nile virus between birds and then to humans, who are dead-end hosts. Fewer than 1% of infections become neuroinvasive, and risk is higher in the elderly.
  26. 26Red feather on head: West Nile neuroinvasive disease can present as encephalitis (inflammation of brain parenchyma) with fever, headache, altered mental status, seizures, and focal neurologic deficits.
  27. 27Neck brace: West Nile virus can cause meningitis (inflammation of the meninges), presenting with fever, headache, neck stiffness, photophobia, and nausea. CSF typically shows lymphocytic pleocytosis with elevated protein and normal glucose.
  28. 28Floppy neck: West Nile virus can cause asymmetric acute flaccid paralysis from damage to anterior horn cells, similar to poliomyelitis. It presents with weakness, decreased tone, and areflexia without sensory loss.
  29. 29Flapping wings: West Nile neuroinvasive disease may also cause tremor and Parkinson-like movement disorders (flapping wings = tremor/abnormal movements), along with seizures, altered mental status, and focal deficits.
  30. 30Comatose bird: Severe West Nile neuroinvasive disease can progress to coma and death, especially in elderly or immunocompromised patients. Diagnosis is by IgM antibody in serum or CSF, and treatment is supportive.

38. Francisella tularensis (Tularemia)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: hunter or rabbit/rodent handler or tick bite victim with an ulcer at the site and painful regional lymphadenopathy, plus fever.
  • Gram-negative coccobacillus, facultative intracellular (macrophages); needs cysteine for growth; bioterrorism agent.
  • Reservoirs: rabbits, rodents; vectors: Dermacentor ticks, deer flies, mosquitoes.
  • Treatment: streptomycin (or gentamicin); report to the CDC.
  • Buzzwords: rabbits, ulceroglandular, Dermacentor tick, granulomas, bioterrorism.
  1. 1Francis the rabbit: Francisella tularensis is a gram-negative coccobacillus.
  2. 2Rabbit: F. tularensis has a broad animal reservoir, including rabbits, rodents, and other mammals.⭐ Rabbit skinning or hunting is a classic exposure.
  3. 3Ticks: F. tularensis is transmitted by arthropod vectors such as ticks (especially Dermacentor), deer flies, horse flies, and mosquitoes. It can be aerosolized, so it is a potential bioterrorism agent and cases must be reported to the CDC.⭐ Any case of F. tularensis must be reported to the CDC.
  4. 4Red radishes: F. tularensis is a gram-negative coccobacillus (red on Gram stain).
  5. 5Open cage: F. tularensis is a facultative intracellular pathogen that survives and replicates in macrophages.⭐ This allows it to evade immune clearance.
  6. 6Rabbit holes: Tularemia can cause a painful skin ulcer with necrosis at the site of infection (e.g., a tick bite), the ulceroglandular form.⭐ Ulceroglandular is the most common form.
  7. 7Tunnels to rotting radishes: The organism spreads through lymphatics to reticuloendothelial organs (lymph nodes, liver, spleen), causing granulomatous inflammation with central necrosis, painful regional lymphadenopathy, fever, and systemic symptoms.
  8. 8Mounds around rabbit: Tularemia classically causes painful regional lymphadenopathy (lymph node enlargement).
  9. 9Sai: Aminoglycosides, particularly streptomycin and gentamicin, are effective against tularemia.⭐ Streptomycin is the classic drug of choice.

39. Gardnerella vaginalis

Exam snapshot
  • Bacterial vaginosis: thin gray-white homogeneous discharge with fishy odor, little or no itching, burning, or inflammation.
  • Gram-variable (gram-indeterminate) coccobacillus with a thin cell wall; overgrowth after loss of protective Lactobacillus.
  • Vaginal pH >4.5, positive whiff test with KOH, and clue cells on wet mount (Amsel criteria).
  • Metronidazole or clindamycin (oral or topical); not usually treating partners.
  • Clue cells, fishy odor, whiff test, pH >4.5, no inflammation (not an STI per se).
  1. 1"Fish garden": Gardnerella vaginalis is a gram-indeterminate (gram-variable) coccobacillus that causes bacterial vaginosis (BV).
  2. 2Purple and pink graffiti: Gardnerella has a thin gram-positive-type cell wall, but it stains inconsistently, appearing gram-positive or gram-negative, so it is called gram-variable.
  3. 3Overgrowth of Venus flytraps: Loss of the normal acid-producing, facultative anaerobic Lactobacillus flora raises vaginal pH and allows Gardnerella overgrowth; triggers include antibiotics, hormonal changes, sexual activity, and douching.⭐ BV is a polymicrobial overgrowth, not a classic STI.
  4. 4Venus flytrap discharge: BV causes a thin, gray-white vaginal discharge with a characteristic "fishy" odor; patients may be asymptomatic or have mild irritation, but itching, burning, and inflammation are typically absent.
  5. 5Fish market: Same presentation: thin gray-white discharge and "fishy" odor, particularly after intercourse or menses, with little inflammation.
  6. 6"p 4.5 and up": Vaginal pH in BV is above 4.5 (often 5.0-6.5), compared with a normal pH of 3.8-4.5.⭐ pH >4.5 is also seen in trichomoniasis.
  7. 7Sniffing dog + "KOH" hat: The whiff test: adding 10% KOH to the discharge produces a strong fishy odor (amines) in BV.⭐ Positive whiff test = BV (also may be positive in trichomoniasis).
  8. 8Spotted fish + looking for clues: Wet mount shows clue cells: vaginal epithelial cells covered with Gardnerella, giving a stippled or granular border; they may look bluish-purple with Gram or Giemsa stain.⭐ Clue cells are pathognomonic of BV.
  9. 9Metro: BV is treated with metronidazole or clindamycin.⭐ Metronidazole: avoid alcohol (disulfiram-like reaction).

40. Giardia lamblia

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: hiker or camper who drank stream or lake water with foul-smelling, greasy, non-bloody diarrhea, bloating, and flatulence
  • Cysts are the infectious form (fecal-oral, contaminated water); trophozoites attach via a ventral sucking disc and cause malabsorption
  • Diagnosis: stool O&P (pear-shaped trophozoites with two nuclei, or cysts) or stool Giardia antigen ELISA
  • Treatment: metronidazole (also tinidazole, nitazoxanide)
  • Associations: backpackers, day care outbreaks, IgA deficiency/CVID; cysts survive chlorination, so boil or filter water
  1. 1"Giardia jungle ride": Giardia intestinalis (also called G. lamblia or G. duodenalis) is an intestinal protozoan with a flagellated trophozoite stage and a cyst stage.
  2. 2Poopy water: Giardia cysts are the infectious stage and spread by the fecal-oral route, usually through contaminated water or food. Ingesting only a small number of cysts can cause infection.
  3. 3Bubbles in water: Infected people shed cysts in feces, contaminating water and food. Cysts are hardy and resistant to chlorination at usual concentrations, so water must be boiled or filtered.
  4. 4Backpacks: Giardiasis is a risk for people drinking from natural water sources, such as hikers and campers, who may ingest contaminated water. Animals (e.g., beavers) can contaminate streams. Day care outbreaks also occur.
  5. 5Water bottle + poopy water: Poorly purified or untreated water is a common vehicle of transmission. Prevention is by filtering, boiling, or treating water before drinking.
  6. 6Campers holding noses: Giardiasis typically causes foul-smelling, greasy, non-bloody diarrhea with bloating, abdominal discomfort, and flatulence. Blood and fecal leukocytes are absent because the organism does not invade the mucosa. It may become chronic.⭐ Foul-smelling, fatty, non-bloody diarrhea in a hiker = Giardia.
  7. 7Yellow stool: Trophozoites attach to the small intestinal mucosa and disrupt villus structure and function, causing malabsorption, steatorrhea (fatty stools), diarrhea, and potential deficiency of fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E, K). Secondary lactase deficiency can also occur.
  8. 8Trophozoite shields: The flagellated trophozoite is pear-shaped (broad anterior, tapering posterior) with two prominent nuclei that look like eyes (a 'face'), multiple flagella for motility, and a ventral adhesive (sucking) disc that attaches to the intestinal mucosa. Trophozoites do not invade the tissue.
  9. 9Shields in water: Finding Giardia cysts or trophozoites in stool confirms the diagnosis. Trophozoites are seen in fresh watery stool and cysts in formed stool.
  10. 10"O&P": Stool ova and parasite (O&P) examination is a reliable method for diagnosing giardiasis. Multiple samples may be needed because shedding is intermittent.
  11. 11"ELISA" boat: Stool ELISA for Giardia antigen is a sensitive diagnostic test, alongside O&P. Duodenal aspirate or the string test is an option if stool studies are negative but suspicion is high.
  12. 12Metro: Effective treatments for giardiasis are metronidazole (first line), tinidazole, and nitazoxanide. Metronidazole is activated by anaerobic organisms to form free radicals that damage DNA.
Show the original legend picture

41. Haemophilus influenzae

Exam snapshot
  • Presentation: epiglottitis (drooling, stridor, tripod posture), otitis media, meningitis, pneumonia; invasive disease with type b capsule.
  • Micro: gram-negative coccobacillus; needs factors X (hemin) and V (NAD+); Hib has polysaccharide capsule.
  • Diagnosis: culture on chocolate agar; satellite growth around Staph aureus on blood agar.
  • Treatment/prevention: ceftriaxone for meningitis; rifampin prophylaxis for close contacts; conjugate Hib vaccine.
  • Buzzwords: chocolate agar, factors V and X, cherry-red epiglottis, thumbprint sign, encapsulated.
  1. 1Phyllis's chocolates: Haemophilus influenzae (Phyllis' chocolates = chocolate agar), a gram-negative coccobacillus.
  2. 2Red hues: Red/pink = gram-negative: H. influenzae stains gram-negative.
  3. 3Pink oval candies: The oval pink candies show the coccobacillus shape (short rods) and gram-negative staining.
  4. 4"Chocolate": Chocolate agar (blood agar heated to lyse red cells, releasing nutrients) supports growth of H. influenzae by providing factors X and V.⭐ Grows on chocolate agar, not on plain blood agar (except as satellite colonies).
  5. 5"5 cents": H. influenzae requires factor V (NAD+); the coins stand for the two required growth factors (5 cents = V).⭐ Factor V = NAD+.check original
  6. 6"10 cents": The second coin (10 cents = X) stands for factor X (hemin); H. influenzae requires both X (hemin) and V (NAD+).⭐ Factor X = hemin.check original
  7. 7Aerosol spray: H. influenzae is transmitted via respiratory droplets.
  8. 8Screaming kid + drool: Hib epiglottitis in children aged about 2-6 presents with dysphagia, drooling and inspiratory stridor.⭐ Epiglottitis: 3 D's - drooling, dysphagia, distress.
  9. 9Cherries: Epiglottitis shows a swollen, inflamed 'cherry-red' epiglottis (thumbprint sign on lateral neck X-ray); avoid examination that could provoke airway closure.⭐ Cherry-red epiglottis; secure airway first.
  10. 10Plugging ears: H. influenzae (mostly nontypeable strains) is a common cause of acute and recurrent otitis media.
  11. 11Bee + glass capsule: 'Hib' (b) = bee: type b has a polysaccharide (polyribosylribitol phosphate) capsule that helps evade immunity and causes invasive disease.⭐ Type b capsule = PRP.
  12. 12Meningitis helmet + holding head: Hib causes meningitis (headache, neck stiffness, photophobia, vomiting) - classically in unvaccinated infants and young children rather than adolescents; today uncommon due to vaccination.⭐ Hib meningitis is mainly a disease of unvaccinated young children.
  13. 13Sickles: Patients with sickle cell disease or asplenia are at higher risk of infection with encapsulated bacteria, including H. influenzae.⭐ Encapsulated organisms: SHiNE SKiS.
  14. 14Kid with syringe: Infants and toddlers should receive the Hib vaccine series to prevent serious invasive infections.
  15. 15"Sugar DIPped": The Hib capsular polysaccharide is a poor immunogen in infants (T-cell independent); it is covalently conjugated to a protein carrier (diphtheria or tetanus toxoid, or meningococcal OMP) to induce a T-cell-dependent response.⭐ Conjugate vaccine converts T-independent antigen to T-dependent.
  16. 16"$2.18": The dollar amounts represent age in months: the Hib vaccine is given as a series from about 2 months to 18 months of age.check original
  17. 173 axes: Third-generation cephalosporins (ceftriaxone) are the initial treatment of choice for Hib meningitis.⭐ Ceftriaxone for meningitis.
  18. 18Rifle: Rifampin prophylaxis is given to close contacts of patients with invasive Hib disease.⭐ Rifampin prophylaxis for contacts.
Show the original legend picture

42. Hantavirus, California Encephalitis & Rift Valley Fever Viruses (Bunyaviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: Hantavirus - deer mouse exposure with fever then rapid noncardiogenic pulmonary edema (HPS) or fever with renal failure (HFRS); California/Rift Valley - mosquito-borne encephalitis.
  • Micro: enveloped, helical, segmented (3 segments) negative-sense ssRNA viruses; envelope from Golgi membrane.
  • Diagnosis: serology and RT-PCR; clinical context (rodent or mosquito exposure).
  • Treatment/prevention: mainly supportive (ribavirin used for HFRS and Rift Valley fever in some settings); rodent control, repellent, protective clothing.
  • Buzzwords: robovirus (rodent-borne), arbovirus, Sin Nombre, Hantaan, capillary leak, deer mouse.
  1. 1Paul Bunyan: Bunyaviruses (hantavirus, California encephalitis virus, Rift Valley fever virus) are RNA viruses.
  2. 2Warm colors: Bunyaviruses are RNA viruses (warm colors = RNA virus).
  3. 3Moon with negative sign-shaped clouds: Bunyaviruses are negative-sense RNA viruses.
  4. 4Enveloping gold robe: Bunyaviruses are enveloped; they acquire their envelope by budding through the Golgi membrane of host cells.
  5. 53 tree stumps: Bunyaviruses have three segments of negative-sense RNA (L, M, S) whose ends base-pair so the segments form circular structures.⭐ Segmented genome allows reassortment.
  6. 6Haunting mice: Hantavirus is a negative-sense RNA virus in the Bunyaviridae family.
  7. 7Rodents + droppings + urine: Hantavirus is transmitted to humans mainly by inhalation of aerosolized urine, feces or saliva from infected rodents (ROdent-BOrne virus = robovirus); the deer mouse is the main reservoir in North America.⭐ Hantavirus is a robovirus, not an arbovirus.
  8. 8Lung-shaped sweat: New World hantaviruses (e.g. Sin Nombre virus, Americas) cause Hantavirus Pulmonary Syndrome (HPS): fever, myalgias and rapidly progressive noncardiogenic pulmonary edema from capillary leak, causing dyspnea, cough, hypoxia, bilateral interstitial infiltrates and potentially respiratory failure and shock.⭐ Sin Nombre = pulmonary syndrome (Four Corners outbreak).
  9. 9Sweat + blood: Old World hantaviruses (e.g. Hantaan virus, Asia and Europe) cause Hemorrhagic Fever with Renal Syndrome (HFRS): fever, myalgias and hypotension from capillary leak, with prerenal azotemia, oliguria, proteinuria, hematuria and, in severe cases, acute kidney injury.
  10. 10Leaking kidney canteen: Same Old World hantavirus disease (HFRS): capillary leak with renal involvement (azotemia, oliguria, proteinuria, hematuria, possible renal failure).⭐ Old World = kidney; New World = lung.
  11. 11"California Rift Elementary": California encephalitis virus and Rift Valley fever virus are negative-sense RNA viruses in the Bunyaviridae family.
  12. 12Red turban: California encephalitis and Rift Valley fever viruses can cause viral encephalitis: fever, myalgias, headache, altered mental status and seizures, with neurologic complications that may progress to coma in severe cases.
  13. 13Shaking: Same viral encephalitis picture (fever, headache, altered mental status, seizures; may progress to coma).
  14. 14Arbor: California encephalitis and Rift Valley fever viruses are arboviruses (ARthropod-BOrne viruses) transmitted to humans and other vertebrates by mosquitoes.⭐ La Crosse (California serogroup) encephalitis mostly affects children in the US Midwest.
  15. 15Mosquitoes: The arthropod vector for California encephalitis and Rift Valley fever viruses is the mosquito.
  16. 16Long sleeves and leg coverings: Prevention: insect repellent (e.g. DEET), long sleeves and long pants against mosquito-borne bunyaviruses, and avoiding exposure to rodent excreta for hantavirus.
Show the original legend picture

43. Helicobacter pylori

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: epigastric pain with chronic gastritis, duodenal or gastric ulcer; pain relieved by meals in duodenal ulcer, worsened by meals in gastric ulcer.
  • Dx: urea breath test, stool antigen, or endoscopic biopsy with rapid urease test, histology, or culture; urease-, oxidase-, and catalase-positive.
  • Tx: triple therapy: PPI + clarithromycin + amoxicillin (or metronidazole if penicillin-allergic); bismuth quadruple therapy is an alternative.
  • Complications: peptic ulcer disease, gastric adenocarcinoma, and gastric MALT lymphoma (can regress with eradication).
  • Buzzwords: curved gram-negative, flagellated, microaerophilic rod; urease makes ammonia to neutralize acid.
  1. 1Helicopter: Helicobacter pylori is a gram-negative bacillus ('Helico-' = helicopter, spiral shaped).
  2. 2Red helicopter: H. pylori is a gram-negative bacillus, so it stains pink/red on Gram stain. It is also microaerophilic, requiring low oxygen levels.
  3. 3Helicopter rotor: H. pylori is motile, propelled by multiple flagella (the rotor). Its corkscrew motility lets it burrow through gastric mucus to reach the epithelium.
  4. 4Curved mustache: H. pylori is a curved rod (spiral or comma-shaped) that is well adapted to colonizing the gastric mucus layer.
  5. 5Ammonia spray: H. pylori is urease positive: urease converts urea to ammonia and CO2, which neutralizes gastric acid and protects the organism. This is the basis of the urea breath test (patient ingests labeled 13C or 14C urea, and labeled CO2 is detected in exhaled air). Other tests are stool antigen and endoscopic biopsy with rapid urease testing, histology, or culture.⭐ Urease positive: urea breath test, rapid urease test.
  6. 6Oxidase blue ring: H. pylori is oxidase positive (blue color change on oxidase testing), reflecting its cytochrome c oxidase in aerobic respiration. It is also catalase positive.
  7. 7Bullet holes: H. pylori infection is strongly associated with peptic ulcers of the stomach and duodenum, caused by chronic inflammation and ammonia-induced mucosal damage.⭐ Other major cause of ulcers: NSAIDs.
  8. 8Cancer crab: Chronic H. pylori infection raises the risk of gastric adenocarcinoma and gastric MALT lymphoma (a marginal zone B-cell lymphoma). Early MALT lymphoma may regress after H. pylori eradication.⭐ Eradicating H. pylori can cure early MALT lymphoma.
  9. 9Mucus-covered tissues: H. pylori lives in the gastric mucus layer, where chronic infection causes gastritis, which can progress to atrophy and intestinal metaplasia. Chronic gastritis is the pathway leading to gastric adenocarcinoma and MALT lymphoma.
  10. 10Proton bomb blocking gas flow: Triple therapy includes a proton pump inhibitor (e.g., omeprazole), which blocks gastric acid secretion by inhibiting the H+/K+ ATPase. Reducing acid lets ulcers heal and improves antibiotic effectiveness.
  11. 11Duodenal gas hose: H. pylori is present in over 90% of duodenal ulcers (and about 70% of gastric ulcers). Duodenal ulcer pain classically improves with meals, whereas gastric ulcer pain worsens with meals.⭐ Duodenal ulcer: pain better with food; gastric ulcer: pain worse with food.
  12. 12Ammo: Triple therapy includes amoxicillin, a beta-lactam cell wall synthesis inhibitor, as one of the antibiotics. Metronidazole is substituted in penicillin-allergic patients.
  13. 13Keep clear + macrolide crow: Triple therapy includes clarithromycin, a macrolide that blocks the 50S ribosomal subunit (inhibiting protein synthesis). The usual regimen is PPI + clarithromycin + amoxicillin (or metronidazole). Bismuth-based quadruple therapy is used where clarithromycin resistance is common.⭐ Bismuth quadruple therapy = PPI + bismuth + tetracycline + metronidazole.
Show the original legend picture

44. Hepatitis A Virus (Picornaviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Picornavirus: naked, positive-sense ssRNA; acid-stable; fecal-oral spread (shellfish, contaminated water, daycare, travel).
  • Presentation: fatigue, nausea, jaundice, dark urine; kids often anicteric; adult smoker loses taste for cigarettes.
  • Dx: anti-HAV IgM (acute); IgG = past infection or vaccination; very high ALT/AST.
  • Self-limited (about 2 months), no chronic infection or carrier state, no cirrhosis/HCC.
  • Prevention: inactivated vaccine; immune globulin for post-exposure; treatment is supportive.
  1. 1"A"-tagged hippos: Hepatitis A virus (HAV) is a positive-sense, single-stranded RNA virus in the Picornaviridae family (genus Hepatovirus). It causes acute viral hepatitis.
  2. 2Warm colors: HAV has a single-stranded RNA genome of positive polarity. Warm colors on the picture represent this positive-sense genome, which can be translated directly by host ribosomes (naked RNA is infectious).
  3. 3Positive sun: The positive-sense genome acts as its own mRNA, so it is translated directly into a polyprotein that includes the RNA-dependent RNA polymerase.
  4. 4Baby hippo: HAV belongs to the Picornaviridae (pico = small, RNA): small, naked, icosahedral RNA viruses. Other picornaviruses are poliovirus, coxsackievirus, echovirus, and rhinovirus.
  5. 5Statue of David: HAV is a naked (non-enveloped) virus. The lack of an envelope makes it resistant to ether, detergents, and heat, so it persists in the environment.⭐ Naked + acid-stable = fecal-oral.
  6. 6Hippos protected from acid: Being non-enveloped, HAV is acid-stable and survives gastric acid, so it can reach the intestine and be transmitted by the fecal-oral route.
  7. 7Poop: HAV is transmitted fecal-oral: contaminated food or water, or contact with an infected person or object. Outbreaks occur in daycares and in close-contact settings.
  8. 8Water cup: In resource-limited settings with poor sanitation, infection is commonly acquired from contaminated drinking water.
  9. 9Water purification methods: Chlorination, UV irradiation, boiling, and bleach (sodium hypochlorite) inactivate HAV in water. Hand-washing is the key prevention step.
  10. 10USA shellfish stand: In the US, a common source is raw or undercooked shellfish from sewage-contaminated or enclosed waters. Less often, cases arise from sewage-contaminated private wells or close contact with infected individuals.
  11. 11Pulling shellfish from poop water: Shellfish (e.g., oysters, clams) are filter feeders that concentrate virus from sewage-contaminated water. Eating them raw or lightly cooked transmits HAV; contaminated produce is another source.
  12. 12Traveler's backpack: Travelers to areas with high HAV prevalence (parts of Asia, Africa, Mexico, Central and South America, the Caribbean) are at increased risk and should be vaccinated before travel.
  13. 13Enlarged liver spot: Infection is often subclinical, but acute hepatitis can cause tender hepatomegaly and less often splenomegaly. Liver injury is immune-mediated (cytotoxic T cells), with markedly elevated ALT/AST (often thousands) and ALT usually higher than AST.
  14. 14Vomiting: Prodromal and acute symptoms: fatigue, anorexia, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, and low-grade fever.
  15. 15Adult in yellow: Most adults develop jaundice from impaired bilirubin processing by the inflamed liver; dark urine and pale stools may occur. Adults usually have more symptomatic disease than children.
  16. 16Child in tan: Infants and young children usually have anicteric (no jaundice) or asymptomatic infection, so they act as an unrecognized source of spread in outbreaks.
  17. 17Putting out cigarette: Distaste for cigarettes is a classic early prodromal sign in smokers, reflecting hepatic inflammation and nausea before jaundice appears.⭐ Classic buzzword: smoker suddenly dislikes cigarettes.
  18. 18"ONE MONTH only": HAV infection is self-limited. Incubation lasts 2-6 weeks (average about 1 month), followed by a 1-2 week prodrome of fatigue, nausea, and anorexia.
  19. 19"LIMIT one per customer": A jaundice phase lasts 1-2 weeks, and symptoms usually resolve within about 2 months. Acute infection is diagnosed by anti-HAV IgM; anti-HAV IgG indicates past infection or vaccination (lifelong immunity).⭐ IgM anti-HAV = acute; IgG = immune.
  20. 20"NO CARRYING out of food": HAV does not cause a chronic carrier state or chronic liver disease (no cirrhosis or HCC). Fulminant hepatitis is rare, though risk is higher with older age or underlying liver disease.
  21. 21Syringe tranquilizer gun: Prevention is with the inactivated (killed) HAV vaccine, which gives lasting immunity. It is recommended for children, travelers to endemic areas, people with chronic liver disease, men who have sex with men, people experiencing homelessness, and injection drug users. Post-exposure prophylaxis is HAV vaccine and/or immune globulin.

45. Hepatitis B Viral Basics & Infectious Diseases (Hepadnaviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • HBV: enveloped, partially dsDNA, circular; replicates via reverse transcriptase (Hepadnaviridae).
  • Spread: sex, blood, vertical; neonatal infection becomes chronic about 90% of the time vs under 10% in adults.
  • Extrahepatic: serum sickness-like prodrome, polyarteritis nodosa, membranous nephropathy and MPGN.
  • Serology: HBsAg first and persists in chronic; window period = IgM anti-HBc only; vaccine = anti-HBs only; ground-glass hepatocytes.
  • Complications: cirrhosis and HCC. HDV is a defective virus needing HBsAg; superinfection is worse than co-infection.
  • Treatment: NRTIs (lamivudine, entecavir, tenofovir) or pegylated IFN-alpha; newborns get HBIG plus vaccine.
  1. 1Hippies in blue: Hepatitis B virus (HBV) is a DNA virus in the Hepadnaviridae family and a major cause of viral hepatitis.
  2. 2Hippo van: HBV is a cause of viral hepatitis (acute and chronic), and is a leading cause of cirrhosis and hepatocellular carcinoma worldwide.
  3. 3"Hippie pad": HBV is the only member of the Hepadnaviridae (hepatotropic DNA viruses) that causes human disease.
  4. 4Cool colors: HBV is a DNA virus. It is unusual among DNA viruses because it replicates through an RNA intermediate.
  5. 5Hippie clothing: HBV is an enveloped virus; its envelope contains HBsAg (surface antigen). It is also the target of the vaccine, which is made of recombinant HBsAg.
  6. 6Hippies inside dome: HBV has an unusual replication process spanning the nucleus and cytoplasm. In the nucleus the genome becomes cccDNA and is transcribed into RNAs, including the pregenomic RNA.
  7. 7Hippies outside of dome: In the cytoplasm, pregenomic RNA is packaged into the core with the polymerase and reverse-transcribed back into DNA, completing the cycle (see the life-cycle video).
  8. 8Kumbaya circle + half circle: The HBV genome is a small, partially double-stranded circular DNA (one full-length strand and one incomplete strand).
  9. 9"Reverso Transcriptum" spellbook: HBV polymerase has reverse transcriptase activity (an RNA-dependent DNA polymerase), converting pregenomic RNA into DNA. This enzyme is the target of the nucleos(t)ide antiviral drugs.
  10. 10"Sex, drugs, rock & roll": HBV is transmitted by sexual contact, blood exposure (injection drug use, needlestick injuries, shared razors), and transfusions (now rare because of screening). HBV is present in blood and body fluids.⭐ HBV is far more infectious than HIV by needlestick.
  11. 11Red tie-dye outfits of mother and baby: Vertical (perinatal) transmission occurs mainly at childbirth through exposure to maternal blood and secretions; it is the major route of transmission worldwide. Risk is highest when the mother is HBeAg-positive.
  12. 12Tiki torch: HBV is grouped with the TORCHeS infections (T: Toxoplasma; O: Other, including varicella-zoster virus, parvovirus B19, Zika virus, HIV, HBV; R: Rubella; C: CMV; He: HSV; S: Syphilis), which can be transmitted vertically.
  13. 13Baby + 90% of liver cookie: Neonatal infection leads to chronic infection in about 90% of cases because of immune tolerance in the immature neonatal immune system. Chronic carriers have a high lifetime risk of cirrhosis and HCC.
  14. 14Adult + 10% of liver cookie: In adults, fewer than 10% (about 5%) develop chronic infection; most mount an effective immune response and clear the virus.
  15. 15Knee pain lines: Early (prodromal) HBV can cause a serum sickness-like syndrome from circulating immune complexes (type III hypersensitivity), with symmetric arthralgias or arthritis of small joints (hands, wrists, knees), often resolving when jaundice begins.
  16. 16Body henna: The same immune-complex process can cause a maculopapular or urticarial rash with fever and malaise, which may progress to a purpuric, non-blanching rash from small-vessel vasculitis.
  17. 17Red beads: HBV is associated with polyarteritis nodosa (PAN): necrotizing inflammation of medium-sized arteries, causing aneurysms with a beads-on-a-string appearance on angiography. Pulmonary vessels are spared.⭐ PAN: medium vessels, spares the lungs, hepatitis B association.
  18. 18Kidney box: HBV-associated PAN commonly involves the kidneys (renal arteries), causing reduced GFR, hypertension, microscopic hematuria, and proteinuria.
  19. 19Thinner glomerular knot: Chronic HBV can cause immune-complex glomerular disease. Membranous nephropathy shows a thickened glomerular basement membrane due to subepithelial immune complex deposits ('spike and dome' on silver stain), and presents as nephrotic syndrome.check original
  20. 20Thicker glomerular knot: Chronic HBV can also cause membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN), with mesangial proliferation and expansion into the capillary walls, producing the classic 'tram-track' double-contour appearance due to immune complex deposition.check original
  21. 21Rising ALT volleyball: ALT rises during acute HBV and typically falls once the symptomatic phase passes; in viral hepatitis ALT is greater than AST. Acute-phase histology may show Councilman bodies (eosinophilic, apoptotic hepatocytes) and lobular inflammation.
  22. 22Baby + deflated ALT volleyball: Acute neonatal HBV often shows normal or only mildly elevated ALT because the immature immune system does not mount a strong response, which allows persistence (immune-tolerant phase).
  23. 23Antigen stickers on front of hippo van around 'S' & 'E': Know three antigens: HBsAg (surface; first marker of infection), HBcAg (core; found in infected hepatocytes, not detectable in serum, and targeted by anti-HBc antibodies), and HBeAg (marker of active replication and high infectivity).
  24. 24Spiky "S": HBsAg is the first serologic marker to appear (about 1-10 weeks after exposure) and indicates current infection, acute or chronic. Persistence for more than 6 months defines chronic infection.⭐ HBsAg > 6 months = chronic infection.
  25. 25Spiky "E": HBeAg is a secreted protein from the precore region and marks active viral replication and high infectivity. It is a marker of infectivity, and anti-HBe appears as replication declines.
  26. 26Flat tire under "S" and "E": In symptomatic acute infection, patients are HBsAg-positive and HBeAg-positive (active replication, high infectivity). Anti-HBc IgM is also present.
  27. 27"C" on window: In the window period, HBsAg has cleared and anti-HBs has not yet appeared, so anti-HBc IgM may be the only detectable marker. This is why anti-HBc IgM is key to diagnosing acute infection in this gap.⭐ Window period = IgM anti-HBc only.
  28. 28Smooth "E": Anti-HBe indicates declining viral replication and lower infectivity (HBeAg seroconversion).
  29. 29Smooth "S": Anti-HBs (surface antibody) signals recovery with immunity (with anti-HBc IgG) or vaccine-induced immunity.
  30. 30"S" line around syringe: Vaccinated people (recombinant HBsAg) are anti-HBs-positive only: negative HBsAg, anti-HBc, and anti-HBe. Anti-HBc positivity with anti-HBs implies natural infection, not vaccination.⭐ Vaccinated = anti-HBs alone.
  31. 31Serologic rainbow decal lines: Serologic timeline: HBsAg appears first, then HBeAg; anti-HBc IgM rises in acute infection (and fills the window period); anti-HBc IgG follows and persists for life; anti-HBe rises as HBeAg falls; anti-HBs appears last. Chronic infection: HBsAg and IgG anti-HBc persist, with no anti-HBs.
  32. 32Liver stone: Chronic HBV can lead to cirrhosis. Histology shows ground-glass hepatocytes (finely granular, eosinophilic cytoplasm) from cytoplasmic accumulation of HBsAg.
  33. 33Crab tarot card: HBV is a major risk factor for hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC), which can occur even without cirrhosis because viral DNA integrates into the host genome. Screen chronic carriers with ultrasound and AFP.⭐ HBV can cause HCC without cirrhosis.
  34. 34Kid hippies in orange: Hepatitis D virus (HDV, delta agent) is a negative-sense RNA virus in the Deltaviridae (genus Deltavirus).
  35. 35Orange hippie clothing: HDV is an enveloped virus with a circular, single-stranded, negative-sense RNA genome; it is a defective (satellite) virus.
  36. 36Circular headbands: HDV has a small, circular ssRNA genome, the smallest of the human viruses. It encodes hepatitis delta antigen (HDAg).
  37. 37Negative moons: The HDV genome is negative-sense: it must be copied to a positive strand before translation. It replicates using host RNA polymerase.
  38. 38Mom attaching buttons: HDV is defective and needs HBsAg as its envelope for assembly, so it only infects people who are also infected with HBV (or carriers). It uses the HBV surface protein to enter hepatocytes.
  39. 39Co-operative hand holding: Co-infection: HBV and HDV are acquired together, causing acute hepatitis that is more severe but usually self-limited and rarely becomes chronic.
  40. 40Kid on shoulders: Superinfection: HDV infects someone with chronic HBV, causing more severe disease with a high risk of fulminant hepatitis, and progression to chronic HDV in most cases.
  41. 41Grimacing father: Superinfection often leads to rapid progression to cirrhosis and a worse prognosis. Suspect HDV when a chronic HBV patient suddenly worsens.⭐ Sudden worsening in a chronic HBV carrier -> think HDV superinfection.
  42. 42Lamb: Lamivudine (3TC, Epivir) is a nucleoside analog that inhibits HBV reverse transcriptase, reducing viral load. It is also used in HIV; resistance can emerge with monotherapy.
  43. 43She-elf + Nucleotide mace + chain: Nucleos(t)ide analogs (lamivudine, entecavir, tenofovir) inhibit HBV reverse transcriptase and suppress replication. They rarely cure (cccDNA persists), so long-term suppressive treatment is often needed.
  44. 44Alpha antenna: Pegylated interferon-alpha is used in some patients; it has immunomodulatory and antiviral effects that reduce HBV replication and can achieve HBeAg seroconversion. Side effects include flu-like illness and depression.
  45. 45Baby + antibody keys: Infants born to HBsAg-positive mothers should receive both HBIG (passive immunity) and the hepatitis B vaccine (active immunity) within 12 hours of birth.⭐ Newborn of HBsAg+ mother: HBIG + vaccine.
Show the original legend picture

46. Hepatitis B Viral Life Cycle (Hepadnaviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Entry via heparan sulfate proteoglycans and the NTCP receptor into hepatocytes.
  • Partially dsDNA is repaired to cccDNA in the nucleus: stable, transcribed by host RNA polymerase II, not eliminated by antivirals.
  • pgRNA serves as the mRNA for core and polymerase and as the template for reverse transcription inside the capsid.
  • HBsAg is made in excess as noninfectious subviral particles (vaccine antigen); virions bud with an HBsAg-containing envelope.
  • Reverse transcriptase is the target of lamivudine, entecavir, tenofovir; HDV borrows HBsAg for its envelope.
  1. 1Sticky entrance: HBV attaches to hepatocytes by binding heparan sulfate proteoglycans on the cell surface, then enters through the NTCP receptor (sodium taurocholate cotransporting polypeptide), which explains its liver tropism.
  2. 2Magically repairing hula hoop: After entry the partially double-stranded DNA goes to the nucleus, where host enzymes repair it into covalently closed circular DNA (cccDNA). cccDNA persists as a stable minichromosome, which is why antivirals rarely cure infection.
  3. 3Orange silly string: Host RNA polymerase II transcribes cccDNA into four main RNA transcripts, including the pregenomic RNA (pgRNA), the surface-protein mRNAs, and the X mRNA.
  4. 4Photo booth + photo strip: Two classes of RNAs matter most: subgenomic RNAs, which are translated into the surface proteins, and pgRNA, which is both the mRNA for core protein and polymerase and the template for the new viral genome. (Sketchy's legend attributes core and polymerase to subgenomic RNA; standard teaching assigns them to pgRNA.)check original
  5. 5Bee antenna: HBsAg is translated from the subgenomic (surface) mRNAs and processed in the ER and Golgi. Excess HBsAg is secreted alone as noninfectious subviral particles, which absorb neutralizing antibody and is the antigen used in the vaccine.
  6. 6Chain bib: HBcAg (core protein) is translated from pgRNA (per standard teaching) and forms the viral capsid. It remains within hepatocytes and is not detectable in serum.check original
  7. 7Reverso Transcriptum: Viral polymerase, which has reverse transcriptase activity, is also translated from pgRNA (per standard teaching). It is the target of nucleos(t)ide analogs.check original
  8. 8Chain link playpen + baby hippo bee: In the hepatocyte cytoplasm, pgRNA and the viral polymerase are encapsidated by core protein into an immature viral core.
  9. 9Reverso transcriptum + broken blue ring: Inside the capsid, reverse transcriptase copies pgRNA into DNA, forming the partially double-stranded circular genome. This RNA-to-DNA step is the target of lamivudine, entecavir, and tenofovir.
  10. 10Blue ring thrown on stage: Some immature cores deliver their new DNA to the nucleus, where it is converted to more cccDNA (amplifying the pool) and transcribed again.
  11. 11Hooded hippo bee: Mature cores acquire their envelope containing HBsAg by budding through host membranes (ER/Golgi) and are released as infectious virions (Dane particles).
  12. 12Delta satellites hijacking party: Hepatitis delta virus (HDV) hijacks the HBV surface antigens (HBsAg) as its own envelope, which it needs for entry, assembly, and exit from hepatocytes.
Show the original legend picture

47. Hepatitis C Virus (Flaviviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: bloodborne (IV drug use, needlestick, transfusion before 1992); usually silent acute infection that becomes chronic in ~75-85%.
  • Dx: anti-HCV antibody screen, then HCV RNA PCR to confirm active infection; antibody can be negative early.
  • Complications: cirrhosis, hepatocellular carcinoma; extrahepatic: mixed cryoglobulinemia, membranoproliferative GN, porphyria cutanea tarda.
  • Tx: direct-acting antivirals (e.g., sofosbuvir, an NS5B polymerase inhibitor) cure >95%; no vaccine.
  • Buzzwords: enveloped (+)ssRNA Flaviviridae (Hepacivirus); error-prone RdRp lacking proofreading means antigenic variation, so no vaccine and no lasting immunity.
  1. 1Hippo + C earring: Hepatitis C virus (HCV) is a positive-sense, single-stranded RNA virus in the Flaviviridae family (genus Hepacivirus).
  2. 2Five Flavors Forest Cafe: HCV belongs to the Flaviviridae family, along with dengue, yellow fever, Zika, and West Nile viruses. Unlike most of these, HCV is not arthropod-borne.
  3. 3Warm colors: HCV is an RNA virus with a non-segmented, single-stranded genome, so it does not undergo reassortment.
  4. 4Positive sun: HCV is positive-sense, so its genomic RNA acts directly as mRNA and is translated on entering the cell. Naked genomic RNA is therefore infectious.
  5. 5Robe: HCV is an enveloped virus, so it is relatively sensitive to detergents, drying, and disinfectants, and the envelope glycoproteins are the targets of neutralizing antibodies.
  6. 6No swimming from 3'-5': HCV's RNA-dependent RNA polymerase lacks 3'-5' proofreading exonuclease activity, causing frequent mutations and antigenic variation. This lets the virus evade immunity and makes vaccine development difficult.⭐ Reinfection is possible because antibodies do not give lasting protection.check original
  7. 7Rainbow robe: The varied (rainbow) envelope antigens reflect HCV's high mutation rate, which produces many quasispecies and multiple genotypes. Hypervariable envelope proteins allow immune escape and persistent infection.
  8. 8Needle pen: HCV is primarily bloodborne and spread by parenteral exposure, especially sharing needles or other drug-injection equipment. Needlestick injuries in healthcare settings are another route.⭐ Injection drug use is the most common route of transmission in the US.
  9. 9Red ink: Less commonly, HCV is spread by blood transfusion or organ transplant (rare since donor screening began in 1992), vertically from mother to child at birth, or by sharing contaminated tattoo or razor equipment. Sexual transmission is uncommon.
  10. 10Young hippo + fluctuating ALT buoy: Acute HCV (first 6 months) is often asymptomatic, but may cause fatigue, nausea, fever, abdominal discomfort, and jaundice. ALT fluctuates, with waxing and waning levels from cycles of viral replication and immune-mediated hepatocyte injury.⭐ About 75-85% of acute infections progress to chronic infection.
  11. 11Older hippo: Chronic HCV is often silent for years but causes progressive liver damage with fatigue, jaundice, and abdominal discomfort. Persistent inflammation drives fibrosis and eventually cirrhosis.
  12. 12Liver rock + crabs: Advanced chronic HCV leads to cirrhosis (easy bruising, spider angiomas, ascites) and in some patients hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC; crabs = cancer). Extrahepatic associations include mixed cryoglobulinemia, membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis, and porphyria cutanea tarda.⭐ HCV cirrhosis is a leading indication for liver transplant.
  13. 13Antibody tree: Screen with an HCV antibody test (anti-HCV), which shows whether a person has ever been exposed. It cannot distinguish resolved from active infection, so a positive result must be followed by HCV RNA testing.
  14. 14Strandy orange seaweed: HCV RNA PCR (strands of RNA) is the confirmatory test for active infection. It also quantifies viral load, which is used to monitor treatment response and to determine the genotype.
  15. 15Hippo playing in orange seaweed: A positive HCV RNA test means active infection, because it directly detects viral genetic material in blood, showing that the virus is currently replicating and not just a past exposure.
  16. 16Hippo hiding in orange seaweed: A negative antibody with positive HCV RNA suggests very early (acute, window-period) infection before antibodies develop, or an immunocompromised patient who cannot mount a detectable antibody response. The hippo is hiding because the antibody test misses it.
  17. 17Director: HCV is effectively treated with direct-acting antivirals (DAAs) that block specific steps of the viral life cycle (NS3/4A protease, NS5A, NS5B polymerase inhibitors, e.g., sofosbuvir). Treatment of 8-12 weeks achieves a sustained virologic response (cure) in over 95%.⭐ Sofosbuvir inhibits NS5B RNA polymerase; the -previr drugs are protease inhibitors.
  18. 18Camera A + Camera B + syringe bases: Make sure HCV patients are vaccinated against hepatitis A and B (unless already immune), because superinfection with another hepatitis virus risks fulminant liver failure. There is no HCV vaccine.⭐ No HCV vaccine exists.
  19. 19Animal Filming Permit: In chronic HCV, especially with cirrhosis, screen routinely for hepatocellular carcinoma with liver ultrasound plus serum alpha-fetoprotein (AFP), typically every 6 months.
  20. 20Megaphone: The megaphone represents AFP, the tumor marker announcing HCC. Elevated AFP with a liver mass in a patient with chronic HCV suggests HCC, which is a major reason for long-term surveillance even after viral cure of cirrhotic patients.
Show the original legend picture

48. Herpes Simplex Virus Types 1 & 2 (Herpesviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Painful grouped vesicles on an erythematous base (cold sore, genital ulcers), recurring in the same dermatome
  • HSV-1: gingivostomatitis, herpes labialis, keratitis, temporal lobe encephalitis; HSV-2: genital herpes, neonatal herpes, aseptic meningitis
  • Diagnosis: PCR (CSF PCR for encephalitis/meningitis) or viral culture; Tzanck smear shows multinucleated giant cells but is nonspecific
  • Treatment: acyclovir/valacyclovir (needs viral thymidine kinase); foscarnet for acyclovir-resistant strains
  • Buzzwords: Cowdry A bodies, dendritic corneal ulcer, temporal lobe hemorrhagic necrosis, latency in trigeminal (HSV-1) and sacral (HSV-2) ganglia
  1. 1Hermes sprinting: HSV-1 and HSV-2 are DNA viruses of the Herpesviridae family. They are highly contagious and spread mainly by direct contact with infected skin, mucosa, or body fluids (including saliva and genital secretions), and also vertically from mother to baby (neonatal herpes, usually at delivery).
  2. 2Cool colors: HSV-1 and HSV-2 are DNA viruses (the cool colors group DNA viruses). Most DNA viruses replicate in the nucleus, which is why HSV produces intranuclear inclusions.
  3. 3Enveloping toga: HSV-1 and HSV-2 are enveloped viruses. The envelope (acquired from the host nuclear membrane) makes them fragile outside the body and explains why transmission needs close contact.
  4. 4Double road lines: HSV-1 and HSV-2 are double-stranded, linear DNA viruses.
  5. 5Cow spots: Intranuclear eosinophilic Cowdry type A inclusions are characteristic of cells infected with HSV (also seen with VZV and CMV). Infected cells (keratinocytes or neurons) show a clear nuclear halo, multinucleation, nuclear molding, and chromatin margination.
  6. 6Torch: HSV is part of the TORCHeS group of infections transmitted vertically across the placenta: T = Toxoplasma; O = Other (varicella-zoster virus, parvovirus B19, Zika virus, HIV); R = Rubella; C = CMV; H = HSV; S = Syphilis. Neonatal HSV, usually acquired at delivery from maternal genital infection, causes skin/eye/mouth lesions, encephalitis, or disseminated disease.
  7. 7Red lips: Primary HSV-1 infection often presents as gingivostomatitis, with painful vesicles and ulcerations inside the mouth and around the lips. It is most common in children.
  8. 8Lip ulcers: Herpes labialis (cold sores) presents as clusters of small, painful vesicles on a red base at the vermilion border of the lip. They rupture into shallow ulcers and heal with a yellow crust. It is most commonly caused by HSV-1.
  9. 9Serpent caduceus: Herpes keratoconjunctivitis, mainly HSV-1, causes eye pain, photophobia, redness, tearing, and decreased visual acuity. Fluorescein slit-lamp exam shows branching (dendritic, 'serpiginous') corneal ulcers, which are diagnostic. Treat with topical antivirals; topical steroids alone worsen it.⭐ Dendritic corneal ulcer on fluorescein = HSV keratitis.
  10. 10Red wings on helmet near temples: HSV-1 encephalitis is the most common cause of sporadic encephalitis in the U.S. It causes hemorrhagic necrosis of the temporal lobes, producing fever, seizures, altered mental status, personality changes, and olfactory hallucinations from limbic involvement. Diagnose with CSF PCR (MRI shows temporal lobe changes) and treat empirically with IV acyclovir.⭐ HSV encephalitis = temporal lobe + hemorrhage; start acyclovir before results.
  11. 11Black helmet: HSV-1 encephalitis is the most common cause of sporadic encephalitis in the U.S. It causes hemorrhagic necrosis of the temporal lobes, producing fever, seizures, altered mental status, personality changes, and olfactory hallucinations from limbic involvement. Diagnose with CSF PCR (MRI shows temporal lobe changes) and treat empirically with IV acyclovir.check original
  12. 12Crazy eyes: HSV-1 encephalitis is the most common cause of sporadic encephalitis in the U.S. It causes hemorrhagic necrosis of the temporal lobes, producing fever, seizures, altered mental status, personality changes, and olfactory hallucinations from limbic involvement. The altered mental status and behavioral change reflect temporal lobe/limbic damage. Diagnose with CSF PCR (MRI shows temporal lobe changes) and treat empirically with IV acyclovir.
  13. 13Three helmet gems: After primary infection HSV-1 remains latent in the trigeminal ganglia (sensory neurons). Reactivation by stress, sunlight (UV), fever, or immunosuppression causes cold sores, keratoconjunctivitis, or, rarely, temporal lobe encephalitis.
  14. 14Dew drops on roses: HSV lesions are painful grouped vesicles or pustules on an erythematous base, classically likened to 'dew drops on a rose petal.' They rupture into shallow ulcers, crust over as they heal, and recur because of viral latency.
  15. 15Dew drops on finger: Herpetic whitlow is an HSV infection of the finger with painful, clustered vesicles on an erythematous base. It is classically seen in healthcare workers and dentists (contact with oral secretions) and in thumb-sucking children.
  16. 16Target stamps on hand and arm: Erythema multiforme is a hypersensitivity reaction triggered by certain drugs and infections, with HSV being a classic infectious trigger. It produces target lesions (dusky center, pale edematous zone, outer erythematous ring) that start on the backs of the hands and feet and spread centrally.⭐ HSV is the most common infectious cause of erythema multiforme.
  17. 17Ruffles by groin: Initial genital herpes, often HSV-2 (HSV-1 is increasingly common), causes painful grouped vesicles on an erythematous base in the genital or perianal region, with shallow ulcers, dysuria, and tender inguinal lymphadenopathy. It is spread by sexual contact, including by asymptomatic shedding.
  18. 18Codpiece: HSV-2 establishes latency mainly in the sacral ganglia. Reactivation causes recurrent genital or perianal lesions, usually milder and shorter than the primary episode.
  19. 19Neck brace: HSV-2 is associated with aseptic (lymphocytic) meningitis, particularly during primary genital infection. Patients have headache, fever, and neck stiffness, with lymphocytic pleocytosis on CSF analysis (diagnose with CSF PCR). It can recur (Mollaret meningitis).
  20. 20Tank + giant spots: A Tzanck smear (scraping of a lesion base) shows multinucleated giant cells in HSV infection. It is nonspecific, since VZV also produces them, and has been replaced by PCR or viral culture for specific diagnosis.
  21. 21Violet recycling bin: Acyclovir and valacyclovir are used for treatment and for prophylaxis (suppressive therapy) against recurrent outbreaks. They are guanosine analogs activated by viral thymidine kinase and terminate viral DNA synthesis by inhibiting viral DNA polymerase. Resistance (thymidine kinase mutation) is treated with foscarnet.
Show the original legend picture

49. HHV-6 & Roseola (Herpesviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: infant 6 months to 2 years with high fever for 3-5 days, then a pink rash as fever breaks; febrile seizure is a common complication.
  • Micro: enveloped, linear dsDNA virus, Herpesviridae; infects CD4+ T cells.
  • Diagnosis: clinical; PCR or serology if needed.
  • Treatment/prevention: supportive (antipyretics, hydration); self-limited.
  • Buzzwords: roseola infantum, exanthem subitum, sixth disease, rash after fever, febrile seizures.
  1. 1"6" on Hermes: Human herpesvirus 6 (HHV-6) is a DNA virus in the Herpesviridae family.
  2. 2Hermes: HHV-6 belongs to the Herpesviridae family (Hermes = herpes).
  3. 3Cool colors: HHV-6 is a DNA virus (cool colors = DNA virus).
  4. 4Helper squire + 4 shaped buckle + 4 feathers in cap: HHV-6 primarily targets CD4+ helper T cells, which can cause immunosuppression by reducing the CD4+ population.⭐ HHV-6 can reactivate in transplant recipients.
  5. 5Roses: HHV-6 is the main cause of roseola infantum, also called exanthem subitum ("sudden rash") or sixth disease.⭐ HHV-7 is a less common cause.
  6. 6Sweat: The fever of roseola is sudden in onset and often high, reaching 39-40 C (102-104 F), typically lasting about 3-5 days.
  7. 74 suns flag: Same fever feature: high fever, 39-40 C (102-104 F), lasting about 3-5 days; the 4 suns represent the 4 days of fever.
  8. 8Child sweating + shaking: Febrile seizures are a possible complication, typically occurring during the high-fever phase before the rash appears.⭐ Roseola is a classic cause of a first febrile seizure.
  9. 9Blue flames: After the high fever resolves, a diffuse pink, lacy, blanching maculopapular rash appears, typically starting on the trunk and spreading to the neck and extremities; it may spare the face, is non-itchy and fades within 1-2 days.⭐ Rash appears AFTER the fever breaks.
  10. 10Lacy pink dress: Same rash: diffuse pink, lacy, blanching maculopapular, starting on the trunk, nonpruritic, fading in 1-2 days.
  11. 11Baby: Roseola commonly affects children aged 6 months to 2 years; treatment is supportive (fever control, hydration) and the illness is self-limited.
  12. 12Holding + caring for baby in arms: Supportive care (antipyretics, hydration) is the treatment; roseola resolves on its own, typically in children aged 6 months to 2 years.
Show the original legend picture

50. HHV-8 and Kaposi Sarcoma (Herpesviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: violaceous skin/mucosal plaques and nodules (often hard palate) in an AIDS patient with CD4 <200; also endemic African, classic elderly Mediterranean/Eastern European men.
  • Microbiology: enveloped, linear double-stranded DNA virus (Herpesviridae); infects endothelial cells and B cells; spread by saliva and sexual contact.
  • Diagnosis: biopsy showing proliferating spindle-shaped endothelial cells, extravasated RBCs, lymphocytic infiltrate, and HHV-8 (LANA) positivity.
  • Treatment: antiretroviral therapy to restore immunity in HIV-associated disease; chemotherapy or local therapy for extensive or visceral disease.
  • Buzzwords: 'AIDS-defining', 'violaceous', 'spindle cells', 'VEGF', 'primary effusion lymphoma', 'mimics bacillary angiomatosis (Bartonella)'.
  1. 1'8' emblem: Human herpesvirus 8 (HHV-8), a DNA virus in the Herpesviridae family; also called Kaposi sarcoma-associated herpesvirus (KSHV).
  2. 2Hermes emblem: HHV-8 is the causative agent of Kaposi sarcoma (the 'H' for herpes and Hermes as a messenger-style cue).⭐ HHV-8 is necessary but not sufficient; immunosuppression is the usual trigger.
  3. 3Kate's Posies: 'Kate's Posies' sounds like Kaposi's: HHV-8 causes Kaposi sarcoma, a vascular tumor.
  4. 4Cool colors: HHV-8 is a double-stranded DNA virus (cool colors = DNA viruses).
  5. 5Immunocompromised cane + AIDS ribbon: Kaposi sarcoma is an AIDS-defining condition and signals advanced HIV with profound immunosuppression, which allows HHV-8 reactivation and proliferation of infected endothelial cells. Treatment is mainly antiretroviral therapy (ART) to restore immune function.⭐ Regression with ART alone is common in HIV-associated KS.
  6. 6Violet petals on nose and arms: Kaposi sarcoma lesions are violaceous (purple-red) vascular tumors from HHV-8-infected endothelial cells. They occur on skin and mucous membranes (e.g., nose, extremities) as patches, plaques, macules, or nodules, sometimes reddish.
  7. 7Arterial hose: Kaposi sarcoma is a vascular proliferation: angiogenesis forms tumor nodules in skin, mucous membranes, and internal organs; the neoplastic cells are of endothelial/mesenchymal origin and the vascularity gives the color.
  8. 8VEG fertilizer: HHV-8 dysregulates vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) signaling, driving abnormal angiogenesis in Kaposi sarcoma.
  9. 9Colon covering: Kaposi sarcoma lesions can involve mucous membranes lining the GI tract (stomach, small intestine, colon), sometimes causing bleeding.
  10. 10Violets on arched ceiling: Kaposi sarcoma lesions frequently appear on the hard palate (arched ceiling = palate).⭐ Oral/palatal lesions are a classic AIDS-associated site.
  11. 11B-cell archer + 'B' rating: HHV-8 can infect B cells and cause primary effusion lymphoma, a B-cell lymphoma presenting as malignant effusions in body cavities (pleural, pericardial, peritoneal), typically in immunocompromised patients.⭐ Also linked to multicentric Castleman disease.
  12. 12African azaleas: Endemic (African) Kaposi sarcoma occurs in sub-Saharan Africa where HHV-8 is common and spreads by saliva in childhood. It occurs without HIV, affects children and young adults, and is often aggressive with lymph node and visceral involvement.
  13. 13Russian rhododendrons: Classic Kaposi sarcoma is sporadic, affecting elderly men of Eastern European, Mediterranean, or Russian descent without HIV. It is likely HHV-8 reactivation from age-related immune decline, with slow-growing violaceous lesions on the lower extremities and minimal visceral involvement.
  14. 14Bart the leopard: Kaposi sarcoma can resemble bacillary angiomatosis (Bartonella henselae) and is distinguished by histology. KS: spindle-shaped endothelial cells forming vascular channels, lymphocytic infiltrate, extravasated RBCs, HHV-8 positive. Bacillary angiomatosis: lobular capillary proliferation with neutrophilic infiltrate, organisms seen on Warthin-Starry silver stain.⭐ Warthin-Starry stain points to Bartonella, not KS.
