51. Histoplasma capsulatum

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: Ohio/Mississippi River valley, bat or bird droppings (caves, chicken coops); flu-like lung illness, or hepatosplenomegaly/pancytopenia in AIDS
  • Micro: thermally dimorphic fungus - mold at 25 C (environment), small yeast at 37 C (body); tiny budding yeast inside macrophages
  • Diagnosis: urine/serum Histoplasma antigen; yeast in macrophages on biopsy or marrow smear; culture on Sabouraud agar
  • Treatment: itraconazole for mild disease; amphotericin B first for severe/disseminated, then itraconazole step-down
  • Buzzwords: 'macrophage full of yeast', 'caves and bats', Ohio River valley, calcified hilar nodes, mimics TB and sarcoid
  1. 1Historian: The icon is a 'Histo' historian: Histoplasma capsulatum is a dimorphic fungus (it changes form depending on temperature).⭐ Dimorphic fungi: Histoplasma, Blastomyces, Coccidioides, Paracoccidioides, Sporothrix.
  2. 2Bat: Histoplasma lives in soil enriched by bat and bird droppings, so exposure comes from caves, bat-roosting sites, chicken coops and old buildings with bird droppings.⭐ Classic exposure: spelunking, cleaning chicken coops, demolition of old buildings.
  3. 3Ohio and Mississippi River valley map: Histoplasma is endemic to the central and eastern United States, especially the Ohio and Mississippi River valleys (also parts of Central and South America).
  4. 4Coughing: Infection is acquired by inhaling airborne spores (microconidia) from the mold form in contaminated soil; it is not spread person to person.⭐ No person-to-person transmission.
  5. 5Birdcage: In the lungs the spores convert to yeast that are engulfed by macrophages; the macrophage is the 'cage' that holds the organisms (intracellular yeast). Yeast are small oval budding cells, smaller than a red blood cell.⭐ Histo = 'hiding in macrophages' - the yeast survive and multiply inside phagocytes.
  6. 6Small spots in macrophage puddle: Same fact seen on microscopy: a macrophage packed with many tiny intracellular yeast (the 'spots'), each smaller than an RBC, often with a clear halo that mimics a capsule (but there is no capsule).⭐ Lots of tiny yeast inside macrophages on a bone marrow or blood smear suggests disseminated histoplasmosis.
  7. 7Red stalactite: Red = blood. Histoplasma antigen can be detected in serum for rapid diagnosis, especially in severe or disseminated disease.
  8. 8Yellow stalactite: Yellow = urine. Histoplasma antigen can be detected in urine, which is the most sensitive rapid test in disseminated or severe disease (antigen can cross-react with Blastomyces).⭐ Urine antigen is the best rapid test in disseminated disease.
  9. 9Dimorphic butterfly: Histoplasma is thermally dimorphic: it grows as a mold (hyphae with microconidia and tuberculate macroconidia) in cooler environments (25-30 C) and as yeast at body temperature (35-37 C).⭐ 'Cold = mold, hot = yeast'.
  10. 10Lung shaped rocks: Inhaled microconidia reach the alveoli, convert to yeast in the warm lung, are ingested by macrophages, and replicate within them; the organism can then spread within the lung or through the blood (and lymphatics) to other organs.
  11. 11White stalactites in lung rocks: Acute pulmonary histoplasmosis is often asymptomatic or mild (fever, cough, myalgias, chest discomfort), but can be severe in the immunocompromised or after heavy exposure. Healing leaves calcifications in the lungs and in mediastinal and hilar lymph nodes, reflecting granulomatous containment.⭐ Calcified granulomas and hilar nodes on CXR can resemble old TB.
  12. 12Tuberculosis cactus: Chronic pulmonary histoplasmosis (often in patients with underlying lung disease such as COPD) causes cough, fatigue, weight loss and upper-lobe cavitary lesions, closely mimicking tuberculosis.⭐ Histoplasma can mimic TB and sarcoidosis.
  13. 13Spotted leg rocks: Erythema nodosum can occur as a hypersensitivity reaction to fungal antigens: painful red nodules from inflammation of subcutaneous fat (septal panniculitis), typically on both shins.
  14. 14Liver + spleen spots: Disseminated histoplasmosis causes hepatomegaly, splenomegaly and lymphadenopathy as yeast proliferate within macrophages of the reticuloendothelial system; other findings can include pancytopenia, oral ulcers and adrenal involvement.⭐ Adrenal insufficiency and pancytopenia can occur in disseminated disease.
  15. 15Immunocompromised cane: Disseminated histoplasmosis is more common in the immunocompromised (advanced HIV with low CD4 counts, transplant recipients, patients on TNF-alpha inhibitors or other immunosuppressants), as well as infants and the elderly.⭐ TNF-alpha inhibitors raise risk of reactivation of histoplasmosis.
  16. 16Pinecones: Pinecones sound like itra-CONE-azole: azoles, with itraconazole preferred, treat mild to moderate histoplasmosis and are used for step-down maintenance after severe disease.⭐ Itraconazole is the oral azole of choice.
  17. 17Amphibians: Amphibians sound like AMPHOtericin B: severe or disseminated histoplasmosis is treated initially with amphotericin B, followed by step-down itraconazole for maintenance.⭐ Amphotericin B for severe disease, then itraconazole for months to a year in disseminated disease.

52. HIV Life Cycle (Retroviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • HIV infects CD4+ cells; the life cycle runs from binding and fusion to reverse transcription, integration, assembly, budding, and maturation.
  • gag (p24, p17), pol (reverse transcriptase, integrase, protease), env (gp160 cleaved to gp120 and gp41).
  • gp120 binds CD4 plus CCR5 or CXCR4; gp41 mediates fusion; reverse transcriptase makes dsDNA that integrase inserts into the host genome.
  • Drug targets: fusion/entry (enfuvirtide, maraviroc), RT (NRTIs, NNRTIs), integrase inhibitors, protease inhibitors.
  • gag-pol-env, gp160, gp120 antigenic variation and glycosylation, neutralizing antibodies target gp120.
  1. 1Ruined "4" castle: The HIV life cycle primarily attacks host CD4+ cells, including macrophages and helper T cells (the ruined castle is the destroyed CD4+ cell).
  2. 2Helper squire with "4"-shaped straps: HIV targets CD4+ cells such as helper T cells (and macrophages).
  3. 3Invading ship: Assembled mature, infective virions go on to infect the next host cell.
  4. 4Gagged prisoners in core of ship: The gag gene encodes the HIV core proteins (p24 capsid, p17 matrix).⭐ gag = group-specific antigen (core).
  5. 524 hour sundial + "1" dagger and "7" sword: Reinforces that gag encodes p24 (sundial) and p17 (the 1 and 7).
  6. 6Envoy coming ashore: The env gene encodes the HIV envelope proteins.
  7. 7"The b16 One" + 120 siege towers + 41 grappling hooks: env encodes the precursor gp160, which is cleaved into gp120 and gp41 (the 120 siege towers and 41 grappling hooks), which facilitate entry into host cells.⭐ gp160 is cleaved into gp120 and gp41 by a host protease.
  8. 8Paul the blacksmith producing spell books, keys, and swords: The pol gene encodes reverse transcriptase (spell book), integrase (key), and protease (sword).⭐ pol = polymerase; target of most antiretrovirals.
  9. 9"120" siege tower binding to castle wall: gp120, an env product, binds host receptors to allow HIV entry into the cell.
  10. 10"4" helper squire + CXCR4 flag + CCR5 banner: gp120 binds the CD4 receptor and then a chemokine co-receptor, CCR5 or CXCR4, to gain entry.⭐ Maraviroc blocks CCR5.
  11. 11"41" grappling hook: gp41, an env product, mediates fusion of the viral and host cell membranes.⭐ Enfuvirtide (T-20) inhibits gp41-mediated fusion.
  12. 12"Reverso Transcriptum" spell book + doubling army: Reverse transcriptase (a pol product) transcribes single-stranded viral RNA into double-stranded DNA.⭐ Target of NRTIs and NNRTIs; error-prone, lacks proofreading.
  13. 13Integrase key + DNA staircase: Integrase (a pol product) integrates the double-stranded viral DNA (provirus) into the host cell DNA.⭐ Target of raltegravir, dolutegravir (-tegravir).
  14. 14Endoplasmic reef + golgi reef: The env precursor gp160 is processed through the host cell's endoplasmic reticulum and Golgi apparatus, where it is glycosylated and cleaved into gp120 and gp41.
  15. 15Boat budding from shore: New HIV virions form by budding from the host cell plasma membrane, acquiring their envelope.
  16. 16Sword + huge hunk of meat: HIV protease (a pol product) cleaves the viral polyproteins (e.g. Gag-Pol) into functional proteins, enabling virion maturation.⭐ Target of protease inhibitors (-navir).
  17. 17Antibody arrows + "120" siege tower: Neutralizing antibodies (host antibodies against viral antigens that provide some immunity) mainly target gp120.
  18. 18Multicolored "120" siege tower: gp120 shows a high degree of antigenic variation, preventing an effective immune response.⭐ Antigenic variation hinders vaccine development.
  19. 19Glycosyl shields: gp120 is heavily glycosylated, forming a glycan shield that protects it from immune surveillance.

53. HIV Overview (Retroviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Acute retroviral syndrome (fever, rash, lymphadenopathy), then asymptomatic latency of about 8-10 years, then AIDS with opportunistic infections.
  • Enveloped diploid (+)ssRNA retrovirus (Lentivirus); gp120/gp41 envelope, p24 capsid; reverse transcriptase makes DNA that integrates; infects CD4+ cells via CD4 plus CCR5 or CXCR4.
  • Screen with 4th-generation Ag/Ab immunoassay (p24 antigen plus HIV-1/2 antibodies); confirm with HIV-1/2 differentiation assay; HIV RNA NAT for acute infection.
  • Antiretroviral therapy (combination); AIDS defined by CD4 <200 cells/uL or an AIDS-defining illness; transmission by blood, sexual contact, and vertically.
  • gp120, gp41, p24, CCR5/CXCR4, CD4 <200, window period, p24 antigen and NAT become positive first.
  1. 1White wizard: Human immunodeficiency virus (HIV), a member of the Retroviridae family (genus Lentivirus).
  2. 2Crutch staff: Untreated HIV infection almost always progresses to AIDS (acquired immunodeficiency syndrome).
  3. 3Wizard's apprentice pointing with 2 fingers: There are two major types: HIV-1 and HIV-2. HIV-2 is less common and less virulent than HIV-1.⭐ HIV-2 is mostly in West Africa and is not detected by some HIV-1 assays.
  4. 4Warm colors: HIV is a positive-sense single-stranded RNA virus.
  5. 5Positive sun: Reinforces that HIV is a positive-sense RNA virus.
  6. 6"Reverso Transcriptum" spellbook: HIV encodes reverse transcriptase (an RNA-dependent DNA polymerase) that converts viral RNA into DNA, which then integrates into the host genome.⭐ Target of NRTIs and NNRTIs.
  7. 72 orange dragons: HIV has a diploid genome: each virion contains two copies of positive-sense single-stranded RNA.⭐ Diploid genome allows recombination and genetic diversity.
  8. 8Conical wizard hat: The HIV virion has a conical capsid made of p24 (capsid protein) subunits.
  9. 924h sundial on hat: Reinforces that the conical capsid is built from p24.
  10. 10Protective chainmail with "1" dagger and "7" sword: The HIV virion has a protective shell (matrix) made of p17 subunits, lying just beneath the envelope.
  11. 11Enveloped by robe: HIV is enveloped: a lipid bilayer studded with glycoproteins.
  12. 12"41" pipe stem: The HIV envelope has glycoprotein complexes composed of gp41 (transmembrane protein) and gp120 (outer protein); each envelope complex is a trimer.⭐ gp41 mediates fusion; the T-20 (enfuvirtide) fusion inhibitor targets it.
  13. 13"120" pipe bowl: gp120 is the outer envelope protein that binds CD4 and the chemokine co-receptor; it sits atop gp41 in a trimeric complex.⭐ gp120 binds CD4 and the co-receptor.
  14. 14Helper squire with 4-shaped strap: HIV infects CD4+ cells, including helper T lymphocytes.
  15. 15Macro-cage with "4" design: HIV also infects CD4+ macrophages (and dendritic cells).
  16. 16"CCRV" banner: HIV must bind the CD4 receptor AND a chemokine co-receptor (CCR5 or CXCR4) to enter the cell; this icon is CCR5.⭐ Maraviroc blocks CCR5; CCR5-delta32 homozygotes are resistant.
  17. 17"CXCR4" flag: The other co-receptor is CXCR4; HIV needs CD4 plus either CCR5 or CXCR4 to enter.
  18. 18"CCRV" banner held by squire: In early infection, HIV is tropic for the CCR5 co-receptor (R5 strains, macrophage-tropic).
  19. 19"CXCR4" flag held in squire's other hand: In later infection, HIV may switch to CXCR4 tropism (X4 strains, T-cell tropic).
  20. 20Bloody potion: HIV is blood-borne: transmitted by shared needles in IV drug use, needlestick injuries, and transfusion.
  21. 21Secretion potions: HIV is transmitted sexually through semen and rectal or vaginal fluids.
  22. 22Pregnant potion poured vertically: HIV can be transmitted vertically from pregnant person to infant during pregnancy, delivery, or breastfeeding.⭐ Risk is greatly reduced by maternal ART, and zidovudine to the neonate.
  23. 23Milk potion poured vertically: Same vertical transmission: breastfeeding is a route of mother-to-child HIV transmission.
  24. 24Orange viral beam: The orange beam represents HIV RNA level (viral load) in plasma.
  25. 25Blue beam targeting helper squire: The blue beam represents the CD4+ T-lymphocyte count ("CD4 count").
  26. 26Initial rise of orange beam: Early in infection, viral RNA rises rapidly because the host immune response has not yet developed.
  27. 27Initial fall of blue beam: Early in infection, the CD4+ T-cell count begins to fall.
  28. 28Sickly goblin with flame helmet: Acute retroviral syndrome (mononucleosis-like illness) occurs early: fever, fatigue, malaise, myalgias, arthralgias, night sweats, generalized lymphadenopathy, sore throat, GI symptoms, headache, and sometimes a maculopapular rash on the trunk, face, or extremities.⭐ Peak viremia with a negative antibody test: use HIV RNA NAT.
  29. 29"Decade of the Ring": Early infection is followed by clinical latency, an asymptomatic period lasting about 8-10 years without treatment.⭐ Virus replicates in lymphoid tissue during latency.
  30. 30Flattening of orange beam: During clinical latency, HIV RNA settles at a relatively stable level (viral set point) as the immune response partially controls replication.
  31. 31Slow decline in blue beam: During latency, the CD4+ count gradually falls because of ongoing destruction by HIV.
  32. 32Orange beam sharply upturning: As HIV progresses to AIDS, viral RNA rises sharply as the immune response fails.
  33. 33Blue beam attacking helper squire: As HIV progresses to AIDS, the CD4+ count continues to fall.
  34. 34"200" key: AIDS = CD4+ count <200 cells/uL OR an AIDS-defining illness (opportunistic infection or certain malignancy).⭐ CD4 <200: start PCP prophylaxis (TMP-SMX); <100: toxoplasma; <50: MAC (historically).
  35. 35Bacterial, viral, and fungal lanterns + crab: AIDS-defining illnesses include certain opportunistic infections (bacterial, viral, fungal, or parasitic) and specific malignancies (the crab represents cancer, e.g. Kaposi sarcoma, primary CNS lymphoma, invasive cervical cancer).
  36. 36"1" and "2" antibody quivers + 24 sunburst: Screen with the fourth-generation immunoassay, which detects HIV-1 and HIV-2 antibodies plus p24 antigen; it is positive if either antibody or antigen is present.⭐ 4th-generation Ag/Ab test is the preferred screen.
  37. 37Different antibody arrows: A positive fourth-generation test is followed by an HIV-1/HIV-2 antibody differentiation immunoassay, which distinguishes HIV-1 from HIV-2.⭐ Older confirmatory Western blot has been replaced.
  38. 3824 sunburst + window: p24 antigen becomes detectable before antibodies (in the serologic window), which helps identify acute HIV infection.
  39. 39Gnats in window near orange sun: Nucleic acid testing (NAT) for HIV RNA becomes positive even earlier than p24 antigen or antibodies (during the window period), and is used to confirm acute infection.⭐ NAT is the earliest test (about 10 days after infection).
Show the original legend picture

54. Human Papillomavirus (Papillomaviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Warts: common warts (HPV 1-4) in kids, anogenital warts (HPV 6, 11); cervical dysplasia/cancer (HPV 16, 18) with postcoital bleeding
  • Naked (non-enveloped) icosahedral dsDNA virus; E6 degrades p53, E7 inactivates Rb
  • Dx: Pap smear/HPV DNA testing; koilocytes (perinuclear halo, raisinoid nucleus); acetowhitening on colposcopy
  • Prevention: Gardasil 9 (recombinant L1 capsid vaccine, types 6, 11, 16, 18, 31, 33, 45, 52, 58); wart treatment is topical/destructive (podophyllotoxin, imiquimod, cryotherapy)
  • Buzzwords: koilocytes, cauliflower warts, E6 = p53, E7 = Rb, most common STI
  1. 1Pill bugs: Human papillomavirus (HPV) is a DNA virus in the family Papillomaviridae.
  2. 2Cool colors: Papillomaviruses are DNA viruses (circular double-stranded DNA).
  3. 3Statue of David: Papillomaviruses lack an envelope, so they are naked viruses.⭐ Naked viruses resist drying and detergents, so they spread well on surfaces.
  4. 4Children holding up 1 & 4 fingers: Common warts (verruca vulgaris) are caused by HPV types 1-4.
  5. 5Pill bugs on young kids: Common warts commonly affect children and young adults: firm, rough, hyperkeratotic papules with a verrucous surface, often on the hands and fingers; black dots from thrombosed capillaries are often seen on the surface.⭐ Pinpoint black dots in a wart are thrombosed capillaries.
  6. 6Kids playing together: Common warts spread by direct skin-to-skin contact or contaminated surfaces (fomites), especially through skin trauma or microabrasions.
  7. 7"11" and "6" kids: HPV types 6 and 11 cause anogenital warts (condyloma acuminata) and laryngeal papillomatosis.⭐ Low-risk types: 6 and 11 cause benign warts, not cancer.
  8. 8Eating bug: HPV 6 and 11 cause laryngeal (recurrent respiratory) papillomatosis: multiple exophytic, wart-like, whitish cauliflower-like papillomas on the vocal cords and larynx that can obstruct the airway and cause hoarseness, chronic cough or respiratory distress.⭐ Acquired by passage through an infected birth canal; presents in a child with hoarseness or stridor.
  9. 9Putting bug down pants: Anogenital warts (condyloma acuminata) are sexually transmitted and most often caused by HPV 6 and 11; they are soft, flesh-colored or pink, cauliflower-like papules or plaques on the genital and perianal areas.
  10. 10Accumulating bugs in jar: Anogenital warts (condyloma acuminata) are sexually transmitted and most often caused by HPV 6 and 11; lesions can multiply and coalesce into larger plaques in the genital and perianal areas.⭐ Treat with podophyllotoxin, imiquimod, trichloroacetic acid or cryotherapy; they are not a sign of cancer risk.
  11. 1116 year old's car keys: HPV types 16, 18, 31 and 33 are high-risk types linked to anogenital and squamous cell cancers, including cancers of the cervix, vulva, vagina, penis and anus.⭐ Types 16 and 18 cause about 70% of cervical cancers.
  12. 1218 year old's voting sticker: HPV types 16, 18, 31 and 33 are high-risk types linked to anogenital and squamous cell cancers (cervix, vulva, vagina, penis, anus).
  13. 13Crab by 16 and 18 y/o: HPV types 16, 18, 31 and 33 are high-risk types linked to anogenital and squamous cell cancers; HPV is also implicated in oropharyngeal cancers.
  14. 14Bug on seat: HPV types 16, 18, 31 and 33 are high-risk types linked to anogenital and squamous cell cancers, including cervix, vulva, vagina, penis and anus.
  15. 15Kissing couple: HPV is transmitted mainly by sexual contact (vaginal, anal or oral) and is the most common sexually transmitted infection worldwide.
  16. 16Syringe fence spikes: The original Gardasil vaccine is a recombinant subunit (L1 capsid protein) vaccine against HPV 6, 11, 16 and 18; it is no longer distributed in the U.S. because Gardasil 9 replaced it, protecting against nine types (6, 11, 16, 18, 31, 33, 45, 52, 58).⭐ Vaccine is non-infectious (virus-like particles); routinely given at age 11-12, before sexual exposure.
  17. 17Fence around 6, 11, 16, & 18 year olds: Gardasil protected against HPV 6, 11, 16 and 18 and has been replaced by Gardasil 9, which covers nine serotypes (6, 11, 16, 18, 31, 33, 45, 52, 58).
  18. 18Children outside fence with 1 & 4 fingers: The HPV vaccines (Gardasil/Gardasil 9) do not cover HPV types 1-4, which cause common warts.
  19. 19"E" fork + "6" shrimp: The E6 protein of high-risk HPV (especially types 16 and 18) targets the p53 tumor suppressor for degradation, leading to uncontrolled cell proliferation and cancer risk.⭐ E6 = p53 degradation (via ubiquitin ligase E6AP).
  20. 20Crab cracker + cell cycle table: p53 is a regulatory checkpoint that halts the cell cycle at the G1-to-S transition when cellular stress or DNA damage is detected.
  21. 21"E" fork + "7" straw in root beer: The E7 protein of high-risk HPV inactivates the Rb tumor suppressor (releasing E2F), causing unchecked cell cycle progression and increasing cancer risk.⭐ E7 = Rb (7 and "root beer").
  22. 22Face smear: Pap smears screen for early cervical changes (dysplasia) that can progress to HPV-caused cervical cancer; postcoital bleeding can be an early manifestation of cervical dysplasia or carcinoma.⭐ Screening: cytology from age 21 (HPV co-testing from 30 per guidelines).
  23. 23Blue sunny-side up egg: Koilocytes, seen in HPV-infected cells of the cervical transformation zone, are squamous cells with perinuclear clearing (halo), enlarged irregular nuclei and frequent binucleation, resembling a sunny-side-up egg (halo = white, dense nucleus = yolk).⭐ Koilocytes are pathognomonic for HPV cytopathic effect.
  24. 24AIDS ribbon: HIV/AIDS and other immunosuppression (organ transplant, long-term corticosteroids) increase the risk of persistent HPV infection and complications such as anogenital warts, cervical dysplasia and cervical cancer, because of impaired viral clearance.⭐ Cervical cancer is AIDS-defining in HIV.
  25. 25Immunocompromised cane: Immunosuppressed patients (HIV/AIDS, transplant recipients, chronic corticosteroids) have impaired viral clearance, increasing risk of persistent HPV, warts, dysplasia and cervical cancer.
Show the original legend picture

55. Influenzavirus (Orthomyxoviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: winter-season abrupt fever, chills, myalgias, headache, dry cough and sore throat; complications include pneumonia (S. aureus, S. pneumoniae), Reye syndrome, GBS
  • Micro: enveloped, segmented (8 segments for A and B), negative-sense ssRNA; replicates in the nucleus; HA binds sialic acid, NA releases virions
  • Diagnosis: rapid antigen test or, better, RT-PCR of nasopharyngeal swab
  • Treatment/prevention: oseltamivir or zanamivir (best within 48 h of onset); amantadine/rimantadine only against A (resistance common); annual vaccine (inactivated or live attenuated intranasal)
  • Buzzwords: drift = point mutations (epidemics), shift = reassortment (pandemics), no aspirin in kids, 'post-flu staph pneumonia'
  1. 1"FLU-oride": Pun on 'fluoride' = FLU: the character stands for influenza virus, a negative-sense RNA virus of the family Orthomyxoviridae.
  2. 2Orthodontist: Orthodontist = ORTHOmyxoviridae, the virus family that includes influenza viruses (negative-sense, segmented RNA viruses).
  3. 3Warm colors: Icon reminds that influenza viruses are negative-sense RNA viruses: the genome is complementary to mRNA and cannot be translated directly.⭐ The virion carries its own RNA-dependent RNA polymerase because the genome must be copied to mRNA first.
  4. 4Moon with negative-sign-shaped clouds: The negative signs again indicate a negative-sense RNA genome (needs a virion-associated RNA-dependent RNA polymerase to make mRNA).
  5. 5Enveloping coat: Influenza viruses are enveloped (lipid envelope with HA and NA spikes), so they are inactivated by detergents and drying and are less stable in the environment than naked viruses.
  6. 6"FLU-oride...it's as easy as ABC!": The orthomyxovirus family includes influenza A, B and C. Influenza A (humans, birds, pigs) causes pandemics, B (humans only) causes epidemics, and C causes mild disease.⭐ Only influenza A undergoes antigenic shift and infects animals.
  7. 7Octopus babies in helmet: Influenza replicates in the host cell nucleus (the helmet = nucleus), unlike most RNA viruses, which replicate in the cytoplasm. The virus uses host nuclear machinery (e.g., cap snatching).⭐ Influenza and retroviruses are exceptions to cytoplasmic RNA replication; influenza replicates in the nucleus.
  8. 88 octopus tentacles: The genome is segmented: influenza A and B each have 8 separate negative-sense RNA segments, influenza C has 7. Segmentation allows reassortment between strains.⭐ Segmented genome = reassortment = antigenic shift.
  9. 9"DoKtor Drift" + one letter altered: Antigenic drift: point mutations in the hemagglutinin (HA) or neuraminidase (NA) genes cause minor changes in these proteins (one letter altered). They let the virus partially escape prior immunity, requiring annual vaccine updates and causing seasonal epidemics.⭐ Drift = minor, point mutations, seasonal epidemics (influenza A and B).
  10. 10"Night sHift": Antigenic shift (influenza A only): reassortment of genome segments between two different influenza A strains infecting the same cell gives a novel virus with major changes in HA and/or NA. If it spreads easily and humans have little immunity, it causes a pandemic.⭐ Shift = major, reassortment, pandemics (e.g., 2009 H1N1 'swine flu').
  11. 11Mixed H's and N's: The mixed H's and N's represent exchange of HA and NA gene segments between strains (often involving animal reservoirs such as pigs and birds), producing new subtypes like the 2009 H1N1 swine flu pandemic strain.⭐ Pigs are 'mixing vessels' for human, avian and swine flu viruses.
  12. 12Multicolored curtains: Influenza shows antigenic variation by both drift (minor mutations) and shift (reassortment), which is why immunity to one strain does not protect fully against the next.
  13. 13Heme aquarium + RBC rocks: Hemagglutinin (HA) lets virions agglutinate red blood cells (the 'heme' aquarium) and bind sialic acid on host cell surfaces to enter the cell by endocytosis. HA is the target of neutralizing antibodies.⭐ HA = attachment and entry (hemagglutinin binds sialic acid).
  14. 14Branched heme aquarium plant: The branched plant represents the sialic acid residues (terminal sugars on glycoproteins) that HA binds on respiratory epithelial cells.
  15. 15Multicolored suckers: There are many HA subtypes; H1, H2 and H3 are the most common in human infection. The HA type determines cell tropism, i.e., which cells and species the virus can infect (human-adapted HA binds alpha-2,6 sialic acid).
  16. 162 "M" shell opening: The M2 proton channel in the influenza A envelope allows protons into the virion, lowering pH to promote uncoating (release of ribonucleoproteins into the cytoplasm after endocytosis).
  17. 17Manta dining + hiding in 2 "M" shell: Manta = AMANTAdine (and rimantadine) plugging the M2 channel and blocking uncoating of influenza A only. Rarely used now because of resistance and side effects. Amantadine also increases dopamine release and blocks NMDA glutamate receptors, so it is used in Parkinson disease.⭐ Amantadine and rimantadine work only against influenza A; amantadine is also used in Parkinson disease.
  18. 18Nurse Asst.'s scalpels: Neuraminidase (NA) ('Nurse Asst.' = NA) cleaves sialic acid on the host cell, which releases newly formed virions and prevents them from clumping at the cell surface.⭐ NA = release of progeny virions.
  19. 19Scalpels cleaving branched plant: The scalpels (neuraminidase) cut the branched plant (sialic acid residues), freeing budding virions from the infected cell.
  20. 20Nurse Asst. Tammy V: Tammy V = Tamiflu (oseltamivir), a neuraminidase inhibitor; zanamivir is another. Taking over the 'Nurse Asst.' means blocking NA.⭐ Oseltamivir (oral) and zanamivir (inhaled) work against influenza A and B.
  21. 21Tammy V capping scalpels: Neuraminidase inhibitors (oseltamivir, zanamivir) cap NA so new virions cannot be released. They shorten illness most when started early, ideally within 48 hours of symptom onset (while replication peaks), but can still be used later in severe or hospitalized patients.⭐ Start within 48 hours; benefit is greatest early.
  22. 22Water droplets: Influenza spreads mainly by respiratory droplets (coughing, sneezing, close contact) and peaks in the colder months (December to February in the US). Presentation: sudden fever, chills, myalgias, headache, sore throat, dry cough and fatigue.⭐ Abrupt systemic symptoms distinguish flu from the common cold.
  23. 23Syringe + skeleton: The injectable influenza vaccine is killed (inactivated); skeleton = dead virus. Given yearly, it is updated each season for drift.⭐ Inactivated vaccine: safe in pregnancy and immunocompromised patients.
  24. 24Nose bubbler: The intranasal vaccine is live attenuated (cold-adapted), delivered as a nasal spray. It is avoided in immunocompromised patients, pregnancy, and very young or asthmatic children.⭐ Live attenuated vaccine is contraindicated in immunocompromised and pregnant patients.
  25. 25Lung bib: Pneumonia is a major complication of influenza: primary viral pneumonia occurs together with the flu illness (dyspnea, tachypnea, hypoxia, high fever, dry cough) and can be life-threatening (ARDS), or pneumonia can be secondary to bacterial or viral coinfection.⭐ Pregnant women, elderly and the chronically ill are at highest risk.
  26. 26Gold dental tool: Gold dental tool = 'gold' = Staphylococcus aureus (golden); the dental tool also hints at Strep pneumoniae. Secondary bacterial pneumonia after influenza (typically S. aureus, S. pneumoniae, also H. influenzae) causes fever, productive purulent cough, pleuritic chest pain, often after initial improvement from the flu.⭐ Post-influenza pneumonia: S. aureus and S. pneumoniae; the patient often improves and then worsens again.
  27. 27Aspirin bottle + skull: Aspirin must not be given to children with influenza or other viral illnesses because of the risk of Reye syndrome. Aspirin damages hepatic mitochondria (inhibits beta-oxidation), causing fatty liver, impaired urea cycle (hyperammonemia leading to cerebral edema) and hypoglycemia.⭐ No aspirin in children with viral illness (influenza, varicella).
  28. 28Sun rays: Sun rays = 'Reye's' (rays): the icon again points to Reye syndrome, the aspirin-associated mitochondrial injury in children with viral infection (decreased ATP, impaired beta-oxidation, urea cycle failure, hypoglycemia).
  29. 29Red turban: Turban around the head = encephalopathy. Reye syndrome causes vomiting, confusion, seizures and coma as ammonia rises and cerebral edema develops.
  30. 30Fat cow + liver spot: Fatty liver: Reye syndrome causes microvesicular hepatic steatosis, hepatomegaly and elevated transaminases, with potential acute liver failure (hypoglycemia, coagulopathy, coma).⭐ Reye syndrome = fatty liver + encephalopathy in a child on aspirin.
  31. 31Bear toy + grabbing leg: Influenza infection (like other respiratory and GI infections, and vaccines rarely) can trigger Guillain-Barre syndrome: ascending symmetric weakness starting in the legs, areflexia, paresthesias and sometimes respiratory failure. CSF shows albuminocytologic dissociation (high protein, normal white cell count).⭐ GBS: ascending paralysis; treat with IVIG or plasmapheresis.

56. Intestinal Nematodes Enterobius, Ancylostoma, Necator, Ascaris, Strongyloides, and Trichinella

Exam snapshot
  • Pinworm (Enterobius): nocturnal perianal itching in a child; Scotch tape test; pyrantel pamoate (treat household).
  • Hookworms (Ancylostoma, Necator): larvae penetrate bare feet, then lungs, then gut; iron deficiency anemia; eggs in stool; albendazole/pyrantel.
  • Ascaris: ingested eggs, lung migration (Loeffler), giant worm can cause intestinal obstruction; eggs in stool; albendazole.
  • Strongyloides: skin penetration plus autoinfection; larvae (not eggs) in stool; hyperinfection with steroids/HTLV-1; ivermectin.
  • Trichinella: undercooked pork or bear; periorbital edema, fever, myalgias, high CK; encysts in striated muscle; albendazole.
  • Eosinophilia is common in tissue-migrating nematodes; skin-penetrators (hookworm, Strongyloides) go blood to lungs to gut.
  1. 1Vermin lady entering wall: Enterobius vermicularis (pinworm) is an intestinal nematode (roundworm). It is the most common helminth infection in the US, especially in school-age children and daycare or household settings.
  2. 2Rocks outside hole: Gravid female worms migrate out of the anus at night to lay eggs on the perianal skin, causing intense perianal pruritus (worse at night). Pruritus leads to scratching and disrupted sleep.
  3. 3Rats eating by egg rocks: Transmission is fecal-oral: infective eggs on contaminated hands, bedding, toys, and surfaces are ingested. Scratching puts eggs under the fingernails, causing autoinfection and rapid spread within households.
  4. 4Scotch tape cape: Scotch tape test: press clear adhesive tape to the perianal skin (best done in the morning before bathing) and examine it microscopically for eggs. Stool O&P is usually negative because eggs are laid on the skin, not in the gut.⭐ Diagnose pinworm with the tape test, not a stool exam.
  5. 5"PAM!" + vermin lady: Pyrantel pamoate is the treatment for pinworm; it is a nicotinic agonist that causes depolarizing spastic paralysis of the worm, which is then expelled. Treat the whole household and repeat in about 2 weeks.
  6. 6Bendy metal bars: Albendazole is a broad-spectrum benzimidazole anthelmintic active against Enterobius, hookworms (Ancylostoma, Necator), Ascaris, Strongyloides, and Trichinella. It binds beta-tubulin and inhibits microtubule formation in the worm. It is contraindicated in the first trimester of pregnancy (teratogenic).
  7. 7America dude: Ancylostoma duodenale (Old World hookworm) and Necator americanus (New World hookworm) are the two hookworms. Both are intestinal nematodes.
  8. 8Rings around ankle: Ancylostoma duodenale is the Old World hookworm (Europe, Asia, Africa). It is an intestinal nematode with teeth-like cutting plates in its mouth.
  9. 9Rings around neck: Necator americanus is the New World hookworm (the Americas and sub-Saharan Africa). It is an intestinal nematode with cutting plates in its mouth.
  10. 10Hook: Hookworms are roundworms with a hook-like (curved) head and cutting mouthparts that attach to the small-intestinal wall and feed on blood.
  11. 11Red boots on America dude: Hookworm larvae in contaminated soil penetrate intact skin, classically of bare feet (going barefoot is the risk factor). The skin entry can cause a pruritic rash (ground itch).
  12. 12Arrow symbol on America dude: After skin penetration the larvae travel via the bloodstream to the lungs, ascend the trachea, and are swallowed at the pharynx. They mature in the small intestine, attach, suck blood, and release eggs in stool to continue the cycle.
  13. 13Iron + small pale steam holes: Chronic intestinal blood loss from attached worms causes iron deficiency anemia (microcytic, hypochromic). Think of it in a patient from an endemic area or with a barefoot-exposure history.
  14. 14Grenades in water: Diagnosis is by finding eggs in stool (O&P). Hookworm eggs are oval with a thin shell and a segmented (multi-cell) embryo inside.
  15. 15Bilobed slingshot kid + pink granules: Eosinophilia is common, especially during larval migration through the tissues. Eosinophils are bilobed with bright red-orange granules.
  16. 16"PAM!" + America dude: Pyrantel pamoate treats hookworm infection (nicotinic agonist causing spastic paralysis of the worm). Albendazole or mebendazole are alternatives, and iron is given for anemia.
  17. 17Lumbering tree man: Ascaris lumbricoides is the giant human roundworm, the largest intestinal nematode. Infection occurs by ingesting embryonated eggs from soil-contaminated hands, vegetables, or water (fecal-oral).
  18. 18Arrow symbol on lumbering tree man: Ingested eggs hatch in the small intestine, and larvae penetrate the gut wall and travel via the bloodstream to the lungs. They ascend the trachea, are swallowed at the pharynx, and mature into adults in the small intestine; eggs pass in stool.
  19. 19Respiratory leaf: During lung migration, larvae can cause cough, wheezing, and transient pulmonary infiltrates with eosinophilia (Loeffler syndrome).
  20. 20Obstructing rubble: A heavy worm burden can cause intestinal obstruction (classically at the ileocecal valve), and may also cause biliary obstruction or pancreatitis if worms migrate. Obstruction is most common in children.⭐ Large ball of worms -> small bowel obstruction in a child from an endemic area.
  21. 21Acorns in water: Diagnosis is by finding characteristic eggs in stool; fertilized eggs are round with a thick, bumpy (mammillated) shell. Adult worms may also be seen passed in stool or vomit.
  22. 22Pink granules by lumbering tree man: Eosinophilia is a common finding, especially during the tissue-migration phase. Treatment is albendazole or mebendazole (pyrantel is an alternative).
  23. 23Strong guy: Strongyloides stercoralis (threadworm) is an intestinal nematode. Unlike the other worms in this video, it can complete its life cycle within the host, which allows persistence for decades.
  24. 24Red boots on strong guy: Filariform (infective) larvae in soil penetrate intact skin, classically of the feet, just like hookworms.
  25. 25Arrow symbol on strong guy: After skin penetration, larvae go through the bloodstream to the lungs, up the trachea, and are swallowed. Adult females embed in the small-intestinal mucosa and lay eggs that hatch there, so rhabditiform larvae (not eggs) are passed in stool; some mature into filariform larvae and cause autoinfection.
  26. 26Rocks inside hole: Autoinfection: rhabditiform larvae molt into filariform larvae inside the gut, penetrate the intestinal wall or perianal skin, and re-enter the cycle. This lets infection persist for decades and, in immunosuppressed patients, causes hyperinfection and disseminated disease (including gram-negative sepsis).⭐ Corticosteroids / HTLV-1 -> Strongyloides hyperinfection; screen before starting steroids.
  27. 27Larval worms in water: Diagnosis is by finding rhabditiform larvae in stool (not eggs, in contrast to the other nematodes). Stool is often negative at first, so serology or repeated stool exams may be needed.⭐ Larvae, not eggs, in stool.
  28. 28Pink granules by strong guy: Eosinophilia is common in chronic strongyloidiasis. It may be absent in disseminated disease, a bad prognostic sign.
  29. 29"No dumping, drains to river": Ivermectin is the treatment of choice for Strongyloides; it activates glutamate-gated chloride channels, causing paralysis of the worm. Albendazole is an alternative, and treatment must also be given to carriers before immunosuppression.
  30. 30Porky trickster + spiral suit: Trichinella spiralis (pork worm) is a nematode that causes trichinosis. Its life cycle includes an intestinal phase and a muscle phase.
  31. 31Pig: Infection occurs by eating undercooked or raw meat containing encysted larvae. Classic sources are pork and wild game such as bear.
  32. 32Glasses: Larval migration causes a hypersensitivity/vasculitic reaction, producing periorbital edema (puffy eyes), often with conjunctival hemorrhages.
  33. 33Green drool: The intestinal phase (adult worms in the gut, days 1-2 after ingestion) causes nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, and abdominal pain.
  34. 34Sweat drops: The invasive phase (larvae migrating) causes fever, along with periorbital edema and myalgias.
  35. 35Red pipes: Larvae released in the gut penetrate the intestinal wall, enter the bloodstream, and are carried to skeletal (striated) muscle. Larval spread in the blood is what triggers fever and edema.
  36. 36Striated brick: Larvae preferentially invade striated muscle (e.g., extraocular muscles, diaphragm, masseters, tongue), where they encyst. Muscle biopsy shows encysted larvae.
  37. 37Explosives in brick: Larvae encyst within striated muscle cells and can calcify later. Muscle cell damage causes elevated creatine kinase (CK).
  38. 38Inflammatory fire: Larval encystment causes myositis, with muscle pain (myalgia), tenderness, and inflammation. Treatment is albendazole or mebendazole (steroids for severe symptoms).
  39. 39Pink granules by pig: Marked eosinophilia is typical. Diagnosis rests on history, eosinophilia, elevated CK, serology, and muscle biopsy.⭐ Eosinophilia + myalgia + periorbital edema after undercooked pork or bear meat = Trichinella.

57. JC Polyomavirus and BK Polyomavirus (Polyomaviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: JC virus causes PML (progressive focal deficits in HIV with low CD4 or natalizumab use); BK virus causes nephropathy in kidney transplant and hemorrhagic cystitis in HSCT.
  • Microbiology: naked, circular, double-stranded DNA viruses; ubiquitous latent infection in kidneys; reactivate with immunosuppression.
  • Diagnosis: MRI (non-enhancing white-matter lesions) and CSF JC virus PCR for PML; urine/blood BK PCR and kidney biopsy (viral inclusions, SV40 stain) for BK nephropathy.
  • Treatment: restore immunity (ART, stop immunosuppressant or natalizumab); for BK, reduce immunosuppression.
  • Buzzwords: 'PML', 'CD4 <200', 'demyelination of oligodendrocytes', 'natalizumab', 'transplant', 'hemorrhagic cystitis', 'polyoma = many tumors'.
  1. 1Cool colors: Polyomaviruses (JC virus, BK virus) are DNA viruses that are ubiquitous in the general population; clinical disease typically occurs only when the immune system is weakened.
  2. 2Circular room + double stranded DNA design: Polyomaviruses have circular, double-stranded DNA genomes.
  3. 3Statue of David: Polyomaviruses lack an envelope (naked viruses).
  4. 4Julius Caesar: JC virus (John Cunningham virus), a DNA virus in the Polyomaviridae family; the JC initials match Julius Caesar.
  5. 5Immunocompromised cane: The JC virus mainly affects immunocompromised individuals; it reactivates in HIV infection or other immunosuppression, and PML is an AIDS-defining illness in HIV-infected patients.
  6. 6AIDS ribbon: The JC virus mainly affects immunocompromised individuals; it reactivates in HIV infection or other immunosuppression, and PML is an AIDS-defining illness in HIV-infected patients.
  7. 7'Senator Count 200': JC virus is more likely to reactivate in HIV patients with a CD4+ count below 200 cells/mm3.
  8. 8'PML' bust: JC virus causes progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML), a rare, often fatal brain disease in immunocompromised patients. 'Progressive' = worsens over time, 'multifocal' = multiple brain regions, 'leukoencephalopathy' = white matter disease via demyelination.⭐ Also occurs with natalizumab, rituximab, and other immunosuppressants.
  9. 9Peeling table legs: PML is caused by JC virus destruction of oligodendrocytes, causing demyelination, disrupted conduction, and progressive deficits, often in parietal and occipital white matter, with hemiparesis, visual field defects, and cognitive impairment.
  10. 10White laurel leaves: MRI in PML shows non-enhancing, multifocal demyelinating lesions in subcortical and periventricular white matter, typically without mass effect or edema. Lesions are bright (hyperintense on T2) from demyelination and do not enhance.⭐ Contrast with CNS toxoplasmosis (ring-enhancing lesions with mass effect).
  11. 11Brutus' knife: BK virus, a DNA virus in the Polyomaviridae family (BK = Brutus, the knife-wielder).
  12. 12Flank stab wound: In immunosuppression, BK virus can reactivate and cause BK virus-associated nephropathy, with progressive renal dysfunction and rising creatinine in kidney transplant recipients.⭐ Biopsy: viral inclusions in tubular cells.
  13. 13Crotch blood: In immunocompromised patients, BK virus can cause hemorrhagic cystitis with hematuria and painful urination.⭐ Differential includes adenovirus and cyclophosphamide toxicity.
  14. 14Organs: BK nephropathy mainly occurs in kidney transplant recipients when immunosuppression allows reactivation, causing tubulointerstitial nephritis and graft failure. In hematopoietic stem cell transplant patients BK more often causes hemorrhagic cystitis.⭐ Treatment is reducing immunosuppression.

58. Klebsiella pneumoniae, Enterobacter spp., Serratia marcescens

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: hospitalized or ventilated patient with pneumonia or catheter UTI; Klebsiella in alcoholics/diabetics with lobar pneumonia and currant jelly sputum
  • Gram-negative rods, Enterobacteriaceae; Klebsiella and Enterobacter ferment lactose (Serratia is a slow/non-lactose fermenter)
  • Klebsiella: thick capsule, nonmotile, urease positive; Enterobacter and Serratia: motile; Serratia makes red prodigiosin
  • Treatment: often multidrug resistant (ESBL, carbapenemase/KPC); carbapenems, or newer agents for CRE
  • Buzzwords: currant jelly sputum, 3 A's (Aspiration, Abscess, Alcoholics/diAbetics), red pigment Serratia
  1. 1Red hues: Enterobacter spp., Serratia marcescens and Klebsiella pneumoniae are gram-negative bacilli in the Enterobacteriaceae family.
  2. 2Oxygen mask: Enterobacter, Serratia and Klebsiella can cause pneumonia (especially ventilator-associated).
  3. 3Urine bag: Enterobacter, Serratia and Klebsiella can cause urinary tract infections (especially catheter-associated).
  4. 4Hospital: Enterobacter, Serratia and Klebsiella are significant nosocomial (hospital-acquired) pathogens.
  5. 5Pills: Enterobacter, Serratia and Klebsiella commonly show multidrug resistance (ESBLs, carbapenemases).⭐ Carbapenem-resistant Enterobacterales (CRE) are a major threat.
  6. 6Pink milk carton: These organisms are classically grouped as lactose fermenters that give pink colonies on MacConkey agar; Klebsiella and Enterobacter ferment lactose readily, whereas Serratia may appear as a slow or non-lactose fermenter.⭐ Lactose fermentation = pink colonies on MacConkey; Serratia is often non-lactose fermenting.
  7. 7Pterodactyl: Enterobacter spp. are gram-negative bacilli.
  8. 8Flying pterodactyl: Enterobacter spp. are motile.
  9. 9Triceratops: Serratia marcescens is a gram-negative bacillus.
  10. 10Triceratops charging: Serratia marcescens is motile.
  11. 11Red triceratops residue: Serratia marcescens produces prodigiosin, a distinctive red pigment (red colonies, red-tinged sputum or urine).
  12. 12"Kleb" tail: Klebsiella pneumoniae is a gram-negative bacillus.
  13. 133 "A" kleb tail: Klebsiella pneumonia is notably common in alcoholics (and diabetics) and causes lung abscesses and aspiration pneumonia.⭐ The 3 A's: Aspiration pneumonia, Abscess in lungs, Alcoholics/diAbetics.
  14. 14Spike coverings: Klebsiella is surrounded by a prominent polysaccharide capsule.⭐ Capsule is the main virulence factor; mucoid colonies.
  15. 15Currant jelly: Sputum in Klebsiella pneumonia is viscous, blood-tinged and resembles currant jelly.⭐ Currant jelly sputum is classic for Klebsiella.
  16. 16Stuck in jelly: Klebsiella is nonmotile.⭐ Klebsiella is the nonmotile one among these three.
  17. 17Cavitary lesion CXR: Klebsiella pneumonia can cause pulmonary necrosis and cavitation (often upper lobes), mimicking tuberculosis.
  18. 18Ammonia bottle: Klebsiella produces urease (urease positive), which hydrolyzes urea into carbon dioxide and ammonia.⭐ Other urease-positive organisms: Proteus, H. pylori.
