59. Legionella pneumophila

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: pneumonia with high fever, GI symptoms (diarrhea), confusion/headache and hyponatremia after exposure to water systems (air conditioners, cooling towers, hotels, cruise ships)
  • Micro: gram-negative bacillus that stains poorly with Gram; facultative intracellular; catalase and oxidase positive; needs iron and cysteine
  • Diagnosis: urine antigen test (serogroup 1); culture on buffered charcoal yeast extract (BCYE) agar; silver stain; PCR
  • Treatment: azithromycin (macrolide) or a fluoroquinolone (levofloxacin, moxifloxacin); no person-to-person spread
  • Buzzwords: air-conditioning/water aerosol, smokers, hyponatremia, Legionnaires (pneumonia) vs Pontiac fever (mild flu-like)
  1. 1Legion battleship: Legion battleship = LEGIONella pneumophila, a gram-negative bacillus (rod). It is acquired from water aerosols, not person to person.⭐ Source: contaminated water systems (air conditioners, cooling towers, humidifiers, hot tubs).
  2. 2Silver paint over red: Legionella stains poorly with Gram stain (the red counterstain fails to color it well), so silver stains (Dieterle) or special media are needed to see it.⭐ Silver stain (Dieterle) shows the bacilli; direct fluorescent antibody is another option.
  3. 3Blue oxidase ring: Legionella is catalase positive and oxidase positive (the blue ring = positive oxidase test). It is an aerobic organism that needs oxygen.⭐ Legionella is a facultative intracellular gram-negative rod that lives in amoebae in water.
  4. 4Charcoal: Legionella is cultured on buffered charcoal yeast extract (BCYE) agar. Charcoal absorbs toxic substances, and the medium is enriched with iron and L-cysteine.⭐ BCYE agar with iron and cysteine is the classic culture medium.
  5. 5S.S. Cysteine + iron anchor: Legionella needs cysteine (S.S. Cysteine) and iron (the anchor, Fe) for growth, which is why both are added to BCYE agar.
  6. 6Steaming Pontiac: Pontiac fever (named for a Pontiac, Michigan outbreak) is a mild, self-limited, flu-like illness without pneumonia that resolves without treatment.⭐ Pontiac fever = mild, flu-like, no pneumonia, self-resolving.
  7. 7Patchy lower lung blueprint: Legionnaires disease is a severe atypical pneumonia: chest X-ray shows patchy lobar infiltrates that progress to consolidation, usually in mid-to-lower lung zones, and may be bilateral.⭐ Legionnaires disease: pneumonia + GI and neurologic symptoms + hyponatremia.
  8. 8Falling salt: Electrolyte disorders, especially hyponatremia (SIADH-like), hypophosphatemia and sometimes hypokalemia, are common in Legionnaires disease; liver enzymes may also rise.⭐ Hyponatremia in a pneumonia patient should make you think of Legionella.
  9. 9Clutching head: Neurologic manifestations (headache, confusion, lethargy) can occur in Legionnaires disease.
  10. 10Brown paint: Brown paint = stool: diarrhea, nausea and vomiting are common extrapulmonary symptoms of Legionnaires disease.
  11. 11Burning furnace: Patients often have high fever, frequently above 39 C (102.2 F), sometimes with relative bradycardia.⭐ High fever with relative bradycardia is a classic clue.
  12. 12Cigarette: Cigarette smoking (and chronic lung disease, older age, immunosuppression such as steroids or transplant) increases the risk of Legionnaires disease and other pulmonary infections.⭐ Risk factors: smoking, COPD, immunosuppression, older age.
  13. 13Pee stream: Diagnosis is usually by urine antigen test, which detects Legionella pneumophila serogroup 1 antigen; culture on BCYE agar and PCR are alternatives.⭐ Urine antigen only detects serogroup 1.
  14. 14Macrolide crows: Crows = macrolides: azithromycin (a macrolide) is highly effective against Legionella because it penetrates cells (the organism is intracellular).⭐ Azithromycin is a first-line drug; beta-lactams and aminoglycosides do not work.
  15. 15Flower: Flower = fluoroquinolones (levofloxacin, moxifloxacin), also effective and a first-line option for Legionella, as they penetrate intracellularly.⭐ Fluoroquinolones are another first-line option.
Show the original legend picture

60. Leishmania braziliensis & donovani (Blood and tissue protozoa)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: L. braziliensis causes skin ulcers (cutaneous/mucocutaneous); L. donovani causes fever, weight loss, huge spleen, pancytopenia after sandfly exposure.
  • Vector: Phlebotomine sandfly (promastigotes transmitted); amastigotes live inside macrophages.
  • Diagnosis: Giemsa-stained biopsy/aspirate showing amastigotes with a kinetoplast in macrophages.
  • Treatment: sodium stibogluconate (cutaneous and visceral); liposomal amphotericin B first-line for visceral disease.
  • Buzzwords: kala-azar (black fever), hepatosplenomegaly, pancytopenia, macrophages with amastigotes.
  1. 1Brazilian belt: Leishmania braziliensis is a protozoan that causes cutaneous and mucocutaneous leishmaniasis (New World).
  2. 2Undead human: Humans and other vertebrates (e.g., dogs, rodents) serve as reservoir hosts for Leishmania braziliensis.
  3. 3Zombie skin: L. braziliensis infection causes cutaneous leishmaniasis: papules that evolve into ulcerative lesions that often scar.⭐ L. braziliensis can spread to cause destructive mucocutaneous disease of the nose and mouth.
  4. 4Sandfly swarm: Phlebotomine sandflies are the vector; they inject the promastigote (flagellated) form of the parasite.⭐ Sandfly transmits promastigotes.
  5. 5Goats in cages: Inside the host, promastigotes transform into amastigotes, the non-flagellated intracellular form that replicates within macrophages.
  6. 6Many dots on spot on goats in cages: On Giemsa-stained smears, amastigotes appear within macrophages as small round to oval organisms, each with a nucleus and a rod-shaped kinetoplast.⭐ Macrophages filled with amastigotes (Leishman-Donovan bodies) are diagnostic.
  7. 7Man named Donovan: Leishmania donovani is a protozoan that causes visceral leishmaniasis (Old World).
  8. 8Dark skin spots: L. donovani causes visceral leishmaniasis, also called kala-azar ('black fever'), named for the skin hyperpigmentation seen in severe cases; post-kala-azar dermal leishmaniasis (hypopigmented lesions) may follow recovery.⭐ Kala-azar means 'black fever' in Hindi.
  9. 9Sweat: Visceral leishmaniasis presents with irregular (often double-quotidian) fever, weight loss, hepatosplenomegaly, and pancytopenia.
  10. 10Barren pan: Pancytopenia results from bone marrow involvement and splenic sequestration (empty pan = low blood cells); other features include fever, weight loss, and hepatosplenomegaly.
  11. 11Liver + spleen spots: Parasitized macrophages in the liver and spleen cause hepatosplenomegaly (massive splenomegaly in visceral disease).⭐ Hypergammaglobulinemia is also common.
  12. 12T-bone steak: Sodium stibogluconate (a pentavalent antimony) is used for both cutaneous and visceral leishmaniasis.
  13. 13Amphibians: Amphotericin B (liposomal) is a first-line treatment for leishmaniasis, particularly visceral leishmaniasis.⭐ Miltefosine is an oral alternative.
Show the original legend picture

61. Leptospira interrogans (Leptospirosis, Spirochetes)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: fever, myalgias (calf), conjunctival suffusion after freshwater exposure (floods, swimming, sewage workers, farmers).
  • Thin, tightly coiled spirochete with hooked ends (question mark); seen on dark-field, not Gram stain.
  • Reservoirs: rodents and dogs shed in urine; enters via skin cuts or mucous membranes.
  • Severe form (Weil disease): jaundice, acute kidney injury, hemorrhage; treat with penicillin or doxycycline (watch for Jarisch-Herxheimer).
  • Buzzwords: Weil disease, conjunctival suffusion, flood water, dog or rat urine.
  1. 1Spiral wave: Leptospira interrogans is a spirochete bacterium.
  2. 2Question mark + spiral leash: L. interrogans has a tight helical coil with hooked ends resembling a question mark on dark-field microscopy.⭐ Visualized by dark-field microscopy; diagnosed by serology (MAT) or PCR.
  3. 3Tropical oasis + yellow water: Leptospira is common in tropical regions and in animals such as rodents and dogs; it is excreted in urine and transmitted to humans through contact with contaminated water (e.g., swimming or flood exposure).⭐ Risk groups: sewer workers, farmers, and people exposed to floods or freshwater.
  4. 4Sweat: Fever is the typical initial symptom, often with flu-like symptoms: headache, myalgia (especially calf), and malaise.⭐ Biphasic course: septicemic phase then immune phase.
  5. 5Pink glasses: Conjunctival suffusion (redness of the conjunctiva without purulent exudate) is frequently seen early in leptospirosis.⭐ Non-purulent conjunctival suffusion is a classic clue.
  6. 6Whale: Weil disease is the severe, potentially life-threatening form of leptospirosis, with jaundice, hemorrhage, renal failure, and respiratory distress.⭐ Weil disease = jaundice plus renal failure plus bleeding.
  7. 7Disseminated red floaties: Leptospira disseminates hematogenously, causing multi-organ involvement.
  8. 8Kidney dinghy: Acute kidney injury (azotemia with elevated BUN and creatinine, electrolyte disturbances, reduced urine output, proteinuria or hematuria) is a major complication of Weil disease; liver involvement is also seen.
  9. 9Yellow wetsuit: Liver involvement presents with jaundice, elevated bilirubin, and mild to moderate transaminase elevation (usually without marked hepatocellular necrosis).⭐ Penicillin or doxycycline treats; Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction can occur.
Show the original legend picture

62. Listeria monocytogenes

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: pregnant woman with fever (to neonatal sepsis/meningitis), newborn, elderly or immunocompromised patient with meningitis after deli meats/unpasteurized dairy
  • Gram-positive rod; beta-hemolytic (narrow zone), catalase positive, tumbling motility at 25 C, grows at 4 C, facultative intracellular
  • Actin rockets (ActA) allow cell-to-cell spread; listeriolysin O escapes phagosome
  • Dx: CSF/blood culture; Treatment: ampicillin (plus gentamicin in severe disease); cephalosporins are NOT active
  • Buzzwords: cold enrichment, tumbling motility, actin rocket tails, unpasteurized soft cheese
  1. 1Santa's list: Listeria monocytogenes is a gram-positive bacillus.
  2. 2Purple rod-shaped ornaments: Listeria monocytogenes is a gram-positive rod.
  3. 3Beta light bulb: Listeria is beta-hemolytic, but often produces only a narrow zone of hemolysis on blood agar.
  4. 4Tumbling ornaments: In broth culture, Listeria shows characteristic tumbling motility (best at room temperature, about 25 C).⭐ Motile at 25 C, poorly at 37 C.
  5. 5Rocket: Listeria forms actin "rocket tails" (via ActA) in host cells, driving intracellular movement and cell-to-cell spread.⭐ Listeriolysin O lets it escape the phagolysosome.
  6. 6Rocket in bag: Listeria is a facultative intracellular pathogen, able to live both inside and outside host cells.⭐ Cell-mediated immunity (T cells) is needed for control, hence risk in pregnancy, elderly and immunocompromised.
  7. 7Catalase cat: Listeria produces catalase (catalase positive), which converts hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen.
  8. 8Icicles: Listeria can survive and multiply at refrigerator temperatures (cold enrichment), contributing to spread in refrigerated foods.
  9. 9Milk: Listeriosis outbreaks are frequently associated with dairy products such as unpasteurized milk, soft cheeses and ice cream.
  10. 10Soft cheese: Listeriosis outbreaks are frequently associated with soft cheeses (and also deli meats and unpasteurized dairy).⭐ Also deli meats and hot dogs.
  11. 11Pregnant woman: Pregnant women are much more susceptible to listeriosis than the general population because of immunologic changes in pregnancy; infection can cause fever, miscarriage, stillbirth or neonatal infection.⭐ Can cross the placenta (transplacental) and cause granulomatosis infantiseptica.
  12. 12Baby in meningitis helmet: Listeria monocytogenes is a significant cause of neonatal meningitis (also early-onset sepsis).⭐ Neonatal meningitis: Group B Strep, E. coli, Listeria.
  13. 13Old Santa Claus: Listeria is a significant cause of meningitis in adults over 60 and in immunocompromised patients, owing to impaired cell-mediated immunity.⭐ Also a cause in patients with impaired T-cell immunity (transplant, steroids).
  14. 14Amp: Ampicillin is the primary antibiotic for Listeria infections (gentamicin is added in severe disease).⭐ Cephalosporins are not active against Listeria, so add ampicillin to empiric meningitis regimens in at-risk patients.
Show the original legend picture

63. Lymphocytic Choriomeningitis Virus (LCMV) (Arenaviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: often asymptomatic; flu-like illness that can progress to aseptic meningitis; congenital infection causes hydrocephalus and chorioretinitis; exposure to house mice or pet hamsters.
  • Micro: enveloped, helical, bisegmented (2 segments) ambisense ssRNA virus; sandy granules on EM from host ribosomes.
  • Diagnosis: serology (IgM/IgG) and RT-PCR; CSF lymphocytic pleocytosis.
  • Treatment/prevention: supportive care; avoid rodent contact and excreta; pregnant women should avoid exposure.
  • Buzzwords: rodent-borne, house mouse, ambisense, sandy appearance, aseptic meningitis, congenital chorioretinitis.
  1. 1Arena: Arenaviruses (e.g. LCMV) are RNA viruses.
  2. 2Warm colors: Arenaviruses are RNA viruses (warm colors = RNA virus).
  3. 3Moon with negative sign-shaped clouds: Arenaviruses are classified as negative-sense RNA viruses, although they also contain regions read in the positive sense (ambisense).
  4. 4Enveloping toga: Arenaviruses are enveloped viruses.
  5. 5Ambidextrous: Arenaviruses are ambisense: though classified as negative-sense, their segments contain both positive- and negative-sense regions.⭐ Ambisense genome is the distinguishing feature of arenaviruses.
  6. 6Column spirals: Arenavirus nucleocapsids are helical (elongated, coiled).
  7. 72 circles: Arenaviruses have a segmented genome of 2 RNA segments (L and S), which are circular.
  8. 8Sandy arena: Arenaviruses look granular ("sandy") on electron microscopy because they incorporate host ribosomes into virions during budding; the ribosomes are nonfunctional.⭐ "Sandy" granules = host ribosomes.
  9. 9Rodents: Rodents are the primary reservoir (rodent-borne viruses, roboviruses); humans are infected through contact with or inhalation of rodent urine, feces or saliva, or, for LCMV, transplacentally from mother to fetus.⭐ LCMV reservoir is the common house mouse (also pet hamsters).
  10. 10"LCV": Lymphocytic choriomeningitis virus (LCMV) is an ambisense RNA virus in the Arenaviridae family.
  11. 11Sweat: LCMV infection is often asymptomatic; when symptomatic it causes systemic symptoms (fever, anorexia, myalgias, headache, nausea, vomiting) and can progress to aseptic meningitis or meningoencephalitis (fever, headache, stiff neck, photophobia).⭐ Aseptic meningitis with lymphocytic CSF.
  12. 12Red turban covering head + encircling neck: Same: neurologic involvement (meningitis with headache, stiff neck, photophobia). Congenital infection can lead to hydrocephalus, chorioretinitis and developmental delay.⭐ Congenital LCMV mimics TORCH infections.
  13. 13Elevated flame: LCMV can be inactivated by heat (denatures proteins) and by detergents or lipid solvents (dissolve the envelope).⭐ Enveloped viruses are easily inactivated by soap and solvents.
  14. 14Radiating light: LCMV can also be inactivated by UV light (damages nucleic acids) and chemical disinfectants (destroy viral components).
Show the original legend picture

64. Malassezia furfur

Exam snapshot
  • Pityriasis (tinea) versicolor: well-demarcated, finely scaling hypo- or hyperpigmented patches on the chest and back, usually non-itchy, in hot humid conditions.
  • Lipophilic yeast (normal skin flora); infection limited to the stratum corneum; lipases produce azelaic acid that inhibits melanocytes.
  • KOH prep shows 'spaghetti and meatballs' (short hyphae plus yeast clusters); fluoresces yellow-green under Wood's lamp.
  • Topical selenium sulfide (Selsun Blue), or topical azoles; oral fluconazole or itraconazole for extensive disease.
  • Spaghetti and meatballs, TPN lipids fungemia in neonates, seborrheic dermatitis, hot and humid conditions.
  1. 1"Ristorante Italiano di Malassezia": Malassezia furfur is a fungus that causes pityriasis (tinea) versicolor and seborrheic dermatitis.
  2. 2Fur coat: M. furfur: the fur in the name; a fungus causing pityriasis versicolor and seborrheic dermatitis.
  3. 3"Versi di calore sauce": Versi = versicolor: the sauce on the chest and back represents pityriasis versicolor, caused by M. furfur (calore = heat, a clue to hot humid climates).
  4. 4KOH salt: On KOH preparation of skin scrapings, M. furfur shows short hyphae and round yeast clusters.
  5. 5Spaghetti and meatballs: The KOH finding is classically described as "spaghetti and meatballs": short hyphae (spaghetti) and round yeast (meatballs).⭐ Spaghetti and meatballs = Malassezia.
  6. 6Heat lamp: Normally a harmless commensal yeast, M. furfur can convert to its pathogenic mycelial form in hot, humid conditions, which promote filamentous growth.
  7. 7Sweating chef: Same point: heat, humidity, and sweating favor the transition to the pathogenic hyphal form.
  8. 8Sauce on chest and back: Pityriasis versicolor presents as oval, well-demarcated patches or plaques with fine scale on the chest and back; they may be hypopigmented, hyperpigmented, or erythematous, can merge, and are usually not itchy.⭐ Hypopigmented patches do not tan; fluoresce under Wood's lamp.
  9. 9Olive oil: M. furfur is lipophilic, thriving on lipid-rich sebum; its lipases break down lipids into fatty acids (e.g. azelaic acid), which damage melanocytes and cause the pigment changes.
  10. 10Broken olive oil bottle: Same point: lipase-mediated lipid breakdown yields acids that injure melanocytes, causing pigment change.
  11. 11Corn on lasagna: M. furfur infection is superficial, confined to the stratum corneum of the epidermis, so there is little inflammation.
  12. 12Baby slurping noodle: M. furfur fungemia is a rare complication in neonates receiving total parenteral nutrition (TPN) through central venous catheters. Lipid-rich TPN supports yeast growth and catheter colonization, leading to bloodstream infection, sepsis, and thrombocytopenia.⭐ Lipid emulsions in TPN are the risk factor.
  13. 13Blue sun: Pityriasis versicolor is treated with topical selenium sulfide (Selsun Blue), which promotes shedding of the stratum corneum where the fungus lives.⭐ Also ketoconazole shampoo.
Show the original legend picture

65. Measles, Mumps, Parainfluenza and RSV

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: unvaccinated child with 3 C's (cough, coryza, conjunctivitis), fever, Koplik spots then cephalocaudal rash (measles); parotitis and orchitis (mumps); barking cough (croup); wheezing infant (RSV).
  • Micro: paramyxoviruses (measles, mumps, parainfluenza) and pneumoviruses (RSV) are enveloped, helical, negative-sense ssRNA viruses with F protein causing syncytia.
  • Diagnosis: mainly clinical; serology or PCR; measles IgM; steeple sign on neck X-ray for croup; RSV antigen or PCR.
  • Treatment: supportive; vitamin A for measles; dexamethasone and racemic epinephrine for croup; ribavirin only in severe RSV in immunocompromised; palivizumab prophylaxis; MMR live vaccine.
  • Buzzwords: Koplik spots, Warthin-Finkeldey cells, SSPE, parotitis, orchitis, steeple sign, palivizumab, bronchiolitis.
  1. 1PARAnormal MiXer: The Paramyxoviridae family includes measles, mumps and parainfluenza viruses (PARAmyxo = 'paranormal'; MiXer = Mumps, Measles = 'MMR' plus parainfluenza).
  2. 2Warm colors: Paramyxoviruses are negative-sense single-stranded RNA viruses; the RNA genome must be transcribed by a viral RNA-dependent RNA polymerase carried in the virion.
  3. 3Moon with negative-sign-shaped clouds: Paramyxoviruses have a negative-sense RNA genome (second reminder using the 'negative sign' clouds).⭐ Negative-sense viruses carry their own RdRp.
  4. 4Ghosts with envelopes: Paramyxoviruses are enveloped (the ghost sheet) and have a helical nucleocapsid.
  5. 5Water droplets: Paramyxoviruses spread by respiratory droplets (measles is airborne and highly contagious).
  6. 6Live puppet show: The MMR vaccine is a live attenuated vaccine given to prevent measles, mumps and rubella (first dose at 12-15 months).⭐ MMR is live, so avoid in pregnancy and severe immunodeficiency.
  7. 7Pregnant puppet: Live vaccines such as MMR should not be given to pregnant people or to people with significant immunocompromise (e.g., severe T-cell deficiency).
  8. 8Weasels + ruby dress: Measles is caused by the rubeola virus, hence it is also called rubeola ('weasel' = measles); not to be confused with rubella (German measles).
  9. 94 'C' buttons: The 4 C's of measles: Cough, Coryza, Conjunctivitis and Koplik spots (plus high fever).
  10. 10Coughing weasel: Measles produces a dry, hacking, nonproductive cough that may be paroxysmal.
  11. 11Runny nose: Coryza is nasal mucosal inflammation causing congestion, watery or clear rhinorrhea and sneezing.
  12. 12Red eyes: Measles causes conjunctivitis with red, watery eyes and photophobia, typically without purulent discharge.
  13. 13Blue candies in pink bowl: Koplik spots are small blue-white or gray-white spots on a red base on the buccal mucosa, opposite the molars. They appear 1-2 days before the rash during the prodrome and are pathognomonic for measles.⭐ Koplik spots = measles (grains of salt on a red background).
  14. 14Sweating weasel: Measles begins with a high abrupt-onset fever, often above 40 C (104 F), lasting several days with chills, malaise and fatigue, peaking when the rash appears.
  15. 15Ruby crown + falling rubies: The maculopapular rash starts on the face (hairline and behind the ears) and spreads downward to the neck, trunk and extremities over 2-3 days.
  16. 16Solid red dress: The red, blotchy, slightly raised spots coalesce (merge) into larger confluent areas of redness as the rash spreads; it appears as the prodromal symptoms fade.⭐ Rash spreads head to toe (cephalocaudal).
  17. 17Lung-shaped bow tie: Pneumonia (viral or secondary bacterial) is the most common cause of measles-related death in children; other complications are otitis media, encephalitis and diarrhea.⭐ Giant-cell (Hecht) pneumonia can occur in the immunocompromised.
  18. 18Tales of SSPEnce: Subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE) is a rare, fatal late complication of measles that appears about 7-10 years after infection, with behavioral and cognitive decline, myoclonic jerks, seizures and eventually coma. CSF shows elevated measles antibodies.⭐ SSPE: years after measles; progressive and fatal.
  19. 19Grey and white turban: The brain imaging in SSPE shows progressive white matter demyelination and cortical atrophy (the grey and white turban depicts the brain white and gray matter).
  20. 20Tentacles and berries near weasel: The measles virus has the surface glycoprotein hemagglutinin (H), which binds host cell receptors (SLAM and nectin-4) and hemagglutinates red blood cells; measles lacks neuraminidase.
  21. 21Sticky substance on weasel hands: The measles F (fusion) protein causes fusion of infected cells to form syncytia, producing multinucleated Warthin-Finkeldey giant cells in lymphoid tissue (tonsils, lymph nodes, appendix), often with eosinophilic intranuclear and cytoplasmic inclusions. These are pathognomonic for measles.⭐ Warthin-Finkeldey cells = measles.
  22. 22'A' party hat on weasel: Vitamin A reduces morbidity and mortality from measles (especially in children) by supporting epithelial integrity and immune responses; give to children with severe or complicated measles.
  23. 23Mummy guest: The mumps virus (mummy) is a paramyxovirus that causes mumps: parotid swelling, fever and malaise, with possible complications of orchitis, meningitis, pancreatitis and sensorineural hearing loss.
  24. 24Swollen cheeks: Mumps replicates in the parotid glands causing painful bilateral or unilateral parotitis with swollen puffy cheeks, pain on chewing or swallowing, fever, malaise and sometimes headache.⭐ Parotitis + orchitis + aseptic meningitis.
  25. 25Orchid: Mumps can cause orchitis (painful, usually unilateral testicular swelling, in postpubertal males), which can lead to testicular atrophy and rarely infertility (oophoritis can occur in females).
  26. 26Neckbrace: Mumps is a cause of viral (aseptic) meningitis, with headache, fever, neck stiffness and photophobia, usually self-limited.
  27. 27Mummy child headed towards puppet show: The MMR live attenuated vaccine includes the mumps virus; two doses give protection and prevent outbreaks.
  28. 28Tentacles and berries on tray held by mummy: Mumps virus has the HN surface glycoprotein, with hemagglutinin activity that attaches to host cell receptors (sialic acid) in addition to neuraminidase activity.
  29. 29Sticky substance on mummy hands: The mumps F (fusion) protein forms syncytia (multinucleated giant cells) in the salivary glands, pancreas, testes and CNS. In measles, Warthin-Finkeldey cells form mainly in lymphoid tissues.
  30. 30Scalpel on tray held by mummy: Mumps (and parainfluenza) virus has neuraminidase (NA), which cleaves sialic acid residues, helping release virions from the host cell. Measles has no neuraminidase.
  31. 31Baying seal howling at moon: Parainfluenza virus causes croup (laryngotracheobronchitis): a barking seal-like cough, inspiratory stridor and hoarseness that are worse at night, usually in children aged 6 months to 3 years.⭐ Croup: parainfluenza 1 and 2; treat with dexamethasone.
  32. 323 wolves: Parainfluenza has hemagglutinin-neuraminidase (HN, for attachment and release) and the F protein (fusion and cell-to-cell spread); there are multiple serotypes (the 3 wolves hint at the main virulence factors HA, NA and F).
  33. 33Steeple: The neck radiograph in croup shows the steeple sign, a narrowing of the subglottic trachea.
  34. 34Wind blowing door in: Croup causes inspiratory stridor, a high-pitched sound heard on inhalation, due to obstruction at the subglottic airway.⭐ Treat with dexamethasone; add racemic epinephrine if severe.
  35. 35RSV tombstone: Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) belongs to the Pneumoviridae family (genus Orthopneumovirus), along with human metapneumovirus; it was previously classified in Paramyxoviridae. Tombstone = RSV is a leading cause of death in infants.
  36. 36Warm colors: Pneumoviridae are negative-sense single-stranded RNA viruses.
  37. 37Moon with negative sign-shaped clouds: Pneumoviridae are negative-sense RNA viruses (second reminder).
  38. 38More ghosts with envelopes: Pneumoviridae are enveloped and have a helical nucleocapsid.
  39. 39Ghost baby holding 'G' letter: Pneumoviridae use the G (attachment) glycoprotein to bind respiratory epithelial cells; G is a target for neutralizing antibodies.
  40. 40Sticky hands: RSV F protein mediates fusion of the envelope with the host membrane and fusion of infected cells into syncytia (the 'syncytial' in RSV). F protein is the target of neutralizing antibodies, including the monoclonal antibody palivizumab.⭐ F protein = target of palivizumab.
  41. 41More water droplets: RSV is spread by direct contact and large respiratory droplets (and fomites), and outbreaks occur in winter.
  42. 42Bronchial tree patterns + infiltrates on baby ghosts: RSV is the most common cause of bronchiolitis and a major cause of pneumonia in infants. It infects bronchiolar epithelium, causing necrosis, mucus plugging and airway obstruction; chest X-ray shows hyperinflation and peribronchial infiltrates rather than lobar consolidation.⭐ Bronchiolitis: wheezing, tachypnea; supportive care.
  43. 43Baby ghosts: RSV mostly affects infants younger than 1 year, peaking at 2-6 months; prematurity is a risk factor for severe disease.
  44. 44Ribs: Ribavirin, a nucleoside analog, can be used for severe RSV in immunocompromised patients; it is teratogenic, so it is contraindicated in pregnancy (also not routine).⭐ Ribavirin = teratogenic.
  45. 45Pale ghost + antibody rattle: Palivizumab is a monoclonal IgG1 antibody against the RSV F protein; it neutralizes the virus and prevents entry into cells. It is used as prophylaxis in high-risk infants (premature, chronic lung disease, hemodynamically significant congenital heart disease).
Show the original legend picture

66. Moraxella catarrhalis

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: otitis media or sinusitis in a child, or COPD exacerbation/bronchopneumonia in an older smoker
  • Micro: gram-negative diplococcus, oxidase positive, aerobic, beta-lactamase producer; biofilm formation; colonizes the nasopharynx
  • Diagnosis: clinical; culture with positive 'hockey puck' sign (colonies slide intact across agar when pushed with a loop)
  • Treatment: amoxicillin-clavulanate, second/third-generation cephalosporins, TMP-SMX, macrolides, or fluoroquinolones (penicillin/ampicillin alone fails because of beta-lactamase)
  • Buzzwords: 'diplococci that cause COPD flares', hockey puck, nonencapsulated bacterium
  1. 1Moray eel-cat: Moray eel + cat = MORAXella CATarrhalis, a gram-negative diplococcus (coffee-bean shaped pairs, like Neisseria).
  2. 2Glow of red flames: Red flames in pairs = Gram-negative (pink/red) diplococci, the Gram stain appearance of M. catarrhalis.⭐ Gram-negative diplococci: Neisseria and Moraxella.
  3. 3Nose of plane: M. catarrhalis colonizes the nasopharynx (the plane's nose) of many children and adults, which leads to upper respiratory infections.
  4. 4Blue gem ring: Blue gem = oxidase test positive (blue/purple color); Moraxella is an oxidase positive aerobe.⭐ Oxidase positive, like Neisseria.
  5. 5Bellows-fish: Bellows = breathing air: M. catarrhalis is an obligate aerobe.
  6. 6Algae: Algae represents biofilm formation, which helps the bacteria persist on the mucosa and bolsters resistance to certain antibiotics.
  7. 7Broken beta-lactam gyroscope: M. catarrhalis produces beta-lactamase (nearly all strains), an enzyme that breaks the beta-lactam ring and inactivates penicillins and ampicillin.⭐ Nearly 100 percent of isolates are penicillin-resistant by beta-lactamase.
  8. 8Sliding plane wheel: Hockey puck sign: when colonies are pushed with a loop, they slide across the agar as intact discs like a hockey puck.⭐ Positive hockey puck sign is characteristic of Moraxella on agar.
  9. 9Amelia Ear-hurt: Amelia Earhart = ear hurt: M. catarrhalis is a common cause of otitis media in children (along with Strep pneumoniae and nontypeable H. influenzae), particularly in colonized children.⭐ Big three causes of otitis media: S. pneumoniae, nontypeable H. influenzae, M. catarrhalis.
  10. 10Red leaky plane nose: A red, leaking nose = sinusitis: M. catarrhalis is a common cause of bacterial sinusitis.
  11. 11Blue bloater fish: Blue bloater = chronic bronchitis (COPD): M. catarrhalis is associated with COPD exacerbations and COPD-related bronchopneumonia.⭐ Cause of COPD exacerbation: H. influenzae, S. pneumoniae, M. catarrhalis.
  12. 12Cephalopod: Cephalopod = cephalosporins: M. catarrhalis is sensitive to second- and third-generation cephalosporins.
  13. 13Box of "AMMO" + clarinet harpoon: AMMO = amoxicillin-clavulanate (the clavulanate inhibits beta-lactamase); the clarinet is clarithromycin (a macrolide). Both are active against Moraxella.⭐ Amoxicillin-clavulanate overcomes beta-lactamase.
  14. 14Rotten sulfa-eggs: Rotten eggs smell of sulfur: sulfa drug, trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX), is active against M. catarrhalis.
  15. 15Flower anemones: Flower = fluoroquinolones: also effective against M. catarrhalis (generally reserved for adults).
Show the original legend picture

67. Mucor spp. and Rhizopus spp. (Mucormycosis)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: diabetic ketoacidosis (or neutropenia/transplant) with facial pain, black necrotic nasal eschar, proptosis and cranial nerve deficits
  • Micro: Mucorales molds with broad, ribbon-like, nonseptate (aseptate) hyphae branching at wide (about 90 degree) angles; angioinvasive
  • Diagnosis: biopsy with KOH or silver stain/histology showing hyphae in tissue; imaging (CT/MRI) of sinuses and brain
  • Treatment: urgent surgical debridement plus liposomal amphotericin B; control diabetes and acidosis; isavuconazole or posaconazole as alternatives
  • Buzzwords: 'rhinocerebral', black eschar, DKA, nonseptate hyphae with right-angle branching, bread mold
  1. 1"Mu Car Auto Shop - Rides'R'Us": The auto shop = MUcor (Mu car) and RHIZOpus (Rides'R'Us): opportunistic fungi (Mucorales) that cause mucormycosis in susceptible hosts.
  2. 2Immunocompromised cane: Mucormycosis mainly affects immunocompromised or metabolically compromised patients, such as those with uncontrolled diabetes (especially diabetic ketoacidosis), neutropenia, transplant or high-dose steroids.⭐ DKA is the classic risk factor.
  3. 3Candy: Candy = high glucose: uncontrolled diabetes mellitus with hyperglycemia and ketoacidosis predisposes to mucormycosis (hyperglycemia impairs neutrophil and macrophage function).
  4. 4Bread: Some Rhizopus species are common bread molds (also Mucor grows on decaying food), which shows they are widespread environmental organisms.⭐ Rhizopus = bread mold.
  5. 5Mechanic coughing from fumes: Infection occurs by inhaling fungal spores from the environment (the mechanic breathing fumes), seeding the sinuses or lungs; it is not spread person to person.
  6. 6"Ketone auto parts": Ketone parts: in ketoacidosis, high glucose impairs neutrophil function, and acidosis reduces transferrin binding of iron, raising free serum iron that Rhizopus uses to grow rapidly and invade tissue.⭐ Acidosis frees iron; deferoxamine (an iron chelator) worsens mucormycosis because Rhizopus uses the siderophore.
  7. 7Right angle lug wrenches: The hyphae are broad, ribbon-like and nonseptate (aseptate) and branch at right (about 90 degree) angles; compare with Aspergillus, which has septate hyphae with acute-angle (45 degree) branching.⭐ Mucor: broad nonseptate, right angle branches. Aspergillus: septate, acute angle.
  8. 8Red jumper cables: Red cables = blood vessels: Mucorales invade vessel walls (angioinvasion), causing thrombosis, tissue infarction and necrosis of surrounding tissue.⭐ Angioinvasion causes black necrotic tissue.
  9. 9Holes in oil plate: Rhinocerebral mucormycosis begins in the nasal sinuses (holes in the plate), where spores germinate. The fungus can erode through the cribriform plate (thin bone between sinus and anterior cranial fossa) into the brain.⭐ Spread from sinuses to orbit and brain.
  10. 10Oil on face: Face oiled in black: black necrotic eschar on the nasal turbinates or palate is a hallmark of rhinocerebral mucormycosis. Other features: facial pain or swelling, bloody nasal discharge, fever, proptosis, ophthalmoplegia, vision loss, cranial nerve deficits, headache, and altered mental status or death in severe cases.⭐ Black eschar on palate or nose in a ketoacidotic patient = mucormycosis.
  11. 11Amphibian car + "FROGLVR?" license plate: Frog (amphibian) = AMPHOtericin B (liposomal), the drug of choice; plus surgical debridement of necrotic tissue. Control of the underlying diabetes/acidosis is also vital.⭐ Treatment: debridement + amphotericin B (liposomal).
Show the original legend picture

68. Mycobacterium avium-intracellulare, M. scrofulaceum, M. marinum

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: MAC - chronic cough in COPD/bronchiectasis or disseminated fever, diarrhea, cytopenias in HIV with CD4 below 50; scrofulaceum - painless unilateral neck node in a child; marinum - nodules on a hand after fish tank exposure
  • Micro: nontuberculous mycobacteria, acid-fast bacilli with mycolic acid cell wall; MAC is intrinsically multidrug resistant
  • Diagnosis: acid-fast stain and culture of blood, sputum, marrow or tissue; granulomas on biopsy
  • Treatment/prevention: MAC - macrolide + ethambutol +/- rifabutin; azithromycin prophylaxis for HIV with CD4 below 50 (when ART not started); marinum - clarithromycin, doxycycline, TMP-SMX
  • Buzzwords: 'MAC = bird (avium)', fish tank granuloma, scrofula, not transmitted person to person
  1. 1Quick draw fuschia gun and holster: Quick draw fuchsin gun = carbol fuchsin: the Ziehl-Neelsen (or Kinyoun) stain is used to detect acid-fast bacilli such as Mycobacterium avium complex.
  2. 2Tassels: Tassels = mycolic acid. Mycobacteria are acid-fast because their cell walls contain large amounts of mycolic acid, which holds the carbol fuchsin dye despite acid-alcohol decolorization.
  3. 3Tower of macrocages: Macrophage 'cages': mycobacteria invade and survive inside macrophages and induce granuloma formation, which helps them persist and evade clearance.
  4. 4Bird inside cage: Bird = M. AVIUM (avium means bird). MAC (M. avium and M. intracellulare) is acquired from the environment (water, soil, dust) by inhalation or ingestion; no person-to-person spread.⭐ MAC is acquired from the environment, not from other people.
  5. 5Lung vest: MAC lung disease affects patients with chronic lung disease (COPD, bronchiectasis, cystic fibrosis), causing chronic progressive cough, hemoptysis, fatigue, night sweats and weight loss that resemble TB.⭐ Look for MAC pulmonary disease in older women with bronchiectasis (Lady Windermere syndrome).
  6. 6Pink puffer prisoner: The pink puffer (emphysema/COPD) confined behind bars shows that underlying COPD predisposes to MAC pulmonary disease; symptoms mimic TB (productive cough, hemoptysis, night sweats, weight loss).
  7. 7Body covered in burs: Disseminated MAC (burs all over the body) occurs in severely immunocompromised patients, especially HIV with CD4 below 50: fever, night sweats, diarrhea, weight loss, hepatosplenomegaly, lymphadenopathy and cytopenias (anemia or pancytopenia).⭐ Disseminated MAC: AIDS with CD4 <50 cells/uL.
  8. 8Crutches: Crutches show the debilitated, wasted state of the patient with disseminated MAC (same fact: fever, night sweats, diarrhea, weight loss, hepatosplenomegaly, cytopenias, with CD4 below 50).check original
  9. 9Ring of pill bottles: MAC is intrinsically resistant to many antibiotics (multidrug resistant), so treatment needs combination therapy for long periods.⭐ Never treat MAC with a single drug.
  10. 10Crow: Crow = macrolide (clarithromycin or azithromycin), the backbone of MAC treatment.
  11. 11"Ethel": Ethel = ethambutol, the second drug in MAC therapy, combined with a macrolide.⭐ Ethambutol side effect: optic neuritis (red-green color blindness).
  12. 12Rifle: Rifle = rifabutin (a rifamycin), considered as a third agent for MAC, especially in severe disease; it has fewer interactions with antiretrovirals than rifampin.
  13. 13Crow sitting <50 mph sign: Prophylaxis: HIV patients with CD4 below 50 who are not on antiretroviral therapy should receive a macrolide (azithromycin) to prevent MAC, and ART should be started. Current guidelines advise prophylaxis only if ART cannot be started immediately.⭐ Azithromycin prophylaxis for CD4 <50 (not needed if ART is started promptly).
  14. 14Puppy's scruff: M. scrofulaceum causes scrofula: 'scruff' of the neck. It typically causes painless, slowly enlarging unilateral cervical lymphadenitis in children. The route is unclear but thought to be environmental exposure (soil, water, animals).⭐ Scrofula = cervical tuberculous-type lymphadenitis (M. scrofulaceum, MAC, or M. tuberculosis).
  15. 15Smiling kid lifting up one side of the puppy's scruff: The child and the neck-scruff show unilateral neck swelling in a child (cervical lymphadenitis, a painless slowly growing mass); a surgical excision is often curative.
  16. 16Horse trough: Mare (horse) trough = M. MARinum, the organism of the next icons (a marine/aquatic mycobacterium). It grows best at cooler temperatures (about 30-32 C), so it infects skin.⭐ M. marinum grows best at 30-32 C, which explains why it affects cool skin of the hands.
  17. 17Dirty water + fish: M. marinum is acquired through contact with contaminated water or infected aquatic animals (fish tanks, aquariums, swimming pools, fish, amphibians, reptiles), usually through a break in the skin.
  18. 18Fish biting hand: M. marinum causes localized skin infection, 'fish tank granuloma' or 'swimming pool granuloma': nodules or ulcers, often spreading up the hand and forearm in a sporotrichoid pattern. Treat with clarithromycin, doxycycline or TMP-SMX.⭐ Hand nodules after cleaning a fish tank = M. marinum.
Show the original legend picture

69. Mycobacterium leprae

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: hypopigmented anesthetic skin patches (tuberculoid) or nodules, leonine facies, thickened nerves and glove-and-stocking neuropathy (lepromatous); armadillo contact in the southern US
  • Micro: acid-fast bacillus with mycolic acid wall; obligate intracellular, cannot be cultured on artificial media; grows best at cool temperatures (skin, extremities)
  • Diagnosis: skin or nerve biopsy with acid-fast stain; lepromin skin test helps classify (positive in tuberculoid)
  • Treatment: tuberculoid (paucibacillary) - dapsone + rifampin; lepromatous (multibacillary) - dapsone + rifampin + clofazimine for longer
  • Buzzwords: Hansen disease, armadillo, Th1 = tuberculoid (strong), Th2 = lepromatous (weak), leonine facies
  1. 1Leaping armadillo: Leaping armadillo = M. leprae ('leaping'), an acid-fast bacillus that is an obligate intracellular organism and can't be grown on standard culture media.⭐ M. leprae cannot be grown on artificial media; it is grown in armadillo footpads.
  2. 2Armadillo County Jail: Armadillos are the only known natural nonhuman reservoir of M. leprae; their low body temperature favors the bacterium, and armadillos can transmit it to humans (e.g., in the southern US).⭐ Armadillo exposure in Texas or Louisiana is a classic clue.
  3. 3'Hansen' the armadillo: M. leprae causes leprosy, also called Hansen disease (named for Gerhard Armauer Hansen).
  4. 4Icicles: M. leprae prefers cooler temperatures (about 30 C), so it infects the skin, peripheral nerves and cooler extremities (earlobes, nose, hands, feet) rather than the warm core.⭐ Cool-temperature preference explains skin and extremity predilection.
  5. 5Mycolic acid tassels: M. leprae is acid-fast because of the high mycolic acid content in its cell wall.
  6. 6Quick draw fuschia gun and holster: Quick draw fuchsin = carbol fuchsin: the Ziehl-Neelsen method uses carbol fuchsin to stain acid-fast bacilli such as M. leprae red; Fite stain (modified, less acid decolorization) is often used on tissue.
  7. 7Jail cell 1: Cell 1 = tuberculoid leprosy, the contained form. A strong Th1 cell-mediated response (IFN-gamma activating macrophages) contains the bacteria.⭐ Tuberculoid = Th1 response = few bacteria = 'paucibacillary'.
  8. 8Prisoner in jail cell 1: The bacterium is locked up: strong Th1 immunity (IFN-gamma) makes macrophages phagocytose and kill M. leprae, resulting in tuberculoid leprosy with well-formed granulomas.
  9. 9Bald prisoner: Tuberculoid leprosy has few (one to a few) skin lesions that are flat or slightly raised, hypopigmented, hairless and anesthetic, with asymmetric nerve involvement.⭐ Few, well-demarcated, anesthetic plaques.
  10. 10Shovel in dirt mound: The lepromin skin test (shovel digging a mound = intradermal injection of inactivated M. leprae) helps classify disease in someone already diagnosed, not diagnose it. Positive (nodule forms) = strong cellular immunity (tuberculoid). Negative = lepromatous (weak cell-mediated response, widespread lesions, high bacterial load).⭐ Lepromin test: positive in tuberculoid, negative in lepromatous.
  11. 11Jail cell 2: Cell 2 = lepromatous leprosy: weak Th1 response with a dominant Th2 humoral response that fails to contain the bacteria, leading to widespread disease.⭐ Lepromatous = Th2 response = many bacteria (multibacillary).
  12. 12Laughing escapee from cell 2: The prisoner escapes: the Th2 humoral response (antibodies, IL-4/IL-10) is not effective against intracellular bacteria, and the weak or absent Th1 response means macrophages cannot contain M. leprae, so bacilli multiply within macrophages (foamy macrophages with globi).
  13. 13Touching hands: Person-to-person spread occurs mainly from untreated lepromatous patients, who carry many bacilli, by respiratory droplets/nasal secretions after prolonged close contact over many months.⭐ Low infectivity; most people are naturally immune.
  14. 14Gloves and socks: Lepromatous leprosy has symmetric glove-and-stocking sensory neuropathy with loss of temperature and pain sensation in the extremities (leading to injuries, ulcers and deformities); thickened peripheral nerves can be felt.⭐ Loss of sensation in cool areas, with thickened nerves.
  15. 15Long John lesions: Lepromatous lesions favor cooler body areas: extensor surfaces of the limbs, face (earlobes, nose) and buttocks. They are symmetric, poorly demarcated and can merge into large plaques or nodules (the 'long johns' show the covered distribution).
  16. 16Lion face bandana: Leonine facies: skin thickening and nodules on the face, loss of eyebrows and eyelashes (madarosis), nasal collapse and nodular earlobes in lepromatous leprosy.⭐ Leonine facies = lepromatous leprosy.
  17. 17Deputy next to Jail cell 1: Deputy = DAPsone ('Dep-uty'). Tuberculoid (paucibacillary) leprosy is treated with dapsone plus rifampin for about 6 months.⭐ Dapsone adverse effects: hemolysis in G6PD deficiency and methemoglobinemia.
  18. 18Rifle next to Jail cell 1: Rifle = rifampin, given with dapsone for the 6-month regimen for tuberculoid leprosy.⭐ Rifampin: orange body fluids, CYP450 inducer.
  19. 19Deputy next to Jail cell 2: Deputy = dapsone: part of the triple therapy for lepromatous (multibacillary) leprosy, which requires a longer course (traditionally 2 years or more; WHO uses 12 months).
  20. 20Rifle next to Jail cell 2: Rifle = rifampin: part of the triple therapy for lepromatous (multibacillary) leprosy.
  21. 21Cloth next to Jail cell 2: Cloth = CLOfazimine: the third drug added for lepromatous leprosy; its side effect is skin discoloration (pink-brown darkening).⭐ Clofazimine causes skin discoloration.
Show the original legend picture

70. Mycobacterium tuberculosis

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: chronic cough, hemoptysis, night sweats, weight loss; upper-lobe cavities (reactivation); hilar nodes and Ghon complex (primary)
  • Micro: acid-fast bacillus, obligate aerobe, slow growing, cord factor and sulfatides; intracellular in macrophages with caseating granulomas
  • Diagnosis: sputum acid-fast smear and culture (Lowenstein-Jensen), NAAT; PPD or IGRA for latent infection; CXR
  • Treatment: RIPE (rifampin, isoniazid, pyrazinamide, ethambutol); latent TB with isoniazid or rifampin-based regimens; BCG vaccine in endemic areas
  • Buzzwords: caseating granuloma, Langhans giant cells, Ghon complex, miliary seeding, Pott disease, cord factor
  1. 1Tuberculosis cactus: The cactus is a TB (tuberculosis) icon: Mycobacterium tuberculosis is an acid-fast bacillus, spread by inhalation, with a thick lipid-rich wall.
  2. 2Mycolic acid tassels: M. tuberculosis stains acid-fast because its cell wall contains abundant mycolic acid (long-chain fatty acids).⭐ Mycolic acid synthesis is inhibited by isoniazid.
  3. 3Quick draw fuschia gun and holster: Quick draw fuchsin = carbol fuchsin: the Ziehl-Neelsen method uses carbol fuchsin to stain acid-fast bacteria such as M. tuberculosis; the auramine-rhodamine fluorescent stain is another option.
  4. 4"Lowenstein General Store": M. tuberculosis is cultured on Lowenstein-Jensen medium (egg-based, with malachite green that inhibits contaminants); growth is slow, with colonies taking 2-6 weeks. Middlebrook agar is another option.⭐ Lowenstein-Jensen = egg-based medium; slow growth (weeks).
  5. 5Aerobic bellows: M. tuberculosis is an obligate aerobe: it prefers well-oxygenated tissue (upper lobes). It spreads from person to person by respiratory droplets (airborne, requiring negative pressure rooms).⭐ Obligate aerobe is why reactivation TB favors the upper lobes (high PO2).
  6. 6Jail cage: TB survives and replicates in macrophages, evading defenses. If the immune system fails to clear it quickly, the bacteria persist, form granulomas, and cause disease. Three outcomes are possible: contained latent infection, disseminated (miliary) TB, or later reactivation.
  7. 7Cord-like lasso + cage: Cord factor (trehalose dimycolate) is a surface glycolipid that makes bacteria grow in serpentine cords. It inhibits macrophage function, blocks phagolysosome fusion, and induces granuloma formation and TNF-alpha release.⭐ Cord factor = virulence factor; correlates with virulence.
  8. 8Spurs + phagosome-lysosome dust clouds: Sulfatides are surface glycolipids that inhibit phagosome-lysosome fusion in macrophages, helping the organism survive intracellularly and modulate immune responses.⭐ Sulfatides block phagolysosome fusion.
  9. 9Bullet holes in middle lung cactus: Primary TB (first infection) causes lesions in the lower/middle lung zones (areas of high ventilation, often the lower part of the upper lobe or the middle and lower lobes): focal infiltrate or consolidation on CXR. It is usually asymptomatic or mild (low-grade fever, fatigue, cough).⭐ Primary TB = lower/middle lobes; reactivation = upper lobes.
  10. 10Swollen cacti flowers: Primary TB commonly shows hilar (and mediastinal) lymphadenopathy because organisms drain to hilar nodes.
  11. 11Gun Complex: Gun = Ghon complex, the radiographic hallmark of primary TB: a Ghon focus (a calcified peripheral subpleural lung lesion) plus ipsilateral hilar or mediastinal lymph node involvement.⭐ Ghon focus + hilar nodes = Ghon complex; calcified = Ranke complex.
  12. 12Necrotic cage wagons: TB forms caseating granulomas: a central area of soft, cheese-like necrosis (caseous necrosis) surrounded by epithelioid macrophages, T lymphocytes and Langhans multinucleated giant cells (fused macrophages in a horseshoe arrangement).⭐ Caseating granuloma + Langhans giant cells = TB.
  13. 13Sleeping kid: Latent TB: M. tuberculosis is dormant, contained by the immune system but not eradicated; the patient is asymptomatic, not contagious, and has a positive PPD/IGRA with normal CXR.⭐ Latent TB is asymptomatic and not contagious.
  14. 14Burlap sack + kid: When primary TB is not completely cleared it heals by fibrosis and calcification, especially in children, walling off the organisms in lung tissue; this healed, contained state is latent TB.
  15. 15Shovel in dirt mound: The PPD (Mantoux) skin test measures induration (not erythema) 48-72 hours after intradermal injection of tuberculin. Positive cutoffs: 5 mm in high-risk people (HIV, recent contact, immunosuppressed, abnormal CXR), 10 mm in other risk groups (high-prevalence countries, health care workers, diabetes, etc.), 15 mm in people without risk factors.⭐ PPD measures a type IV hypersensitivity reaction; IGRA is not affected by BCG.
  16. 16"BCG" syringe in dirt mound: Prior BCG vaccination (Bacille Calmette-Guerin, live attenuated M. bovis) can cause false-positive PPD results; an interferon-gamma release assay (IGRA) is not affected by BCG.⭐ BCG causes a false-positive PPD, not IGRA.
  17. 17"Millet" sign: Miliary TB: widespread hematogenous dissemination (millet seeds) involving lungs, liver, spleen, bone marrow, etc., with fever, weight loss and fatigue; CXR shows numerous tiny 1-3 mm nodules (millet seed pattern).
  18. 18Millet seeds spreading everywhere: Miliary TB: seeds scattered all over show hematogenous spread to multiple organs; diffuse tiny nodules ('millet seeds') on CXR.⭐ Miliary TB: tiny nodules throughout both lungs.
  19. 19Skull on millet seed: Miliary TB can be rapidly fatal if untreated (the skull), especially with meningitis or in the immunocompromised.
  20. 20Cane: Reactivation of latent TB occurs with immunosuppression (HIV/AIDS, aging, cancer, immunosuppressive drugs), when TNF-alpha and cell-mediated immunity that maintain the granuloma fall and dormant bacteria reactivate.⭐ HIV is the strongest risk factor for reactivation.
  21. 21Tied to "TNF" barrels: TNF-alpha inhibitors (infliximab, adalimumab, etanercept) impair granuloma integrity and increase reactivation of latent TB, so screen for latent TB before starting them.⭐ Screen for latent TB before TNF-alpha inhibitor therapy.
  22. 22Bullet holes in top of cactus: Reactivation (secondary) TB usually involves the upper lobes (apical, high oxygen tension) with cavitation, fibrosis and patchy infiltrates.⭐ Secondary TB = apical cavities.
  23. 23Coughing blood + sweating: Reactivation TB presents with chronic cough, hemoptysis, night sweats, anorexia and weight loss.⭐ Hemoptysis may come from a Rasmussen aneurysm (pulmonary artery erosion in a cavity).
  24. 24Cracked vertebral pots: Pott disease is spinal TB (hematogenous spread to vertebral bodies): vertebral destruction, soft tissue (paravertebral cold abscess) swelling, vertebral collapse, kyphosis (gibbus) and neurologic deficits. It is the most common form of skeletal TB.⭐ Pott disease = TB of the vertebrae.
  25. 25Bullet hole in hat: CNS TB: TB meningitis (headache, fever, neck stiffness, basilar meningitis with cranial nerve palsies) and tuberculomas (granulomatous masses causing focal deficits or seizures).⭐ CSF: lymphocytic pleocytosis, high protein, low glucose.
  26. 26"RIPE taters": Standard TB treatment: Rifampin, Isoniazid, Pyrazinamide and Ethambutol (RIPE) for 2 months, then rifampin and isoniazid for 4 more months.⭐ Side effects: rifampin - orange fluids; INH - neuropathy (give B6) and hepatitis; PZA - hyperuricemia; ethambutol - optic neuritis.
  27. 27Rifle: Rifle = rifampin: with isoniazid, rifampin is used in latent TB to prevent reactivation (e.g., rifampin alone for 4 months or isoniazid plus rifapentine for 3 months).⭐ Rifampin is a CYP450 inducer.
  28. 28Isolated behind cage wagon: Isolated = isoniazid (INH). Isoniazid, alone (9 months) or with rifampin or rifapentine in shorter regimens, treats latent TB (the cage wagon being the contained granuloma) to prevent reactivation.⭐ Give pyridoxine (B6) with isoniazid to prevent neuropathy.
Show the original legend picture

71. Mycoplasma pneumoniae

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: young adult or military recruit with gradual malaise, low-grade fever and dry cough ('walking pneumonia'); CXR looks worse than the patient
  • Micro: smallest free-living bacterium, no cell wall, cholesterol in the membrane, doesn't Gram stain; pleomorphic
  • Diagnosis: PCR or serology; cold agglutinins (IgM) titer; culture on Eaton agar (rarely used)
  • Treatment: macrolide (azithromycin) or doxycycline or a fluoroquinolone; beta-lactams are ineffective
  • Buzzwords: atypical/walking pneumonia, cold agglutinins, no cell wall, Eaton agar, bullous myringitis, erythema multiforme
  1. 1Walking on ice: Walking on ice = cold (cold agglutinins) and walking pneumonia: Mycoplasma pneumoniae is a pleomorphic bacterium that is gram-indeterminate (does not Gram stain well).⭐ Smallest free-living bacterium.
  2. 2No wall: Mycoplasma lacks a cell wall (no peptidoglycan), so it cannot be seen on Gram stain and is intrinsically resistant to cell-wall-active drugs (beta-lactams, vancomycin).⭐ Lacks a cell wall; resistant to penicillins.
  3. 3Sterol net pattern: The only bacterial membrane with cholesterol (a four-ring sterol) is Mycoplasma's, providing stability in the absence of a wall; sterols must be added to culture media.⭐ Mycoplasma has sterols in its plasma membrane.
  4. 4Patchy clouds: On CXR, Mycoplasma pneumonia shows reticulonodular or patchy (interstitial) infiltrates that look worse than the mild clinical picture.⭐ X-ray looks much worse than the patient.
  5. 5Walking: 'Walking pneumonia': atypical pneumonia with a slow onset, low-grade fever, dry (nonproductive) cough and malaise; patients can still walk around.⭐ Atypical pneumonia: dry cough, extrapulmonary features, no sputum organisms on Gram stain.
  6. 6Camo jersey: Spread by respiratory droplets in crowded settings with close contact: military recruits, college dormitories, prisons.
  7. 7"<30" jersey: Mycoplasma pneumonia is most common in young adults and school-age children, typically people under 30.
  8. 8IgM snowflakes connecting RBC pucks: Mycoplasma induces IgM cold agglutinins (anti-I antibodies) that bind red cells at low temperatures and cause agglutination, sometimes leading to hemolytic anemia. A cold agglutinin titer supports the diagnosis.⭐ Cold agglutinins are IgM against the I antigen.
  9. 9"Don't EAT ON ice": Eaton agar (contains sterols and serum) is the special medium for growing Mycoplasma pneumoniae; colonies are tiny 'fried egg'-like. It is rarely used in practice; PCR is preferred.⭐ Eaton agar for Mycoplasma.
  10. 10Macrolide crows: Crows = macrolides (azithromycin), the first-line treatment; Mycoplasma has no cell wall, so beta-lactams are ineffective. Doxycycline and fluoroquinolones are alternatives.⭐ Macrolides, doxycycline, fluoroquinolones; not beta-lactams.
Show the original legend picture

72. Naegleria fowleri

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: fresh-water swimming or nasal irrigation in warm water, then rapidly fatal meningoencephalitis within days.
  • Micro: free-living amoeba (protozoan) found in warm freshwater; trophozoites enter via the nose and cribriform plate.
  • Diagnosis: CSF with motile trophozoites on wet mount, PCR; CSF is purulent with low glucose and high protein.
  • Treatment: amphotericin B (plus miltefosine and other agents) but mortality is >95%; prevention is avoiding warm water nasal exposure.
  • Buzzwords: primary amebic meningoencephalitis (PAM), cribriform plate, lakes or hot springs, neti pot.
  1. 1Niagara Falls: Naegleria fowleri is a free-living amoeba (a protozoan) that causes infection of the central nervous system. Think 'Naegleria' = 'Niagara' (a waterfall, i.e., fresh water).⭐ It is a free-living amoeba, not transmitted person to person.
  2. 2Freshwater: N. fowleri thrives in warm, stagnant freshwater such as lakes, rivers, hot springs and poorly chlorinated pools, especially when the water is above about 30 C (86 F), and is more common in summer.
  3. 3Cribs: The amoeba enters through the nose, then migrates along the olfactory nerves through the cribriform plate to reach the brain (frontal lobes and olfactory bulbs).⭐ Cribriform plate = portal of entry.
  4. 4Neck brace and red turban: N. fowleri causes primary amebic meningoencephalitis (PAM). Early signs resemble bacterial meningitis (headache, fever, stiff neck, nausea, vomiting), followed by encephalitis (confusion, seizures, neurologic deterioration, coma) and rapid progression toward death.
  5. 5Dead barrel rider: PAM progresses very quickly (death typically within about 1-2 weeks of symptom onset) and has an extremely high mortality rate, over 95%.⭐ Fulminant, almost uniformly fatal.
  6. 6Windsurfer: Infection is associated with freshwater recreation such as swimming, diving or water sports, where contaminated water is forced up the nose. It is not acquired by swallowing the water.
  7. 7Contaminated water bottles: Infection has been linked to nasal irrigation (e.g., neti pots) or ritual nasal rinsing with contaminated untreated tap water. Use boiled, distilled or sterile water for nasal rinses.⭐ Neti pot with tap water = classic exposure.
  8. 8Champagne spinal needle cork: Diagnosis requires lumbar puncture: CSF shows motile trophozoites on a fresh wet mount, along with a purulent picture (neutrophils, high protein, low glucose). PCR can confirm the organism.⭐ Examine CSF fresh and warm; the trophozoites are motile.
  9. 9Amphibians: Amphotericin B (an 'amphibian' pun) is the mainstay of treatment, often combined with miltefosine, azithromycin and other agents, but outcomes are poor and nearly all patients die despite treatment.⭐ Amphotericin B +/- miltefosine; early treatment is critical.
Show the original legend picture

73. Neisseria gonorrhoeae

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: sexually active patient with purulent urethral or cervical discharge, or migratory polyarthralgia, tenosynovitis and pustular rash (disseminated infection); neonatal conjunctivitis within days of birth.
  • Micro: gram-negative diplococci inside neutrophils, oxidase positive, grows on Thayer-Martin, no capsule, IgA protease and pilus antigenic variation.
  • Diagnosis: NAAT (urine or swab); Gram stain and culture on Thayer-Martin.
  • Treatment/prevention: ceftriaxone plus empiric treatment for chlamydia coinfection (doxycycline); condoms; neonatal prophylaxis; test and treat partners.
  • Buzzwords: intracellular diplococci, PID, Fitz-Hugh-Curtis violin-string adhesions, disseminated gonococcal infection, ophthalmia neonatorum.
  1. 1Gonzo's Bar: N. gonorrhoeae are gram-negative diplococci and the cause of gonorrhea.
  2. 22 red cherries in mixed drink: N. gonorrhoeae diplococci are found within polymorphonuclear leukocytes (PMNs) on Gram stain (they survive intracellularly).⭐ Gram-negative intracellular diplococci in PMNs.
  3. 3Broken cake capsule: N. gonorrhoeae has no capsule (unlike N. meningitidis).⭐ Lack of capsule explains why there is no vaccine and re-infection is possible.
  4. 4Embracing couple: Gonorrhea is a reportable sexually transmitted infection (STI).
  5. 5Care-free workers: N. gonorrhoeae can reside in asymptomatic carriers (especially women and pharyngeal or rectal infection), allowing silent spread.
  6. 6Blindfolded baby cherub: N. gonorrhoeae can be transmitted to the newborn in the birth canal, causing ophthalmia neonatorum within about 5 days of birth; the infection is typically purulent and severe. Chlamydia trachomatis also causes neonatal conjunctivitis at 5-14 days, usually milder and less purulent.⭐ Gonococcal conjunctivitis appears earlier (day 2-5) than chlamydial (day 5-14).
  7. 7Leaky tap: Gonorrhea can cause urethritis that is often asymptomatic but may show WBCs in urine (sterile pyuria).
  8. 8Flaming milk bomb: Symptomatic gonococcal urethritis causes dysuria and purulent urethral discharge.
  9. 9Apple seat cushion: Ascending gonococcal urethritis can involve the prostate, causing prostatitis.
  10. 10Orchid: Ascending infection can involve the epididymis and testis, causing epididymo-orchitis.
  11. 11Neck of chandelier leaking candle wax: Gonorrhea can cause mucopurulent cervicitis.
  12. 12Dripping red wax between calendar dates: Intermenstrual bleeding, such as postcoital spotting, is a warning symptom of gonococcal cervicitis.
  13. 13Arms of chandelier leaking candle wax: Ascending infection causes pelvic inflammatory disease (PID), including salpingitis and tubo-ovarian abscess, which can lead to ectopic pregnancy and infertility from tubal scarring.⭐ PID is a leading cause of ectopic pregnancy and infertility.
  14. 14Fitz, Hugh, & Curtis Trio: Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome, a complication of PID, is perihepatitis in which infection spreads to the liver capsule and peritoneum, causing right upper quadrant pain.
  15. 15Violin strings: Perihepatic inflammation leads to thin, string-like adhesions between the liver capsule and the abdominal wall ("violin string" adhesions).⭐ Violin-string adhesions = Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome (also seen with chlamydia).
  16. 16Cracked piano leg: Disseminated gonococcal infection includes purulent arthritis (septic arthritis, often a single large joint).⭐ Most common cause of septic arthritis in sexually active young adults.
  17. 17Broken bass heel string: Disseminated gonococcal infection includes tenosynovitis, commonly of the tendons of the hands, wrists or ankles (e.g. Achilles).
  18. 18Red suits: Disseminated gonococcal infection includes dermatitis (pustular or vesiculopustular skin lesions).⭐ Triad: tenosynovitis, dermatitis, migratory polyarthralgia.
  19. 19Gnats: Nucleic acid amplification testing (NAAT) is the test of choice for diagnosing N. gonorrhoeae.
  20. 203 axes in Gonzo's bar: Ceftriaxone is the drug of choice for gonorrhea (third-generation cephalosporin).⭐ Add doxycycline for presumptive chlamydia coinfection.
  21. 21Clam lady + Cycle + Crow: Treat for presumptive Chlamydia trachomatis coinfection with doxycycline (or a macrolide such as azithromycin).
  22. 22Protective equipment: Counsel patients on barrier methods, such as consistent condom use, to prevent transmission.
Show the original legend picture

74. Neisseria meningitidis

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: college student or military recruit with fever, headache, neck stiffness and a rapidly spreading petechial or purpuric rash.
  • Micro: gram-negative diplococcus, oxidase positive, ferments maltose and glucose, polysaccharide capsule, LOS endotoxin.
  • Diagnosis: Gram stain and culture of CSF and blood (chocolate or Thayer-Martin agar); CSF shows high protein, low glucose, neutrophils.
  • Treatment: ceftriaxone (penicillin G alternative); prophylaxis for close contacts with rifampin, ciprofloxacin or ceftriaxone; vaccines (ACWY, B).
  • Buzzwords: maltose fermenter, Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome, DIC, terminal complement (C5-C9) deficiency, asplenia.
  1. 1General Mater: Neisseria meningitidis (meningococcus) is a gram-negative diplococcus and human-only pathogen that causes bacterial (meningococcal) meningitis and meningococcemia.⭐ Gram-negative diplococci that are oxidase positive.
  2. 2Single MALT whiskey: N. meningitidis ferments maltose (and glucose), whereas N. gonorrhoeae ferments glucose only.⭐ MeninGOCOCCUS = Maltose and Glucose; Gonococcus = Glucose only.
  3. 3Pili earring hanging by nose: N. meningitidis expresses pili that promote attachment and colonization of the nasopharynx (many healthy people carry it asymptomatically).
  4. 4Liquor capsule: The polysaccharide capsule is an antiphagocytic virulence factor that prevents phagocytosis and complement killing; it is also the basis of the vaccines.
  5. 5Bourbon in liquor capsule: The serogroup B capsule is poorly immunogenic (it resembles polysialic acid on human neural cells), so the capsular polysaccharide vaccine does not work for serogroup B.
  6. 6Torn ace card: N. meningitidis secretes IgA protease, which cleaves secretory IgA and helps the bacteria adhere to and colonize mucosal surfaces.
  7. 7The LOSt flame: N. meningitidis has lipooligosaccharide (LOS) in its outer membrane, an endotoxin that is highly proinflammatory.⭐ LOS (not LPS) is the endotoxin; it triggers TNF, IL-1 and IL-6.
  8. 8Spreading sweat droplets: The organism is spread person to person by respiratory droplets and close contact (e.g., kissing, sharing utensils).
  9. 9Military photo + diploma: Outbreaks occur where people live in close quarters, such as military barracks and college dormitories (hence vaccination of recruits and college students).
  10. 10Flowing red fountain: After colonizing the nasopharynx, the bacteria invade the mucosa and spread hematogenously, causing bacteremia (meningococcemia) and seeding the meninges.
  11. 11Burning LOSt flame: Once in the bloodstream, LOS triggers a powerful proinflammatory cytokine response (TNF, IL-1, IL-6) that drives septic shock and tissue damage.
  12. 12Spreading embers: Meningococci shed blebs of outer membrane, each laden with LOS, which amplify the massive inflammatory response.
  13. 13Red vines overtaking fountain: The inflammatory response injures blood vessels: endothelial damage, capillary leakage, intravascular thrombosis and hemorrhagic necrosis, producing the purpuric skin lesions of meningococcemia.
  14. 14Lightning bolt: Capillary leakage allows fluid to leave the vasculature, causing hypovolemia and ultimately septic shock (hypotension).
  15. 15Fallen plates + stacked firewood: Endothelial injury and activation of coagulation lead to platelet consumption (thrombocytopenia) and microvascular thrombosis.
  16. 16Rolled dice: Meningococcemia can trigger disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC), with simultaneous clotting and bleeding.⭐ Low platelets, high PT/PTT, elevated D-dimer.
  17. 17Protein C corporal: Acquired dysfunction of the protein C anticoagulant pathway contributes to the development and progression of the coagulopathy (purpura fulminans).
  18. 18WF Water tower: Hemorrhagic necrosis of the adrenal glands in fulminant meningococcemia causes acute adrenal insufficiency, called Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome (WF).⭐ WF syndrome = adrenal hemorrhage + shock + purpura.
  19. 19Falling peanuts and candies + hanging bananas: Adrenal insufficiency causes metabolic derangements: hypoglycemia (low cortisol), hyponatremia, and hyperkalemia (low aldosterone), plus refractory hypotension.
  20. 20Meningitis mohawk: N. meningitidis is a leading cause of bacterial meningitis, especially in adolescents and young adults, with fever, headache and nuchal rigidity.
  21. 21Antibody flame arrow: Late (immune-complex mediated) sequelae of meningococcal infection can occur, such as pericarditis and arthritis, typically days after the acute illness.
  22. 22Flame bandana: The prodrome of meningococcal disease is a nonspecific febrile, flu-like illness.
  23. 23Pain lines: Meningococcal infection can cause intense myalgias and limb pain, often early in sepsis.
  24. 24Red necktie: Meningococcus can cause pharyngitis resembling strep throat, reflecting nasopharyngeal colonization or infection.
  25. 25Red spotted jacket: Meningococcemia produces a petechial and purpuric rash that spreads rapidly and does not blanch, a medical emergency.⭐ Non-blanching petechiae + fever = treat immediately.
  26. 262nd place: N. meningitidis is the second most common cause of acute bacterial meningitis in adults (after Streptococcus pneumoniae) and a leading cause in teens and young adults.
  27. 27Neck brace: Meningococcal meningitis presents with the classic triad of fever, headache and neck stiffness (nuchal rigidity), often with photophobia and altered mental status.
  28. 28Needle cork: Lumbar puncture with CSF analysis shows elevated protein, low glucose, neutrophilic pleocytosis (leukocytosis), and gram-negative diplococci on Gram stain or culture.⭐ Do not delay antibiotics for LP.
  29. 29Tri-axe: Ceftriaxone is the usual empiric and definitive antibiotic for meningococcal disease.⭐ Ceftriaxone is a drug of choice.
  30. 30Purple pencil: Penicillin G is also effective against N. meningitidis and used when the isolate is susceptible.
  31. 31Chloroform: Chloramphenicol is an alternative for patients with severe penicillin or beta-lactam allergy.
  32. 32PPx shield: Antibiotic post-exposure prophylaxis is given to close contacts (household members, those with prolonged close contact such as more than 8 hours in the prior 7 days, or direct contact with oral secretions). Options are rifampin, ceftriaxone or ciprofloxacin.
  33. 33Collector's rifle: Rifampin is commonly used for prophylaxis of close contacts (a rifle for shooting down colonization).⭐ Rifampin turns body fluids orange and induces CYP450.
  34. 34Flower: Ciprofloxacin is another prophylaxis option, as a single dose.
  35. 354 syringe shooters under capsule: The quadrivalent meningococcal vaccine covers capsular serogroups A, C, Y and W-135. Older versions used plain capsular polysaccharide; current vaccines are conjugated.
  36. 36It's DIPped: The conjugate vaccine links capsular polysaccharide to a carrier protein such as diphtheria toxoid, producing a T-cell dependent response with memory.
  37. 37Syringe in Bourbon: The serogroup B vaccine is made of recombinant (protein) antigens because the B capsule is poorly immunogenic.
  38. 38Spleen-shaped hole: The vaccine is indicated for asplenic patients (including functional asplenia such as sickle cell disease) because they cannot clear encapsulated bacteria.
  39. 39MAC's 5-9: The vaccine is also indicated for patients with terminal complement component deficiency (C5-C9, the membrane attack complex) or on complement inhibitors such as eculizumab; they are prone to recurrent Neisseria infections.⭐ MAC deficiency = recurrent Neisseria.

75. Neisseria Overview

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: gonorrhea (urethritis, PID, septic arthritis) or meningococcal meningitis/sepsis with petechiae; recurrent infection suggests terminal complement (C5-C9) deficiency.
  • Micro: gram-negative diplococci, oxidase positive, grow on Thayer-Martin (chocolate agar with VPN); gonococcus ferments glucose only, meningococcus glucose and maltose.
  • Diagnosis: Gram stain, culture on Thayer-Martin; NAAT for gonorrhea.
  • Treatment/prevention: ceftriaxone; meningococcal vaccination and chemoprophylaxis for contacts; condoms for gonorrhea.
  • Buzzwords: diplococci, chocolate agar, IgA protease, pilus antigenic variation, LOS, MAC deficiency.
  1. 1Red curtains: Neisseria are gram-negative bacteria; the genus includes N. gonorrhoeae and N. meningitidis.
  2. 2Handcuffs: Neisseria are gram-negative diplococci (paired, kidney-bean shaped).
  3. 3Movie candy: N. gonorrhoeae ferments glucose only, whereas N. meningitidis ferments both glucose and maltose.⭐ Meningococcus = Maltose and glucose (MeninGOCOCCUS).
  4. 4Blue onyx pendant: Neisseria are oxidase positive.
  5. 5Chocolate bar: Neisseria do not grow well on plain sheep blood agar but grow on chocolate agar (blood agar heated to lyse RBCs, releasing factors V [NAD+] and X [hemin]).⭐ Factors V and X are also needed by Haemophilus.
  6. 6Vice city Private iNvestigator: VPN is the selective supplement added to chocolate agar: Vancomycin (kills gram-positives), Polymyxin (kills most gram-negatives except Neisseria), Nystatin (kills fungi) and Trimethoprim (kills Proteus).
  7. 7Starring Thayer Martin: Thayer-Martin medium = chocolate agar plus VPN antibiotics; this enriched, selective medium is used clinically to isolate Neisseria species.⭐ Thayer-Martin is the classic selective medium for Neisseria.
  8. 8MAC, P.IL.: The membrane attack complex (MAC), formed at the terminal stage of the complement pathway, is an essential host defense against Neisseria infections.
  9. 95-9 PM: Deficiency of terminal complement components C5-C9 increases susceptibility to recurrent Neisseria infections.⭐ Recurrent meningococcemia or disseminated gonococcal infection: check CH50.
  10. 10Variable pili earrings: Pili mediate attachment to mucosal surfaces and undergo frequent antigenic variation to evade the immune system.⭐ Antigenic variation of pili hinders vaccine development for gonorrhea.
  11. 11Torn Ace card: Neisseria produce IgA protease, which cleaves IgA at the hinge region and allows easier mucosal colonization.⭐ Also made by H. influenzae and S. pneumoniae.
  12. 12The LOSt Flame: Lipooligosaccharide (LOS) is a proinflammatory endotoxin-like molecule of Neisseria, similar to endotoxin of other gram-negative bacteria.⭐ LOS lacks the O antigen of LPS.
  13. 13Opaque window: Opa (opacity) proteins on the Neisseria outer surface promote tight binding to each other and to host cells, aiding colonization.
