86. Proteus mirabilis

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: complicated UTI with alkaline urine, flank pain, and staghorn calculi.
  • Microbiology: gram-negative bacillus, lactose non-fermenter, urease positive, highly motile (swarming on agar), H2S producer.
  • Diagnosis: urine culture; alkaline urine pH; struvite (magnesium ammonium phosphate) stones, coffin-lid crystals, on imaging/urinalysis.
  • Treatment: antibiotics (e.g., trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole for uncomplicated UTI); large stones often need urologic removal because bacteria persist in the stone.
  • Buzzwords: 'swarming', 'urease', 'struvite', 'staghorn calculi', 'alkaline urine', 'fishy/ammonia odor'.
  1. 1Proteus: Proteus mirabilis, a gram-negative bacillus.
  2. 2Red hues: Red hues = gram-negative: Proteus mirabilis is a gram-negative bacillus (lactose non-fermenter).
  3. 3Swarming tentacles: Proteus mirabilis shows swarming motility on agar plates, spreading in concentric waves (a clue to its flagellar motility).⭐ 'Swarming' on culture is a classic buzzword.
  4. 4Staghorn helmet: Urease-positive bacteria like Proteus can cause staghorn calculi (struvite stones) that fill the renal pelvis and calyces like stag horns, causing flank pain and renal impairment and harboring bacteria.⭐ Staghorn calculus = think Proteus.
  5. 5Ammonia bottle: Proteus produces urease, which hydrolyzes urea into CO2 and ammonia, raising urine pH. The alkaline urine promotes precipitation of magnesium ammonium phosphate (struvite) stones.
  6. 6Stone: Struvite stones cause pain and urinary tract damage and act as a nidus for recurrent Proteus infections, since bacteria hide within the stone.⭐ Urine pH is alkaline (>7).
  7. 7Peeing: Proteus mirabilis is a common cause of urinary tract infections, especially complicated cases or those with abnormal urinary tracts or catheters.
  8. 8Stinky fish: Proteus mirabilis produces a characteristic fishy (ammonia) odor.
  9. 9Eggs: Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (a sulfonamide combination) is active against Proteus mirabilis and commonly used for uncomplicated UTIs; the egg is a cue for the antibiotic choice.⭐ Fluoroquinolones and cephalosporins are other options.
Show the original legend picture

87. Pseudomonas aeruginosa

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: hospitalized, burned, cystic fibrosis, diabetic or neutropenic patient with blue-green pus, pneumonia, UTI or ecthyma gangrenosum.
  • Micro: gram-negative rod, obligate aerobe, oxidase positive, non-lactose fermenter, motile, produces pyocyanin and a grape-like odor.
  • Diagnosis: culture (blue-green colonies on agar); oxidase positive, lactose non-fermenter.
  • Treatment: antipseudomonal agents such as piperacillin-tazobactam, ceftazidime, cefepime, carbapenems, aminoglycosides, fluoroquinolones.
  • Buzzwords: blue-green pus, grape-like odor, hot tub folliculitis, swimmer's ear, exotoxin A, mucoid alginate.
  1. 1Pseudo Mona: Pseudomonas aeruginosa is a gram-negative bacillus (the name is a pun on 'Pseudo Mona').⭐ It is a lactose non-fermenter that grows aerobically.
  2. 2Red bathtub: Gram-negative bacilli stain pink-red on Gram stain (the red bathtub represents the pink rod shape and its water habitat).
  3. 3Water: P. aeruginosa is found in moist and aquatic environments such as lakes, rivers, pools, hot tubs, sinks and hospital equipment, where it survives easily.
  4. 4Blue oxidase ring: P. aeruginosa is oxidase positive; the oxidase enzyme is part of the aerobic electron transport chain.⭐ Oxidase positive helps distinguish from Enterobacterales.
  5. 5Catalase cat: P. aeruginosa is catalase positive; catalase breaks hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen.
  6. 6Blue-green residue: P. aeruginosa makes pigments including pyocyanin (blue-green), pyoverdin (yellow-green, fluorescent), pyomelanin (brown) and pyorubin (red); pus and cultures can look blue-green.⭐ Blue-green pus = Pseudomonas.
  7. 7Aromatic grapes: Cultures have a sweet, fruity, grape-like odor, from 2-aminoacetophenone.
  8. 8Aerobic bellows: P. aeruginosa is an obligate aerobe that requires oxygen (although it can use nitrate as an electron acceptor in some conditions).
  9. 9Nurse: It is a leading cause of nosocomial (hospital-acquired) infection, including ventilator-associated pneumonia and infections of burn and catheter patients.
  10. 10Chloride + coughing: In cystic fibrosis, defective CFTR chloride channels reduce Cl- secretion into the airway lumen, and unrestrained ENaC pulls sodium (and water) into cells, so airway mucus is dehydrated and thick. Impaired mucociliary clearance leads to chronic Pseudomonas colonization, biofilm formation, chronic lung infection and eventually respiratory failure.⭐ Pseudomonas is the major cause of CF lung disease morbidity.
  11. 11Candy: Diabetes (candy = sugar) predisposes to Pseudomonas osteomyelitis, e.g., of the foot, and to malignant otitis externa.
  12. 12Mortar and pestle: Intravenous drug use (preparing drugs, mortar and pestle) predisposes to Pseudomonas osteomyelitis, often of the vertebrae or sternoclavicular joint.
  13. 13Fish bones: Bone infection (osteomyelitis) from Pseudomonas; classically puncture wounds through a sneaker in children (foot osteomyelitis) and in diabetics and IV drug users.⭐ Nail puncture through a sneaker = Pseudomonas osteomyelitis.
  14. 14Glass capsule: P. aeruginosa makes a polysaccharide capsule-like biofilm called alginate, which makes colonies mucoid and protects against phagocytosis and antibiotics; mucoid strains are typical in cystic fibrosis.
  15. 15Burning fire: Burn injuries disrupt the skin barrier and create a moist wound where Pseudomonas colonizes and infects, leading to wound infection and sepsis; topical silver sulfadiazine or mafenide is used for prevention.
  16. 16Chamber pot: Pseudomonas is a common cause of urinary tract infections, especially with indwelling urinary catheters, due to biofilm formation.
  17. 17Bathtub: Hot tub folliculitis presents as itchy red papules and pustules around hair follicles after exposure to warm, poorly chlorinated water, usually self-limited.
  18. 18Dalmatian spots: Ecthyma gangrenosum is a necrotic black skin lesion with a surrounding erythematous halo, caused by Pseudomonas bacteremia in immunocompromised (especially neutropenic) patients.
  19. 19Running dalmatian: P. aeruginosa is motile, with a polar flagellum, allowing swimming and swarming motility on surfaces.
  20. 20Ear trumpet: Pseudomonas is the classic cause of otitis externa (swimmer's ear), causing ear pain, itching and purulent discharge; in diabetics it can cause malignant (necrotizing) otitis externa.⭐ Malignant otitis externa: diabetic elderly, Pseudomonas.
  21. 21Accordion + bowtie: Exotoxin A inactivates elongation factor 2 (EF-2) by ADP ribosylation, which stops protein synthesis in host cells. This is the same mechanism as diphtheria toxin and causes cell death (liver necrosis).⭐ Same mechanism as diphtheria toxin.
  22. 22Piper: Piperacillin and ticarcillin (antipseudomonal penicillins, usually given with a beta-lactamase inhibitor such as tazobactam) cover Pseudomonas; ceftazidime, cefepime, carbapenems and aztreonam are also active.
  23. 23Flower: Fluoroquinolones (ciprofloxacin, levofloxacin) have antipseudomonal activity, although resistance develops easily.
  24. 24Sai: Aminoglycosides (tobramycin, gentamicin, amikacin) are used against Pseudomonas, often combined with a beta-lactam for serious infection.⭐ Aminoglycoside toxicities: nephrotoxicity, ototoxicity.
Show the original legend picture

88. Rabies virus

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: animal bite (bat, raccoon, skunk, dog abroad) followed weeks later by paresthesias, agitation, hydrophobia and fatal encephalitis.
  • Micro: Rhabdoviridae; enveloped, bullet-shaped, helical, negative-sense ssRNA virus with G glycoprotein; Negri bodies.
  • Diagnosis: direct fluorescent antibody on nuchal skin biopsy, PCR on saliva, antibody in serum or CSF; Negri bodies on autopsy.
  • Treatment: post-exposure prophylaxis with rabies immune globulin and inactivated vaccine; almost uniformly fatal once symptomatic.
  • Buzzwords: Negri bodies, hydrophobia, bullet-shaped, retrograde axonal transport, bat.
  1. 1Rabid Bulldog: Rabies virus is a negative-sense RNA virus in the Rhabdoviridae family, causing a fatal encephalitis.
  2. 2Warm colors: Rhabdoviridae have negative-sense single-stranded RNA genomes.
  3. 3Moon with negative sign-shaped clouds: Rhabdoviridae are negative-sense ssRNA viruses (second reminder); the virion carries its own RNA-dependent RNA polymerase.
  4. 4Curly tail: Rhabdoviruses have a helical nucleocapsid, which is wound around the RNA.
  5. 5Hoodie: Rhabdoviridae are enveloped, acquiring the envelope by budding from the host cell membrane; the spike-shaped glycoprotein G mediates attachment to host cell receptors.
  6. 6Bullets: Rhabdoviruses are bullet-shaped (rod-shaped with one rounded end) viruses on electron microscopy; 'rhabdo' means rod.
  7. 7Bat: Rabies is a zoonosis, spread from animals to humans; in the US most human cases are from bats (often unnoticed bites).⭐ Bats are the leading source of human rabies in the US.
  8. 8Racoon: In the US wildlife reservoirs of rabies include raccoons (as well as bats, skunks and foxes), with the main reservoir varying by region.
  9. 9Skunk: Skunks are another major US wildlife reservoir of rabies virus.
  10. 10Fox: Foxes are also a US wildlife reservoir; worldwide, dogs are the major source of human rabies.
  11. 11Cigar: Rabies virus attaches to the nicotinic acetylcholine receptor (nicotine = cigar) at the neuromuscular junction (motor end plate).
  12. 12Acetyl cola cans: Acetylcholine cans: the virus binds nicotinic acetylcholine receptors on the postsynaptic membrane, also called the motor end plate.
  13. 13Outlet cover plate: The 'plate' stands for the motor end plate, the neuromuscular junction site where rabies binds.
  14. 14Baby bulldogs near motor area of car: After a bite, the virus replicates locally in muscle near the motor end plate; this is the incubation period (usually weeks to months, depending on proximity to the CNS).
  15. 15Bullet leash: The virus enters peripheral nerves and travels by retrograde axonal transport to the CNS; it then spreads outward along autonomic and somatic nerves to highly innervated tissues such as salivary glands, cornea and skin, which allows shedding in saliva.⭐ Retrograde axonal transport.
  16. 16Tree roots: In the CNS the virus infects neurons including dorsal root ganglia, contributing to pain, tingling and paresthesia at the bite site.
  17. 17Sweating: The prodrome includes flu-like symptoms (fever, sore throat, headache, photophobia, malaise) and abnormal sensations (burning, tingling, itching) at the bite site.
  18. 18Foam on mouth: Virus spreads to the salivary glands, causing hypersalivation, and involves the brainstem with laryngeal and pharyngeal spasms and dysphagia. Together they cause foaming at the mouth and hydrophobia (painful swallowing spasms), and the virus is shed in saliva.⭐ Hydrophobia and foaming at the mouth.
  19. 19Red turban: Rabies causes severe encephalitis with fever, headache, altered mental status, agitation or paralysis, seizures and autonomic instability; it is almost always fatal once symptoms begin.
  20. 20iNtEGRIty boat: Negri bodies are eosinophilic (pink-red) round or oval cytoplasmic inclusions in neurons seen on H&E stain; they are made of rabies nucleocapsid protein aggregates. They are pathognomonic for rabies (seen on autopsy).⭐ Negri bodies = rabies.
  21. 21Rust spots: The rust-colored spots again represent eosinophilic cytoplasmic Negri bodies in infected neurons.
  22. 22Seahorse decal + pyramid-shaped tent: The seahorse is the hippocampus and the pyramid-shaped tent represents pyramidal neurons: Negri bodies are found in hippocampal pyramidal neurons.
  23. 23Bungee cords + cerebellar tree: The bungee cord and tree represent cerebellar Purkinje cells (arbor vitae), where Negri bodies are also found.
  24. 24Antibody keys: Passive immunization: rabies immune globulin (RIG) provides pre-formed antibodies to neutralize virus at the wound site, giving immediate short-term protection until the vaccine produces active immunity. Infiltrate it into and around the wound.⭐ RIG at the wound + vaccine at a distant site.
  25. 25Syringe tranquilizer gun + skull and cross bones: Active immunization: the rabies vaccine is an inactivated (killed) virus vaccine; it induces neutralizing antibodies in about 7-14 days (given on days 0, 3, 7 and 14). Pre-exposure vaccination is for high-risk people (veterinarians, lab workers, animal handlers, travelers to endemic areas). The skull and crossbones signify that rabies is fatal if prophylaxis is not given.⭐ Give both RIG and vaccine as post-exposure prophylaxis.
Show the original legend picture

89. Rhinovirus

Exam snapshot
  • Common cold: rhinorrhea, nasal congestion, sneezing, sore throat; self-limited
  • Naked (non-enveloped), positive-sense ssRNA picornavirus; acid-labile; grows best at 33C; >100 serotypes
  • Diagnosis is clinical; no routine testing needed
  • Supportive care only; hand hygiene reduces spread; no vaccine
  • Buzzwords: common cold, ICAM-1 receptor, acid-labile, 33C nasal temperature
  1. 1Rhinos: Rhinoviruses are members of the Picornaviridae family. They are small, positive-sense single-stranded RNA viruses that cause the common cold.⭐ Rhinovirus = 'rhino' = nose; the #1 cause of the common cold.
  2. 2Warm colors: Warm colors signal that rhinoviruses are RNA viruses.
  3. 3Positive sun: The positive sun signals that the RNA genome is positive-sense: it can act directly as mRNA and be translated by host ribosomes.⭐ Naked + positive-sense RNA genome is infectious by itself if purified.
  4. 4Baby rhino: The baby rhino reinforces the family name: rhinoviruses belong to Picornaviridae (pico = small, RNA viruses).
  5. 5Statue of David: The statue is nude: rhinoviruses lack an envelope (naked viruses), so they have no lipid membrane and are relatively resistant to detergents and alcohol-based disinfectants.
  6. 6Acidic lemon: Rhinoviruses are acid-labile: stomach acid destroys them, so unlike other picornaviruses (enteroviruses, hepatitis A) they do not spread by the fecal-oral route.⭐ Acid-labile = rhinovirus; acid-stable = enteroviruses and HAV.
  7. 7Sneeze spray: Rhinoviruses spread mainly by respiratory droplets (inhaled) and by fomites: contaminated surfaces such as doorknobs or phone screens carry virus to the hands, then the nose or eyes.
  8. 8"Please wash hands": Hand hygiene is the key preventive measure, because much transmission occurs through contaminated hands and fomites.⭐ Handwashing is the best prevention; there is no vaccine.
  9. 91 camera: Rhinovirus attaches to host cells through the ICAM-1 (intercellular adhesion molecule 1) receptor on respiratory epithelium (the '1' in the picture = ICAM-1; the camera = the receptor).⭐ Major-group rhinoviruses use ICAM-1.
  10. 10Rhinos in 33 degree shade: Rhinovirus replicates best at about 33C (91.4F), the temperature of the nasal cavity, which is cooler than core body temperature. This explains its predilection for the upper respiratory tract rather than the lungs.⭐ Grows best at 33C, so nose not lungs.
  11. 11Mud on nose and mouth: Rhinovirus most commonly causes a self-limited upper respiratory infection (common cold): rhinorrhea, nasal congestion, sneezing, and sore throat.⭐ It can trigger asthma and COPD exacerbations.
  12. 12Multicolored tent: Rhinoviruses have more than 100 serotypes. Immunity to one serotype gives little or no protection against the others, so vaccine development is impractical and people get repeated colds.⭐ Too many serotypes = no vaccine.
Show the original legend picture

90. Rickettsia Overview

Exam snapshot
  • Headache and fever followed by a maculopapular or petechial rash from small-vessel vasculitis, often after an arthropod vector bite.
  • Obligate intracellular, weakly gram-negative coccobacilli; need host CoA and NAD+; stain with Giemsa or Gimenez; infect endothelial cells.
  • Clinical suspicion plus serology (immunofluorescence) or PCR; Weil-Felix (Proteus OX cross-reactivity) is historical.
  • Doxycycline is first-line for all rickettsial infections (including children and pregnancy); do not wait for confirmation.
  • Obligate intracellular, vasculitis, Weil-Felix, arthropod vectors, Giemsa/Gimenez.
  1. 1"Rickett's tires": Rickettsia spp. are weakly gram-negative coccobacilli.
  2. 2Indoor stadium: Rickettsia are obligate intracellular pathogens that need host cells for replication and survival.
  3. 3Colorless crowd: Although the cell wall is chemically gram-negative-like, Rickettsia stain poorly with Gram stain; they are visualized with Giemsa or Gimenez stain.⭐ Gram-negative cell wall but poor Gram staining.
  4. 4CoAch: Rickettsia depend on host cells for coenzyme A (CoA), needed for metabolism, and for NAD+, needed for energy production and growth.
  5. 5"NAD+" water bottle: Same point: host-derived NAD+ (along with CoA) is required, which is why they cannot grow outside host cells.
  6. 6Light pink tennis balls: Rickettsia are weakly gram-negative (light pink) coccobacilli.
  7. 7Weil and Felix: The Weil-Felix test is an agglutination assay based on cross-reactivity between Proteus antigens (OX-19, OX-2, OX-K) and Rickettsia antibodies. It was historically the main diagnostic test, has been largely replaced by immunofluorescence and other serology, but may still be used where resources are limited.⭐ Weil-Felix: Proteus antigens agglutinate with Rickettsia antibodies.
  8. 8Holding head: Common initial symptoms of rickettsial infection include headache and fever.
  9. 9Sweating: Fever is a common early symptom of rickettsial infection, along with headache.
  10. 10Broken red strings: Rickettsiae infect endothelial cells, causing injury and inflammation (small-vessel vasculitis) with increased vascular permeability and red blood cell leakage, producing a maculopapular or petechial rash.⭐ Vasculitis explains rash, edema, and hypotension.
  11. 11Bumpy red ground: The resulting rash is maculopapular or petechial, from small-vessel vasculitis and RBC leakage.
  12. 12Bicycle tires: Doxycycline is first-line treatment for all rickettsial infections, including children and pregnant women, because benefits outweigh risks.⭐ Treat empirically; do not delay for test results.
Show the original legend picture

91. Rickettsia prowazekii

Exam snapshot
  • Epidemic typhus: fever, headache, and a rash that starts on the trunk and spreads outward, sparing palms, soles, and face, in crowded, unsanitary settings.
  • Weakly gram-negative coccobacillus, obligate intracellular; transmitted by body louse feces rubbed into bite wounds.
  • Clinical and epidemiologic suspicion; serology (immunofluorescence); Weil-Felix is historical; stain with Giemsa or Gimenez.
  • Doxycycline is first-line; control by delousing and hygiene.
  • Epidemic typhus, body louse (Pediculus humanus corporis), war and refugee camps, centrifugal rash, Brill-Zinsser disease.
  1. 1"PRO bootcamp": Rickettsia prowazekii is a weakly gram-negative coccobacillus that causes epidemic typhus.
  2. 2Indoor stadium: R. prowazekii is an obligate intracellular pathogen requiring host cells for replication and survival.
  3. 3Colorless crowd: Despite a gram-negative-type cell wall, R. prowazekii stains poorly with Gram stain and is visualized with Giemsa or Gimenez stain.
  4. 4CoAch: R. prowazekii depends on host cells for coenzyme A (CoA) and NAD+, needed for metabolism, energy production, and growth.
  5. 5"NAD+" water bottle: Same point: host NAD+ (and CoA) are required for its energy production and growth.
  6. 6Bicycle tires: Doxycycline is first-line treatment for R. prowazekii infection, including in children and pregnant women.
  7. 7Outward football play: The epidemic typhus rash starts on the trunk and spreads outward to the limbs (centrifugal), usually sparing the face, palms, and soles. Early lesions are small pink macules; later they become dull red and maculopapular (blanchable); in severe cases petechiae develop from small-vessel vasculitis.⭐ Centrifugal spread, sparing palms and soles (opposite of RMSF, which starts on wrists and ankles).
  8. 8Red clothes + white helmet, gloves, and shoes: Same rash description: the trunk (red clothes) is involved, while the face (helmet), palms (gloves), and soles (shoes) are spared.
  9. 9Military coach: Epidemic typhus is associated with war (military recruits, prisoners of war), refugee camps, poverty, homelessness, crowding, and unsanitary conditions.
  10. 10Lice footballs: Transmitted by body louse feces: the louse bites and defecates near the bite site, and scratching rubs the infected feces into the skin.⭐ Vector: Pediculus humanus corporis (body louse); not by the bite itself.
  11. 11"The outbreak" play: "Epidemic" typhus: outbreaks occur in crowded, unsanitary settings where body louse infestations spread rapidly from person to person.⭐ Recrudescent disease years later is Brill-Zinsser disease.
  12. 12Painful tackle: Myalgias and arthralgias are common in epidemic typhus, from systemic small-vessel vasculitis and inflammation.
  13. 13Tackling chest: Some patients develop pulmonary complications such as pneumonia, due to systemic vasculitis, impaired immune responses, and secondary lung infection.
  14. 14Holding head + confused: Epidemic typhus can cause encephalitis from rickettsial vasculitis in the brain; vascular injury and ischemia cause fever, dizziness, and confusion, and in severe cases seizures or coma.
Show the original legend picture

92. Rickettsia rickettsii

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: fever, headache and myalgias after a tick bite, followed 2-14 days later by a rash starting on wrists and ankles
  • Weakly gram-negative coccobacillus, obligate intracellular, infects endothelial cells (vasculitis)
  • Dx: clinical, then skin biopsy (immunohistochemistry), PCR, serology; Giemsa or Gimenez stain
  • Treatment: doxycycline (all ages, including children and pregnancy), started empirically
  • Buzzwords: Rocky Mountain spotted fever, Dermacentor tick, centripetal rash, palms and soles, Weil-Felix
  1. 1Rickett's rock climbing: Rickettsia rickettsii is a weakly gram-negative coccobacillus.
  2. 2Rock climbing holds: Rocky Mountain spotted fever, a serious tick-borne illness, is caused by Rickettsia rickettsii.⭐ Most cases in the southeastern and south-central U.S., not the Rockies.
  3. 3Indoor arena: Rickettsia rickettsii is an obligate intracellular pathogen that needs host cells for replication and survival.
  4. 4Colorless crowd: Although its cell wall is similar to that of gram-negative bacteria, R. rickettsii stains poorly with Gram stain; Rickettsia are visualized with Giemsa or Gimenez stain.
  5. 5CoAch: R. rickettsii depends on host cells for coenzyme A (CoA) and for NAD+, both needed for metabolism and growth.⭐ It cannot make its own CoA or NAD+, so it is an obligate intracellular parasite.
  6. 6"NAD+" water bottle: R. rickettsii depends on host cells for NAD+ (and coenzyme A), which are essential for energy production and growth.
  7. 7Bicycle tire: Doxycycline is first-line treatment for R. rickettsii infection, including in children and pregnant women.⭐ Start empirically; do not wait for confirmatory tests. Chloramphenicol is an alternative only in pregnancy.
  8. 8"Derma-Center" arena: The Dermacentor tick (dog or wood tick) is the primary vector; the tick transmits R. rickettsii through its saliva during a blood meal, usually requiring 6-10 hours of attachment; the bite is often painless and unnoticed, and most infections occur in spring and summer.
  9. 9Tick climbing holds: The Dermacentor tick is the primary vector of R. rickettsii; transmission usually requires 6-10 hours of attachment, the bite is often painless and unnoticed, and most infections occur in spring and summer.
  10. 10Holding head: The RMSF rash typically appears 2-14 days after early symptoms (fever, headache, myalgias), so patients may initially present without a rash.⭐ Do not wait for the rash to treat.
  11. 11No spots: The RMSF rash typically develops days after the onset of fever, headache and myalgias, so early disease can look like a nonspecific viral illness without spots.
  12. 12Spots on wrists and ankles: The RMSF rash usually begins on the wrists and ankles and spreads centrally to the trunk. Early lesions are small, pink macules that may become maculopapular, and in severe cases petechial; the palms and soles are often involved.⭐ Centripetal spread (wrists/ankles to trunk) is the classic pattern.
  13. 13Spots on whole body: The rash spreads centrally to the trunk and can become petechial, often involving the palms and soles, in more severe disease.⭐ Contrast with the rash of typhus (begins on trunk and spreads outward, sparing palms and soles).
  14. 14Grabbing head + sweating: RMSF manifests with headache and fever, caused by systemic inflammation and cytokine release from endothelial infection and small-vessel vasculitis.
  15. 15Soreness + rash: RMSF classically presents with headache, fever, myalgias and rash, resulting from endothelial infection and small-vessel vasculitis (vascular leak).⭐ Classic triad: fever, headache, rash, plus tick exposure.
Show the original legend picture

93. Rotavirus & Colorado Tick Fever Virus (Reoviridae)

Exam snapshot
  • Rotavirus: most common cause of severe watery diarrhea and dehydration in children under 5 worldwide, peaking in winter
  • Mechanism: NSP4 enterotoxin plus villous destruction causes secretory and malabsorptive diarrhea; diagnosis by stool antigen (ELISA)
  • Reoviridae: naked, double-stranded segmented RNA viruses (rotavirus 11 segments), replicating in the cytoplasm
  • Treatment is supportive (oral rehydration); prevention is the live oral vaccine (RotaTeq, Rotarix), with a small intussusception risk
  • Colorado tick fever: Rocky Mountain tick bite (Dermacentor andersoni), biphasic fever, flu-like illness, usually no rash
  1. 1Rio Grande Prix: Reoviruses (e.g., rotavirus and Colorado tick fever virus) are RNA viruses; 'Reo' stands for Respiratory Enteric Orphan.
  2. 2Warm colors: Reoviruses are RNA viruses (the warm colors group RNA viruses).
  3. 3Statue of David: Reoviruses lack an envelope (naked viruses). They are resistant to drying and stomach acid and spread fecal-oral.
  4. 4Double-stranded rope: Reoviruses are double-stranded RNA viruses. Because they are dsRNA, they are not classified as positive- or negative-sense, and they replicate in the cytoplasm. They carry their own RNA-dependent RNA polymerase in the virion, because host cells cannot copy dsRNA.
  5. 5No positive sun + no moon with negative sign-shaped clouds: Reoviruses are not classified as positive-sense or negative-sense (no sun, no negative moon) because the genome is double-stranded RNA. Each segment is transcribed by the viral polymerase to make mRNA in the cytoplasm.
  6. 611 boats: Reoviruses have segmented genomes (about 10-12 dsRNA segments); rotavirus has 11 segments. Segmentation allows reassortment between strains, which can generate new strains.⭐ Rotavirus = 11 segments, which can reassort.
  7. 7Boat rotor: Rotavirus is a double-stranded RNA virus in the Reoviridae family. Its wheel-like appearance on electron microscopy gives the name (Latin rota = wheel).
  8. 8Brown water: Rotavirus causes gastroenteritis with profuse watery diarrhea (non-bloody), often with vomiting and fever, and can lead to dehydration in infants. Diagnosis is by stool antigen detection (ELISA).
  9. 9"Nine SPeed 4 stroke": Rotavirus watery diarrhea is mediated by the NSP4 (nonstructural protein 4) enterotoxin, which increases Cl- permeability and causes Cl- secretion into the intestinal lumen. Na+ follows to maintain electroneutrality, water follows Na+ osmotically, and destruction of villous epithelium worsens fluid and electrolyte loss.check original
  10. 10"Chlo-rider" boat: NSP4 enterotoxin increases chloride permeability, so Cl- is secreted into the lumen and Na+ and water follow. Destruction of mature villous enterocytes adds malabsorptive loss of fluid and electrolytes.check original
  11. 11Snow-capped mountains: Rotavirus incidence peaks in winter in temperate climates, because the virus survives longer in cold, low-humidity conditions and indoor crowding promotes fecal-oral spread.
  12. 12Kids: Children, especially those under 5 years old, are most susceptible to rotavirus. Day care outbreaks are common.
  13. 13"#1" foam finger: Rotavirus is the leading cause of severe diarrhea in young children worldwide. Vaccination has dramatically reduced hospitalizations.
  14. 14"Welcome to Colorado" + tick: Colorado tick fever virus is a dsRNA virus in the Reoviridae family. It is transmitted by the Rocky Mountain wood tick (Dermacentor andersoni).
  15. 15Vomiting + sweating: Colorado tick fever presents with flu-like symptoms (fever, chills, headache, myalgias, fatigue, anorexia, vomiting), often with a biphasic ('saddleback') fever, in high-elevation areas of the western U.S. (Rocky Mountain states). Unlike Rocky Mountain spotted fever, it typically has no rash.⭐ Tick-borne fever without rash in the Rockies = Colorado tick fever.
  16. 16Water bottle: Treatment of rotavirus and Colorado tick fever is supportive. Oral rehydration therapy is the mainstay for rotavirus (IV fluids if severe dehydration).
  17. 17"Live" + syringe microphone: The rotavirus vaccine is a live attenuated oral vaccine: RotaTeq (pentavalent, 3 doses at 2, 4, and 6 months) or Rotarix (monovalent, 2 doses at 2 and 4 months). The first dose must be given before 15 weeks of age and the series completed by 8 months, to reduce intussusception risk. It is contraindicated in severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) and prior intussusception.
  18. 18Telescope: The rotavirus vaccine can rarely cause intussusception (bowel 'telescoping' into itself): viral replication in the gut causes Peyer's patch hypertrophy, which can act as a lead point. Presents with colicky abdominal pain, vomiting, and 'currant jelly' stool.
Show the original legend picture

94. RSV / hMPV

Exam snapshot
  • RSV: infant bronchiolitis (wheeze, cough, respiratory distress) in winter; hMPV: similar illness in children, older adults, COPD
  • Enveloped, negative-sense ssRNA, helical nucleocapsid, Pneumoviridae; F protein causes syncytia
  • Diagnosis: clinical; RSV antigen/PCR on nasopharyngeal swab
  • Supportive care; ribavirin only in severe/immunocompromised cases; palivizumab (anti-F mAb) prophylaxis in high-risk infants; droplet/contact precautions
  • Buzzwords: bronchiolitis, syncytia, F protein, palivizumab, premature infants
  1. 1Pneumo tree + viral lanterns: Pneumoviridae family: Orthopneumovirus (respiratory syncytial virus, RSV) and Metapneumovirus (human metapneumovirus, hMPV). The pneumo (lung) tree with viral lanterns marks respiratory viruses.
  2. 2Warm colors: Warm colors indicate that Pneumoviridae are RNA viruses.
  3. 3Moon with negative-sign clouds: Pneumoviridae are negative-sense single-stranded RNA viruses, so the genome must be copied by a viral RNA-dependent RNA polymerase carried in the virion.⭐ Negative-sense RNA viruses carry their own RdRp.
  4. 4Snuggly hoodie: Pneumoviridae are enveloped viruses (the hoodie = envelope), so they are fragile outside the host and are readily inactivated by soap and disinfectants.
  5. 5Spirally straw: Pneumoviridae have a helical nucleocapsid (the spiral straw).
  6. 6Graham crackers: The G protein (G for 'Graham') mediates attachment to respiratory epithelial cells. It is a major target of neutralizing antibodies.
  7. 7Sticky hands: The F (fusion) protein fuses the viral envelope with the host cell membrane and causes neighboring cells to fuse into multinucleated giant cells (syncytia). F protein is a major target of neutralizing antibodies and of the prophylactic monoclonal antibody palivizumab.⭐ F protein = fusion = syncytia; palivizumab targets F.
  8. 8Ocean droplets: Pneumoviridae spread by direct contact with secretions and by large respiratory droplets (and contaminated surfaces).
  9. 9Older ghost + cane: Pneumoviridae cause mild respiratory illness in healthy people but more severe disease in susceptible hosts: the very young, older adults with chronic conditions, and the immunocompromised.
  10. 10RSVp: RSV (human orthopneumovirus) usually causes mild rhinorrhea, congestion and cough in healthy people but can cause severe bronchiolitis and pneumonia in infants, older adults and the immunocompromised.⭐ RSV = #1 cause of bronchiolitis in infants.
  11. 11Early hatching: Prematurity is a risk factor for severe RSV infection. RSV infects children mostly in the first year of life.
  12. 12Grandfather clock + pendulungs: Children up to 2 years old with chronic lung disease (e.g., bronchopulmonary dysplasia) are at risk for severe RSV disease.
  13. 13First aid kit: RSV is a significant cause of nosocomial (hospital-acquired) infection and spreads readily in healthcare settings unless strict infection control is used.
  14. 14Water-resistant gear: Patients with RSV should be isolated with droplet (and contact) precautions to limit transmission and nosocomial outbreaks.
  15. 15Bronchial trees: RSV is the most common cause of bronchiolitis and pneumonia in infants. In bronchiolitis it infects small-airway epithelium, causing necrosis and sloughing, inflammation, edema and mucus plugging that obstruct the airway. A URI prodrome (rhinorrhea, congestion) progresses to cough, wheezing and respiratory distress; severe cases can cause respiratory failure. In pneumonia, alveoli are involved with consolidation and impaired gas exchange. Treatment is mainly supportive.⭐ Infant with wheeze in winter = RSV bronchiolitis; supportive care (oxygen, hydration).
  16. 16Ribs: Ribavirin, a nucleoside analog, can be used for severe RSV infection in immunocompromised patients. It is teratogenic, so pregnancy is an important contraindication.⭐ Ribavirin is contraindicated in pregnancy.
  17. 17Pale ghost + antibody rattle: Palivizumab is a humanized monoclonal IgG1 against the RSV F protein. It binds the virus and prevents fusion and cell entry. It is given prophylactically to high-risk children (premature infants, chronic lung disease, hemodynamically significant congenital heart disease).⭐ Palivizumab = anti-F mAb for RSV prophylaxis.
  18. 18META humans: Human metapneumovirus (hMPV) is a negative-sense RNA virus in the Pneumoviridae family (genus Metapneumovirus).
  19. 19META human child: hMPV causes lower respiratory tract infection in children with symptoms similar to RSV: cough, rhinorrhea, wheezing, fever, and in severe cases bronchiolitis or pneumonia.
  20. 20Early hatching: Prematurity is a risk factor for severe hMPV infection.
  21. 21Grandfather clock + pendulungs: Asthma and other underlying respiratory conditions increase the risk of severe hMPV disease in children (bronchiolitis, pneumonia, hospitalization).
  22. 22META human adult: hMPV is a less common cause of community-acquired pneumonia in adults than in children but can still cause significant lower respiratory disease, especially in older adults and those with underlying conditions.
  23. 23Grandfather clock + pendulungs: In adults, hMPV can precipitate COPD exacerbations, with increased respiratory symptoms, hospitalization and morbidity (the pendulum-lungs icon marks chronic lung disease, here COPD).
Show the original legend picture

95. Rubella virus

Exam snapshot
  • Mild 3-day rash starting on face with posterior auricular lymphadenopathy +/- arthralgia; congenital rubella causes cataracts, deafness, PDA
  • Enveloped, positive-sense ssRNA, Matonaviridae (Rubivirus); single polyprotein cleaved by proteases
  • Diagnosis: rubella IgM / PCR; IgG avidity and seroconversion in pregnancy
  • Prevention with live attenuated MMR (12 months+); contraindicated in pregnancy and severe immunodeficiency
  • Buzzwords: German measles, 3-day measles, blueberry muffin baby, PDA, TORCH, Forchheimer spots
  1. 1Rubies: Rubella virus is the only member of the genus Rubivirus, in the family Matonaviridae (formerly classified with Togaviridae). Rubies = Rubella/Rubivirus, and the red color hints at the rash.
  2. 2Child emperor: Rubella is an important cause of a childhood exanthem (widespread skin rash), sometimes called German measles.
  3. 3Warm colors: Warm colors signal that rubella is an RNA virus.
  4. 4Positive sun: Rubella has a single-stranded positive-sense RNA genome, which can be translated directly by host ribosomes.
  5. 5Roll of tickets: The positive-sense RNA genome is translated into one long polyprotein (the roll of tickets), which viral and host proteases then cleave into the individual functional proteins.
  6. 6Nucleus-shaped dome in background: Positive-sense RNA viruses replicate in the cytoplasm, outside the nucleus; the genomic RNA is immediately available for translation by host ribosomes.
  7. 7Robe: Rubella virus is enveloped (the robe = envelope).
  8. 8Sneezing: Rubella spreads by respiratory droplets. It replicates in the nasopharynx during a 2-3 week incubation period (usually asymptomatic), spreads to lymph nodes, then enters the blood (viremia). A mild prodrome of low-grade fever, malaise, mild URI symptoms and tender lymphadenopathy precedes the rash.⭐ Forchheimer spots (petechiae on the soft palate) can occur.
  9. 9Spreading rubies: The rash is a maculopapular exanthem that starts on the face and spreads to the trunk and extremities, appearing after viremia as an immune-mediated reaction to viral antigens in small skin vessels. It spreads faster than measles, does not darken or coalesce, and resolves in about 3 days ('3-day measles').⭐ Rubella rash lasts about 3 days and does not coalesce.
  10. 10Beaded necklace behind ears: Tender lymphadenopathy, especially posterior auricular, suboccipital and posterior cervical nodes, results from the immune response to viral replication and often precedes the rash.⭐ Posterior auricular lymphadenopathy = rubella.
  11. 11Kneeling adult in pain: Adults, especially women, may develop arthralgias or arthritis, often of small joints (hands, wrists, knees), sometimes with mild fever and malaise.
  12. 12Empty baby carriage: Congenital rubella infection includes adverse pregnancy outcomes (miscarriage, stillbirth) as well as the congenital rubella syndrome in the live-born neonate. Risk and severity are highest with infection in the first trimester.⭐ Highest risk of CRS in the first trimester.
  13. 13Torches: Congenital rubella syndrome (CRS) is a TORCH infection, i.e., an infection acquired in utero: Toxoplasma, Other (syphilis, VZV, parvovirus B19), Rubella, CMV, HSV.
  14. 14Blueberry gemmed babies: CRS can cause the 'blueberry muffin rash': purplish, non-blanching nodules due to extramedullary hematopoiesis (dermal erythropoiesis) in response to fetal anemia.⭐ Blueberry muffin baby = rubella or CMV.
  15. 15Golden babies: CRS can cause neonatal jaundice and hyperbilirubinemia from fetal hepatocellular injury and cholestasis due to in utero infection.
  16. 16X'd out eyes: CRS causes congenital cataracts (opaque lens, white pupil) because in utero rubella infection disrupts development of the lens. Other ocular findings: corneal clouding, glaucoma and retinopathy ('salt-and-pepper' retinopathy).⭐ Cataracts are classic for CRS.
  17. 17Covering ears: CRS causes congenital bilateral sensorineural hearing loss from virus-induced damage to the developing cochlea and auditory neurons; this is the most common CRS manifestation.⭐ Sensorineural deafness is the most common CRS finding.
  18. 18Tiny head: CRS causes microcephaly (also meningoencephalitis, developmental delay and intellectual disability) from disruption of fetal brain development in early gestation.
  19. 19Ductus arteriosus: CRS causes patent ductus arteriosus (also pulmonary artery stenosis, aortic stenosis, ASD and VSD) from disruption of cardiac development in early gestation.⭐ Rubella = PDA (and pulmonary artery stenosis).
  20. 20LIVE puppet show: Active immunity to rubella is achieved with a live attenuated vaccine, which induces both humoral and cell-mediated immunity.
  21. 213 puppets: MMR is a trivalent live vaccine (measles, mumps, rubella); MMRV adds varicella. It is given to children from 12 months of age (two doses) and is contraindicated in pregnancy and severe immunocompromise.
  22. 22Pregnant puppet: MMR is contraindicated in pregnancy because it is a live attenuated vaccine that could theoretically infect the fetus and cause congenital rubella syndrome. Pregnancy should be avoided for 1 month after vaccination.⭐ Nonimmune pregnant women get MMR postpartum.
  23. 23Only 200 tickets: MMR is contraindicated in severe immunodeficiency (e.g., HIV with CD4 count below 200 cells/uL) because the live vaccine virus could replicate uncontrollably and cause serious disease.
Show the original legend picture

96. Salmonella Intro to Salmonella & NTS

Exam snapshot
  • Gram-negative, motile (peritrichous flagella), facultative intracellular rod; H2S producer (black colonies on Hektoen agar), non-lactose fermenter.
  • NTS (S. Enteritidis, S. Typhimurium) acquired from poultry, eggs, and reptiles/amphibians (turtles).
  • Usual presentation: self-limited gastroenteritis (fever, cramps, diarrhea); no antibiotics, supportive care only.
  • Bacteremia risk and osteomyelitis in sickle cell disease (also young infants, elderly, immunocompromised): treat with fluoroquinolone or ceftriaxone.
  • Invasion via M cells of Peyer's patches; low gastric acid (PPIs, antacids) lowers the infectious dose.
  1. 1Red rod salmon: Salmonella is a gram-negative rod. It is a non-lactose fermenter in the Enterobacterales and ferments glucose.
  2. 2Swimming salmon: Salmonella is motile via peritrichous flagella (H antigen), unlike Shigella, which is non-motile.⭐ Salmonella motile, Shigella non-motile.
  3. 3Fishbowl capsule: Salmonella produces a protective polysaccharide capsule (as given in the Sketchy picture). The best-known capsule is the Vi antigen of S. Typhi, which helps evade host defenses; most NTS lack it.
  4. 4Open cage: Salmonella is a facultative intracellular organism: it can survive and replicate inside host macrophages as well as outside cells.
  5. 5Salmo crossing M window: Invasive serotypes (all typhoidal and some NTS) cross the intestinal barrier via enterocytes and M cells, the specialized epithelial cells overlying lymphoid tissue of Peyer's patches.
  6. 6Lemons: Salmonella is acid-labile, so gastric acid is a major host defense. Low-acid states (PPIs, antacids, achlorhydria) reduce the infectious dose.
  7. 7Farting salmon: Salmonella produces hydrogen sulfide (H2S), which gives black colonies on certain media (e.g., hydrogen sulfide-detecting agars).
  8. 8Black plates: H2S production gives black colonies on Hektoen enteric agar, where Salmonella is distinguished from Shigella (colorless/green colonies, no H2S).
  9. 9"Thai Food": Typhoidal Salmonella (TS) serotypes are S. Typhi and S. Paratyphi, which cause enteric (typhoid) fever.
  10. 10"Auntie Rita's" Turmeric: Nontyphoidal Salmonella (NTS) comprises the many serotypes other than S. Typhi and S. Paratyphi; S. Enteritidis and S. Typhimurium are the most common.
  11. 11Smorgasbord: NTS causes foodborne and waterborne illness, spread through a wide variety of fresh and processed foods.
  12. 12Chicken and eggs: NTS is zoonotic; poultry (chicken, eggs) are the primary reservoir, so undercooked poultry and raw eggs are classic sources.
  13. 13Turtle handler: Animal contact, especially reptiles and amphibians (e.g., pet turtles), can transmit NTS.
  14. 14Queasy kid with flame bandana: The most common presentation of NTS is self-limited gastroenteritis: fever, nausea, vomiting, abdominal cramps, and diarrhea (can be bloody and inflammatory).
  15. 15Red river: NTS can cause extraintestinal bloodstream infection (bacteremia), leading to severe sepsis or death, especially in the immunocompromised, infants, and elderly.
  16. 16Aorta tree: Bacteremic NTS can disseminate, causing endovascular infection (e.g., infected aortic aneurysm/atherosclerotic plaques), meningitis, and osteomyelitis.
  17. 17Glowing brain tree: Disseminated NTS can seed the CNS and cause meningitis, particularly in infants and immunocompromised patients.
  18. 18Fish bones: Disseminated NTS can seed bone and cause osteomyelitis.
  19. 19Sickling guy: Osteomyelitis is a classic complication of invasive NTS in patients with sickle cell disease (functional asplenia) and other underlying conditions.⭐ Sickle cell + osteomyelitis: Salmonella and S. aureus.
  20. 20"Don't touch" Abx: NTS gastroenteritis is self-limited; manage with supportive care and hydration. Antibiotics are generally not indicated, and may prolong carriage.
  21. 21Flowers: Fluoroquinolones are the preferred antibiotic for NTS when treatment is indicated (to prevent invasive disease in high-risk patients and to treat invasive infection).
  22. 223 axes: Third-generation cephalosporins (e.g., ceftriaxone) are an alternative for NTS, used to prevent invasive disease when indicated and to treat invasive infection, including in children.
Show the original legend picture

97. Salmonella Typhoidal Salmonella

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: traveler to South-Central/East Asia or Southern Africa with stepwise fever, relative bradycardia, rose spots, and abdominal pain.
  • S. Typhi/Paratyphi: humans are the only reservoir; fecal-oral spread; chronic gallbladder carriers (Typhoid Mary).
  • Vi capsule blunts neutrophil response; bacteria multiply inside macrophages of Peyer's patches.
  • Week 3 complications: sepsis, hepatosplenomegaly, intestinal bleeding, and perforation.
  • Treatment: antibiotics (typically a fluoroquinolone; ceftriaxone or azithromycin where resistance exists).
  • Vaccines: oral live attenuated (Ty21a), injectable Vi polysaccharide, and Vi-conjugate (to tetanus toxoid) vaccine.
  1. 1Thai Food sign: S. Typhi and S. Paratyphi are the typhoidal Salmonella (TS) serotypes; they cause enteric (typhoid) fever in humans.
  2. 2Parrot: S. Typhi and S. Paratyphi are the typhoidal Salmonella (TS) serotypes, which cause enteric (typhoid) fever.
  3. 3"No animals" sign: Humans are the exclusive reservoir of TS; there is no animal reservoir, unlike NTS.⭐ Humans only: TS vs. poultry/reptiles for NTS.
  4. 4Outhouse: TS is acquired by consuming food or water contaminated with human feces (fecal-oral route), so poor sanitation drives spread.
  5. 5Parrots in papaya tree: TS can colonize the gallbladder, leading to a chronic, asymptomatic carrier state that sheds bacteria in feces and perpetuates transmission.⭐ Gallstones raise carrier risk; chronic carriage predisposes to gallbladder cancer.
  6. 6Thai mountains: TS predominantly occurs in developing countries with poor sanitation; endemic regions include South-Central and East Asia and Southern Africa.
  7. 7Vi-olet punch: TS possesses the Vi polysaccharide capsular antigen, which helps resist host defenses by blunting the neutrophilic response (inhibits neutrophil chemotaxis, opsonization, and oxidative burst).
  8. 8MacroCAGE: TS triggers a monocytic rather than neutrophilic response: monocytes/macrophages are recruited to sites harboring the bacteria, such as Peyer's patches.
  9. 9Caged parrots: TS survives and extensively proliferates within macrophages, which carry the organism through the reticuloendothelial system.
  10. 10Flaming lanterns: Fever is a classic early sign of TS infection and the hallmark of enteric fever.
  11. 11Stepwise rock island: Enteric fever begins with a stepwise rising fever that reaches a high plateau, typically over the first week.
  12. 12Fallen heart watch: Relative bradycardia (pulse lower than expected for the degree of fever, Faget sign) is a characteristic feature of enteric fever, typically developing in the first week.
  13. 13Red algae: Flat, rose-colored macules ("rose spots") may develop on the chest and abdomen, typically during the second week. Constipation, headache, and abdominal pain are also common.
  14. 14Dugong with liver and spleen spots: If untreated, sepsis, hepatosplenomegaly, and intestinal bleeding and perforation may develop in the third week.
  15. 15Leaking intestine engine: Peyer's patch necrosis from the monocytic infiltrate causes intestinal bleeding and perforation in the third week of untreated enteric fever.⭐ Typhoid perforation typically involves the terminal ileum.
  16. 16Flowers: Enteric fever requires antibiotic treatment, typically a fluoroquinolone (ceftriaxone or azithromycin where resistance is common).
  17. 17"LIVE music" sign + syringe mic: A live attenuated oral vaccine (Ty21a) protects against enteric fever. It is contraindicated in immunocompromised patients and cannot be given with concurrent antibiotics.
  18. 18Skull and Syringe shirt + Vi-olet punch: The Vi capsular polysaccharide vaccine is given by injection to protect against enteric fever; it is a purified polysaccharide vaccine (T-cell independent).
  19. 19Rhesus + Vi-olet punch: The conjugate vaccine (Vi capsular polysaccharide linked to tetanus toxoid protein) is given by injection and gives more robust, T-cell dependent protection against enteric fever.
Show the original legend picture

98. Scabies, Lice, Crabs

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: intensely itchy rash worse at night with finger-web burrows (scabies); itching with nits in clothing seams (body lice) or pubic hair (crabs)
  • Sarcoptes scabiei is a mite; Pediculus humanus corporis is body louse; Phthirus pubis is pubic louse
  • Dx: skin scraping (mite, eggs, feces) for scabies; visualize lice or nits
  • Treatment: topical permethrin (plus oral ivermectin for scabies); nit comb for crabs; hygiene and hot-washing clothes for body lice
  • Buzzwords: burrows, nocturnal pruritus, Norwegian (crusted) scabies, nits, maculae ceruleae
  1. 1Scabbard: Scabies (Sarcoptes scabiei) is a tiny parasitic mite that infects only humans.
  2. 2Tunnel: Scabies mites burrow under the skin to lay eggs, leaving visible burrow lines; larvae hatch and travel to the skin surface to molt.
  3. 3Dorm setting: Scabies spreads by prolonged skin-to-skin contact and is seen in crowded households and institutions (nursing homes, prisons).⭐ Close contacts should be treated at the same time.
  4. 4Camp Itch 'n Scratch: Scabies causes intense pruritus with excoriations, worst at night, from a delayed (type IV) hypersensitivity reaction to the mites, their eggs and fecal matter.⭐ Itching may persist for weeks after successful treatment.
  5. 5Nighttime setting: Scabies itching is most pronounced at night, from delayed hypersensitivity to mites, eggs and feces.
  6. 6Red accessories: Scabies commonly involves skin folds: finger webs, wrists, elbows, knees, waistline and buttocks. In infants, the head, palms and soles are more commonly involved.⭐ Spares the face in adults.
  7. 7Triangle hat: Scabies is diagnosed by seeing the triangular head of a mite at the end of a burrow, irregular burrow lines, or excoriated erythematous papules; skin scraping shows mites, eggs or feces.
  8. 8Sandwich crusts + Norwegian flag: Crusted (Norwegian) scabies occurs with a very high mite load or immunocompromise, causing thick crusted scales and cracking.⭐ Highly contagious; seen in HIV and transplant patients.
  9. 9Camp counselor Ivan: Scabies can be treated with oral ivermectin and/or topical permethrin; Norwegian scabies needs both.
  10. 10suPER flaMETHRower: Topical permethrin (applied neck down and washed off after 8-14 hours) treats scabies, and is combined with oral ivermectin for Norwegian scabies.⭐ Permethrin is first line; ivermectin is useful in outbreaks or crusted disease.
  11. 11Corporal outfit: Pediculosis corporis (Pediculus humanus corporis), the body louse, lives in clothing, lays eggs in the seams and feeds on the skin.⭐ The louse lives in clothes, not on the body.
  12. 12Seam of fabric: Body lice (Pediculus humanus corporis) lay their eggs in clothing seams and feed on human skin.
  13. 13Camp Itch 'n Scratch: Lice infestation causes pruritus and excoriations from local bites.
  14. 14Bunkhouse + stinky teenager: Body lice are associated with crowded living conditions and poor hygiene; lice can transmit epidemic typhus (Rickettsia prowazekii), relapsing fever (Borrelia recurrentis) and trench fever (Bartonella quintana).
  15. 15"Don't forget to BATHE! <3 mom": Improved hygiene and heat-washing of clothing and bedding treat pediculosis corporis.
  16. 16suPER flaMETHRower: For severe infestations, or if nits are found in body hair, permethrin should be used.
  17. 17Crab shorts: Pediculosis pubis (Phthirus pubis), pubic lice or crabs, is a translucent gray insect with claws that grip coarse hair; the nit is attached to the base of the hair shaft; found in pubic, axillary and facial hair and eyelashes.
  18. 18Hair in comb: Pubic lice (Phthirus pubis) grip coarse hair with claws, and nits attach at the base of the hair shaft; they are found in pubic, axillary and facial hair and eyelashes.
  19. 19Kiss: Pubic lice (crabs) are most commonly transmitted by sexual contact.⭐ Eyelash infestation in a child raises concern for sexual abuse.
  20. 20Blue polka dots: Blue-gray discoloration of bite sites (maculae ceruleae) can occur because crab saliva contains an anticoagulant.
  21. 21Camp Itch 'n Scratch: Crab infestation causes pruritus and excoriations from local bites.
  22. 22Glasses on hair: Crab lice or nits can be seen with the naked eye; a magnifying glass or dermatoscope is helpful.
  23. 23Comb: Treatment of crabs includes a nit comb (to remove nits) and topical permethrin.
  24. 24suPER flaMETHRower again: Treatment of crabs includes a nit comb and topical permethrin; treat sexual partners and wash clothing and bedding.
