99. Shigella spp

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: bloody (dysenteric) diarrhea with fever and abdominal cramps, with fecal leukocytes; very low infectious dose (10-100 organisms), spread person to person fecal-oral.
  • Gram-negative, non-motile, non-lactose-fermenting, non-H2S-producing rod (colorless/green colonies on Hektoen agar, unlike black Salmonella).
  • Invades M cells and spreads cell to cell via actin rockets; Type III secretion system injects effector proteins.
  • Shiga toxin (S. dysenteriae type 1) inactivates 60S ribosomal subunit (28S rRNA) and causes HUS (anemia, thrombocytopenia, AKI).
  • Treatment: fluoroquinolone or azithromycin (ceftriaxone), but mainly supportive; avoid antimotility drugs.
  • Buzzwords: day-care outbreaks, men who have sex with men, travelers, and HUS in children.
  1. 1She-gorilla: Shigella spp. are gram-negative bacilli and belong to the Enterobacterales; they are non-lactose fermenters.
  2. 2Red hues: Shigella spp. are gram-negative bacilli (red/pink on Gram stain).
  3. 3Green tutu: On Hektoen enteric agar, Shigella forms blue-green (colorless) colonies because it does not produce H2S. Salmonella makes black colonies on HE due to H2S.
  4. 4Chained she-gorilla: Shigella spp. are non-motile (no flagella), unlike Salmonella, which is motile.⭐ Shigella non-motile, Salmonella motile.
  5. 5Protected from acid: Shigella is relatively resistant to gastric acid, so very small inocula (10-100 organisms) can cause infection, which facilitates person-to-person spread.
  6. 6Motion tail: Shigella induces apoptosis in host cells and polymerizes host actin into tails ("actin rockets") to move through the cytoplasm and spread cell to cell, making it a facultative intracellular bacterium.
  7. 7"M" pad: Shigella invades M cells overlying Peyer's patches of the gut mucosa, then infects the colonic epithelium from the basolateral side.
  8. 8Red stool: The main sign of shigellosis is diarrhea, which is often bloody (dysentery), usually with fever and abdominal cramps.
  9. 9Inflammatory flames: Shigellosis diarrhea is inflammatory, with leukocytes (and often blood) in stool, reflecting invasion of the colonic mucosa.
  10. 10Glomerular knot: In rare cases, Shiga toxin from S. dysenteriae type 1 causes hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS) by damaging endothelial cells of glomerular capillaries. This triggers platelet aggregation, causing thrombocytopenia, microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA), and acute kidney injury.⭐ HUS triad: MAHA, thrombocytopenia, AKI.
  11. 11Popped red balloon: In MAHA (part of HUS), red blood cells are mechanically sheared into schistocytes as they pass through small vessels occluded by platelet-rich clots, lowering the RBC count.
  12. 12Aggregation of plates: In HUS, platelet aggregation in damaged glomerular capillaries consumes platelets (thrombocytopenia) and impairs renal function, causing acute kidney injury.
  13. 13Young ringmaster: HUS occurs more commonly in children under 5 years of age.
  14. 1460S sand timer: Shiga toxin of S. dysenteriae (similar to the Shiga-like toxin of enterohemorrhagic E. coli O157:H7) inactivates the 60S ribosomal subunit by removing an adenine from 28S rRNA, inhibiting protein synthesis.
  15. 15Turkey baster: Shigella has a type III secretion system, a needle-like apparatus that injects effector proteins into host cells, helping the bacteria invade, evade immune responses, and survive intracellularly.
Show the original legend picture

100. Smallpox / Cowpox virus

Exam snapshot
  • Smallpox: fever then synchronous deep pustular rash; molluscum: umbilicated flesh-colored dome papules; cowpox: pustule on hands after cat/cow contact
  • Large, brick-shaped, enveloped dsDNA viruses (Poxviridae) that replicate in the cytoplasm with their own DNA-dependent RNA polymerase
  • Diagnosis: Guarnieri bodies (cytoplasmic inclusions), PCR, EM; molluscum is clinical (molluscum/Henderson-Paterson bodies on biopsy)
  • Smallpox was eradicated in 1980 using vaccinia vaccination; molluscum resolves spontaneously or with curettage/cryotherapy
  • Buzzwords: Guarnieri bodies, umbilicated lesions, largest DNA virus, cytoplasmic replication
  1. 1Boxes: The boxes represent poxviruses (Poxviridae): smallpox (variola) virus, cowpox virus, and molluscum contagiosum virus. Poxviruses are brick/box-shaped.
  2. 2Cool colors: Cool colors indicate DNA viruses: poxviruses are large, complex double-stranded DNA viruses.
  3. 3Workers preparing envelopes: Poxviruses are enveloped; they acquire their envelope by wrapping in membranes derived from host intracellular membranes (e.g., Golgi/ER-derived).
  4. 4Prepackaged boxes: Each poxvirion is pre-packaged with the enzymes needed for replication, including a DNA-dependent RNA polymerase, because it replicates without the host nucleus.⭐ Poxviruses carry their own DNA-dependent RNA polymerase.
  5. 5Conveyor belt: The conveyor belt = viral machinery producing mRNA: the virion's own DNA-dependent RNA polymerase (and other enzymes, such as those for capping and polyadenylation) transcribes viral genes in the cytoplasm.
  6. 6Circled Headquarters (HQ) on map: The circled HQ is the host nucleus. Unlike most DNA viruses, poxviruses do not replicate in the nucleus; they use their own enzymes and bypass host nuclear machinery.⭐ Poxvirus and the other exception: replicates in cytoplasm (most DNA viruses use the nucleus).
  7. 7Locations outside HQ: Locations outside the HQ represent the cytoplasm, where poxviruses exclusively replicate and assemble in 'viral factories'.
  8. 8"Guarnieri's shipping": Guarnieri bodies are cytoplasmic, eosinophilic (pink) inclusion bodies seen in cells infected with poxviruses, classically variola (smallpox) and vaccinia. They are round or oval, dense pink structures on light microscopy and mark sites of viral replication.⭐ Guarnieri bodies = cytoplasmic inclusions in smallpox/vaccinia.
  9. 9Red "G"s outside HQ: The red 'G's mark Guarnieri bodies outside the nucleus (HQ), emphasizing that these inclusions are cytoplasmic and eosinophilic (reddish-pink on H&E).
  10. 10Dumbbells: Poxviruses are brick- or oval-shaped with a distinctive dumbbell-shaped core containing the dsDNA genome and enzymes, flanked by two lateral bodies.
  11. 11"World's largest": Poxviruses are the largest DNA viruses and among the largest viruses overall (visible even by light microscopy as elementary bodies).⭐ Largest DNA virus = poxvirus.
  12. 12"Small boxes": Smallpox (variola virus) is a febrile illness with a synchronous pustular rash. It spreads by respiratory droplets and direct contact with lesion material. The disease was eradicated in 1980; there is a vaccine (live vaccinia).⭐ Smallpox is eradicated; the vaccine used live vaccinia (cowpox-related) virus.
  13. 13"Same day shipping": 'Same day' = synchronous lesions: smallpox lesions are deep, firm, well-circumscribed, often umbilicated, and all at the same stage of development. In chickenpox (varicella), lesions are superficial vesicles appearing in crops, so macules, papules, vesicles and crusts are seen together.⭐ Smallpox: same stage, centrifugal (face/extremities). Varicella: different stages, centripetal.
  14. 14Udder: Cowpox virus causes localized pustular skin lesions, often on the hands or arms, with possible fever and lymphadenopathy, acquired through contact with infected animals.
  15. 15Material from udder dispenser: Historically cowpox was acquired from contact with infected cow udders (milkmaids). Today, transmission from domestic cats (and rodent reservoirs) is more common. Cowpox exposure protected milkmaids against smallpox, leading to Jenner's vaccine.⭐ Jenner used cowpox to protect against smallpox.
  16. 16Snail: Molluscum contagiosum virus causes small, firm, umbilicated, flesh-colored papules on the skin, spread by direct contact or contaminated objects.
  17. 17"Snail mail" + dome shell: Molluscum lesions are smooth, dome-shaped, flesh-colored papules (2-5 mm) with central umbilication. They are firm and painless, may occur in clusters, and are common on the trunk, limbs (antecubital and popliteal fossae) and genital area. Histology shows molluscum (Henderson-Paterson) bodies.⭐ Dome-shaped umbilicated papules = molluscum contagiosum.
  18. 18Kid with snail stamps on trunk: In children, molluscum presents as clustered umbilicated lesions on the trunk and limbs, acquired by skin-to-skin contact or fomites (towels, clothing, toys, shared baths).
  19. 19Adult with immunocompromised cane: In adults, widespread or persistent molluscum, especially with large or facial lesions, suggests immunodeficiency (e.g., HIV). The virus is acquired by skin contact or sexual transmission (genital molluscum).⭐ Extensive molluscum in an adult: test for HIV.
Show the original legend picture

101. Sporothrix schenckii

Exam snapshot
  • Rose gardener with a thorn prick developing nodules and ulcers that ascend the arm along lymphatics.
  • Thermally dimorphic fungus: mold at 25-30 C, yeast at 35-37 C; found in soil, plants, thorns, sphagnum moss.
  • Biopsy or culture; yeast phase shows cigar-shaped budding yeast; mold phase shows rosette (daisy-like) conidia.
  • Itraconazole for cutaneous and lymphocutaneous disease; amphotericin B for severe or disseminated disease; SSKI is historical.
  • Rose gardener's disease, lymphocutaneous spread, cigar-shaped yeast, rosette conidia.
  1. 1Shanked by a thorn: Sporothrix schenckii is a dimorphic fungus that causes cutaneous (subcutaneous) mycosis after traumatic inoculation.
  2. 2Garden: S. schenckii lives in soil, plant debris, tree bark, and thorny vegetation such as rose bushes and sphagnum moss.
  3. 3Dimorphic butterfly: S. schenckii is thermally dimorphic: mold in cooler environments (25-30 C) and yeast at body temperature (35-37 C).⭐ Mold in the cold, yeast in the heat.
  4. 4Roses: Sporotrichosis is called "rose gardener's disease" because infection follows a prick from thorns or other contaminated plant material.⭐ Rose gardener's disease.
  5. 5Branching rose stems: The mold phase has septate, branching hyphae with small round conidia in rosette or daisy-like clusters at the tips of conidiophores (spore-bearing stalks).
  6. 6Cigar: The yeast phase appears as small oval to elongated, cigar-shaped yeast cells with narrow-based budding, often surrounded by histiocytes, multinucleated giant cells, and granulomatous inflammation.⭐ Cigar-shaped yeast.
  7. 7Thorny vine around arm: Sporotrichosis typically follows traumatic inoculation of contaminated plant matter or soil, causing subcutaneous nodules and ulcers.
  8. 8Ascending vines: In lymphocutaneous sporotrichosis, the fungus ascends from the inoculation site along lymphatics, forming sequential subcutaneous nodules that may enlarge, become tender, and ulcerate, usually on the hands or forearms.⭐ Ascending nodular lymphangitis after a thorn prick.
  9. 9Roses on vines on arm: Same point: nodules arranged along the lymphatic channel of the arm (lymphocutaneous sporotrichosis).
  10. 10Pinecones: Oral itraconazole is the preferred treatment for cutaneous and lymphocutaneous sporotrichosis.⭐ Severe or disseminated disease needs amphotericin B.
  11. 11Potassium iodide pesticide: A saturated solution of potassium iodide (SSKI) was historically used for sporotrichosis; modern antifungals (itraconazole) are now preferred.
Show the original legend picture

102. Staphylococcus aureus

Exam snapshot
  • Classic: abscesses, impetigo, post-influenza pneumonia, acute endocarditis (IVDU, tricuspid), septic arthritis and osteomyelitis
  • Gram-positive cocci in clusters; catalase +, coagulase + (distinguishes from S. epidermidis/saprophyticus), golden pigment, beta-hemolytic, ferments mannitol
  • Toxins: TSST-1 (superantigen, toxic shock syndrome), exfoliative toxin (scalded skin), preformed enterotoxin (food poisoning in 1-6 h)
  • Protein A binds Fc of IgG, blocking opsonization and phagocytosis; nasal carriage is the reservoir
  • Treatment: nafcillin/oxacillin for MSSA; vancomycin for MRSA (altered PBP, mecA gene)
  1. 1Golden staff: Staphylococcus aureus is a gram-positive coccus that grows in grape-like clusters; its colonies are golden because of staphyloxanthin pigment.
  2. 2Purple robe: S. aureus is a gram-positive coccus (purple on Gram stain), seen in clusters.
  3. 3Catalase cat: S. aureus is catalase positive: catalase converts hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen (bubbles on testing). Staphylococci are catalase +, whereas streptococci are catalase -.
  4. 4Coagulated river: S. aureus is coagulase positive: coagulase converts fibrinogen to fibrin, forming a clot in plasma. This distinguishes it from other catalase-positive, coagulase-negative staphylococci (S. epidermidis, S. saprophyticus).⭐ Coagulase + = S. aureus.
  5. 5Beta light bulb: Most strains are beta-hemolytic: complete lysis of red cells gives a clear zone around colonies on blood agar.
  6. 6Tall man in yellow: S. aureus ferments mannitol, turning mannitol salt agar yellow. The high-salt medium selects for staphylococci, and mannitol fermentation distinguishes S. aureus from non-pathogenic staphylococci, which usually do not ferment mannitol.
  7. 7"A" bound to IgG: Protein A is a key virulence factor that binds the Fc region of IgG, which prevents opsonization, complement activation, and phagocytosis.
  8. 8Nose: S. aureus is normal flora of the anterior nares and skin; nasal carriage is a source of autoinfection and of spread (e.g., in healthcare workers).
  9. 9Coughing + patchy cloth: S. aureus pneumonia shows patchy infiltrates on chest X-ray that can progress to lobar consolidation, and may cause abscesses, cavities, or empyema.
  10. 10Icosahedron viral lanterns: S. aureus pneumonia classically follows a viral respiratory infection, especially influenza (viral damage to the airway epithelium predisposes to bacterial superinfection).⭐ Post-influenza pneumonia: S. aureus (also S. pneumoniae).
  11. 11Joint bandages: S. aureus is the most common cause of septic arthritis in adults (N. gonorrhoeae is common in sexually active young adults). It presents as an acutely warm, swollen, tender joint, with purulent synovial fluid on aspiration.
  12. 12Inflamed camel humps: S. aureus causes skin and soft tissue infections: abscesses, impetigo, folliculitis, furuncles, carbuncles, and cellulitis.
  13. 13Hearts on running camel: S. aureus is the most common cause of acute infective endocarditis, with rapid onset of fever, chills, tachycardia, and sepsis, and it can infect previously normal valves because of its virulence.
  14. 14Three pyramids + mortar and pestle: Tricuspid valve endocarditis caused by S. aureus is strongly associated with IV drug use. It causes septic pulmonary emboli (multiple lung nodules/cavitations).⭐ IVDU + endocarditis = tricuspid valve, S. aureus.
  15. 15Fish bones: S. aureus is the leading cause of osteomyelitis (bone infection), commonly from hematogenous spread or direct inoculation. (Salmonella is classic in sickle cell disease.)
  16. 16Exfoliative burn: Exfoliative toxin (a protease) cleaves desmoglein-1 in the stratum granulosum, causing staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome with widespread skin peeling, mostly in infants and young children. Nikolsky sign is positive.
  17. 17Superhero cape + bolt: TSST-1 is a superantigen that cross-links MHC II on antigen-presenting cells with the T-cell receptor outside the antigen groove, causing massive polyclonal T-cell activation and cytokine release. It causes toxic shock syndrome: high fever, diffuse desquamating rash, hypotension/shock, and multi-organ involvement (classically tampon use or surgical packing).
  18. 18Rapid camel running + nausea: Ingestion of preformed, heat-stable enterotoxin causes food poisoning with rapid onset (Sketchy: ~30 min to 8 h; classically 1-6 h): abrupt nausea, vomiting, and cramps. It is self-limited and non-febrile, and there is usually little or no diarrhea.
  19. 19Mayo and meats: Foods contaminated by food handlers and left at room temperature, such as mayonnaise-based dishes, custards, and meats, are classic sources of staphylococcal food poisoning. Reheating does not help because the toxin is heat-stable.⭐ Short incubation + mayo/custard/potato salad = S. aureus.
  20. 20Merciful pharaoh: Some strains are methicillin-resistant (MRSA), which is resistant to all beta-lactams except newer anti-MRSA cephalosporins. MRSA is a major cause of both hospital- and community-acquired infection.
  21. 21Altered builders: MRSA carries the mecA gene, which encodes an altered penicillin-binding protein (PBP2a) with low affinity for beta-lactams, so cell-wall synthesis proceeds despite the drug.
  22. 22Purple caravan: Vancomycin is the drug of choice for serious MRSA infections. Alternatives include linezolid, daptomycin (not for pneumonia), and ceftaroline.
  23. 23Pencil staff: Nafcillin (or oxacillin, dicloxacillin) is a penicillinase-resistant beta-lactam used for methicillin-sensitive S. aureus; it is effective because it resists staphylococcal beta-lactamase.
Show the original legend picture

103. Staphylococcus epidermidis & saprophyticus

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: S. epidermidis - prosthetic valve/joint or catheter infection; S. saprophyticus - UTI in a young sexually active woman.
  • Micro: gram-positive cocci in clusters, catalase positive, coagulase negative; S. epidermidis is novobiocin sensitive, S. saprophyticus is novobiocin resistant.
  • Diagnosis: culture; repeated positive blood cultures for epidermidis; urinalysis and culture for saprophyticus.
  • Treatment/prevention: S. epidermidis - vancomycin (often methicillin resistant), remove device; S. saprophyticus - TMP-SMX or nitrofurantoin.
  • Buzzwords: biofilm, blood culture contaminant, honeymoon cystitis, novobiocin, coagulase negative.
  1. 1Plumber: Staphylococcus epidermidis is a gram-positive coccus.
  2. 2Sappy couple: Staphylococcus saprophyticus is a gram-positive coccus.
  3. 3Purple hues: S. epidermidis and S. saprophyticus are gram-positive cocci (purple = gram-positive).
  4. 4Catalase cat: Both produce catalase (catalase positive), an enzyme that converts hydrogen peroxide to water and oxygen.⭐ Catalase positive separates staphylococci from streptococci.
  5. 5Runny juice: Both are coagulase negative (do not convert fibrinogen to fibrin, so no clot); the coagulase test distinguishes them from coagulase-positive S. aureus.⭐ They are the "coagulase-negative staphylococci".
  6. 6Ammonia spray: Both produce urease (urease positive), hydrolyzing urea into carbon dioxide and ammonia.
  7. 7Plumber's exposed navel: S. epidermidis is susceptible to novobiocin, whereas S. saprophyticus is resistant.⭐ Saprophyticus = resistant ("Sapro = Stubborn to novobiocin").
  8. 8Dirty hands: S. epidermidis is part of the normal flora of skin and mucous membranes.
  9. 9Petri dish pan: As normal skin flora, S. epidermidis is a common contaminant of blood cultures.⭐ Multiple positive cultures suggest true infection rather than contamination.
  10. 10Plumbing joints: S. epidermidis is one of the most frequently isolated pathogens in prosthetic joint infections.
  11. 11Tubing: S. epidermidis is a leading cause of catheter-associated (intravascular device) infections.
  12. 12Heart valve: S. epidermidis is the most common cause of prosthetic valve endocarditis (early, within about 2 months of surgery).
  13. 13Gunk on pipes and tubes: S. epidermidis produces biofilm (extracellular polysaccharide slime), allowing adherence to prosthetic devices and IV catheters.⭐ Biofilm is the key virulence factor and protects against antibiotics.
  14. 14Van: Vancomycin is the drug of choice for S. epidermidis because most strains are resistant to nafcillin and methicillin (mecA); biofilm formation further impairs antibiotic penetration.⭐ Often requires device removal as well.
  15. 15Honeymooner: S. saprophyticus commonly causes uncomplicated urinary tract infection in young, sexually active women (honeymoon cystitis).⭐ Second most common cause of UTI in this group.
  16. 16Bladder cup: S. saprophyticus is an important cause of uncomplicated UTI, second after E. coli (E. coli and Klebsiella are also frequent causes).⭐ Suspect in a UTI that is nitrite negative, urease positive with alkaline urine.
  17. 17Inflamed prostate fruit: S. saprophyticus has been reported as a cause of acute bacterial prostatitis (uncommon; E. coli is far more common).
Show the original legend picture

104. Streptococcus agalactiae (Group B Strep)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: neonate with sepsis, pneumonia or meningitis in the first days to weeks of life; mother colonized in the vagina or rectum.
  • Micro: gram-positive cocci in chains, beta-hemolytic, group B antigen, encapsulated; bacitracin resistant, PYR negative, hippurate positive, CAMP positive.
  • Diagnosis: CAMP test, hippurate hydrolysis; vaginal-rectal culture at 36-37+6 weeks.
  • Treatment/prevention: intrapartum IV penicillin (or ampicillin) prophylaxis for colonized mothers.
  • Buzzwords: neonatal meningitis and sepsis, CAMP factor, arrowhead hemolysis, hippurate positive, bacitracin resistant.
  1. 1Galactic baby: Streptococcus agalactiae (group B strep) is a gram-positive coccus.
  2. 2Purple hues: S. agalactiae is a gram-positive coccus (purple = gram-positive).
  3. 3Hippo: S. agalactiae is hippurate hydrolysis positive; this helps differentiate it from other streptococci such as S. pyogenes, which is hippurate negative.
  4. 4Glass helmet: S. agalactiae is surrounded by a polysaccharide capsule, an important virulence factor.⭐ The capsule helps evade phagocytosis in neonates.
  5. 5CAMP tent + golden staff: When S. agalactiae and S. aureus are grown near each other on blood agar, the CAMP factor produced by S. agalactiae enhances the hemolysis caused by S. aureus, producing an "arrowhead" zone of hemolysis; the CAMP test differentiates S. agalactiae from other streptococci.⭐ CAMP positive = group B strep.
  6. 6Beta light bulb: S. agalactiae is beta-hemolytic (complete lysis of red cells, clear zones around colonies on blood agar).
  7. 7Inhibited basset hound: S. agalactiae is bacitracin resistant and PYR negative, distinguishing it from S. pyogenes (bacitracin sensitive, PYR positive).⭐ Group A = Bacitracin sensitive; group B = Bacitracin resistant.
  8. 8Meningitis helmet: S. agalactiae is a leading cause of neonatal bacterial meningitis.
  9. 9Red onesie: S. agalactiae is one of the most common causes of neonatal sepsis.
  10. 10Coughing baby: Infected neonates can present with pneumonia (respiratory distress soon after birth).
  11. 11Red arrow: The CAMP test produces a characteristic arrowhead zone of enhanced hemolysis where S. agalactiae meets S. aureus on blood agar; used to identify S. agalactiae.
  12. 12"35 wk": Routine screening for S. agalactiae colonization is recommended for pregnant women at 36-37 weeks of gestation (older teaching: 35-37 weeks) because it can pass to the neonate in the birth canal.⭐ Screen with a vaginal-rectal swab culture.
  13. 13Purple pencil in mothership: If screening is positive, intravenous penicillin is given intrapartum to prevent neonatal infection.⭐ Penicillin G (or ampicillin) is the standard intrapartum prophylaxis.
Show the original legend picture

105. Streptococcus gallolyticus (Group D Strep)

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: older adult with subacute endocarditis or bacteremia; underlying colon cancer or polyps.
  • Micro: gram-positive cocci in chains or pairs, group D, bile tolerant, nonhemolytic (gamma), formerly S. bovis.
  • Diagnosis: blood cultures; colonoscopy is required after S. gallolyticus bacteremia or endocarditis.
  • Treatment/prevention: penicillin, ceftriaxone or vancomycin; evaluate for colorectal neoplasia.
  • Buzzwords: S. bovis, colon cancer, endocarditis, bile esculin, group D.
  1. 1Galloping cow: Streptococcus bovis/equinus complex (SBSEC) has been reclassified, with S. gallolyticus (formerly S. bovis biotype I) the clinically important member.
  2. 2Big D ears: S. gallolyticus is a group D streptococcus.
  3. 3Purple moon: S. gallolyticus is a gram-positive coccus (purple = gram-positive).
  4. 4Chains & pairs of planets: S. gallolyticus appears as gram-positive cocci in chains or pairs.
  5. 5Glass helmet: S. gallolyticus is encapsulated.
  6. 6Gamma aurora: S. gallolyticus is gamma hemolytic (nonhemolytic, no hemolysis on blood agar; it still grows).⭐ Nonhemolytic, unlike group A and B strep.
  7. 7Liver + bile asteroid: S. gallolyticus grows in bile salt media (bile tolerant, as in bile esculin agar), which aids survival in the GI tract and lab identification.⭐ Group D strep: bile esculin positive; Enterococcus also grows in 6.5% NaCl, S. gallolyticus does not.
  8. 8Moon moss: S. gallolyticus forms biofilms on collagen-rich surfaces such as damaged heart valves.
  9. 9Colon milky way: S. gallolyticus is part of the normal GI flora of some animals and humans.
  10. 10Heart lantern: S. gallolyticus can cause bacteremia and endocarditis.⭐ Endocarditis with S. gallolyticus should prompt a colonoscopy.
  11. 11Crab constellation: There is a strong association between S. gallolyticus infection and colorectal neoplasia (colon cancer).⭐ Bacteremia with S. bovis = look for colon cancer.
  12. 12Squid: S. gallolyticus is sensitive to third-generation cephalosporins (e.g. ceftriaxone), vancomycin and penicillin.
  13. 13Van: Vancomycin is an effective treatment (S. gallolyticus is sensitive to it, as to penicillin and ceftriaxone).
  14. 14Purple pencil rocket: Penicillin (PCN) is an effective treatment for S. gallolyticus, which is also sensitive to third-generation cephalosporins and vancomycin.
Show the original legend picture

106. Streptococcus pneumoniae

Exam snapshot
  • Lobar pneumonia with rusty sputum, meningitis, otitis media, sinusitis (MOPS); viridans: subacute endocarditis after dental work
  • S. pneumoniae: alpha-hemolytic, lancet-shaped diplococci, encapsulated, optochin-sensitive, bile-soluble; viridans: optochin-resistant, bile-insoluble
  • Diagnosis: Gram stain, culture, urinary antigen, Quellung (capsule swelling)
  • Treatment: amoxicillin/penicillin, ceftriaxone +/- vancomycin for resistant strains/meningitis, macrolides as alternatives; vaccinate with PCV and PPSV23
  • Buzzwords: MOPS, rusty sputum, lancet-shaped diplococci, optochin, IgA protease, sickle cell, mitral valve endocarditis, dextrans
  1. 1Sir Pneumo: Streptococcus pneumoniae (pneumococcus), a gram-positive coccus; the knight 'Sir Pneumo' is the main character.
  2. 2Purple hues: Purple = gram-positive. Both S. pneumoniae and the viridans group streptococci are gram-positive cocci.
  3. 3"A knight tournament": Both are alpha-hemolytic ('A' knight): partial hemolysis with oxidation of hemoglobin to methemoglobin gives a greenish discoloration around colonies on blood agar.⭐ Alpha-hemolytic = partial hemolysis, green zone.
  4. 4Sir Pneumo's armor: S. pneumoniae has a polysaccharide capsule, its major virulence factor; it protects against phagocytosis. The capsule is detected by the Quellung reaction (swelling with specific antiserum).⭐ Encapsulated organism: asplenic patients are at risk.
  5. 5Sensitive chin: S. pneumoniae is sensitive to optochin (ethylhydrocupreine), which inhibits growth on agar and distinguishes it from viridans streptococci.⭐ Optochin-sensitive = pneumococcus.
  6. 6Double lances: S. pneumoniae appears as lancet-shaped (pointed-end) gram-positive diplococci on Gram stain.
  7. 7No bile boots: S. pneumoniae is bile-soluble: bile acids (deoxycholate) lyse the organism via autolysis, so colonies disappear when bile is added (it cannot survive bile exposure).
  8. 8One sided rusty chest armor: S. pneumoniae is the most common cause of community-acquired lobar pneumonia, with rust-colored sputum (from blood/hemolysis) and consolidation of a single lobe.⭐ Rust-colored sputum = pneumococcal pneumonia.
  9. 9MOPS: S. pneumoniae is a leading cause of Meningitis (most common cause in adults), Otitis media, Pneumonia, and Sinusitis: MOPS.⭐ MOPS: Meningitis, Otitis media, Pneumonia, Sinusitis.
  10. 10#1 sign: The '#1' reinforces that S. pneumoniae is the number one cause of these infections (MOPS), including bacterial meningitis in adults and community-acquired pneumonia.
  11. 11IgA shield: S. pneumoniae produces IgA protease, which cleaves secretory IgA on mucosal surfaces and thereby promotes colonization of the nasopharynx.⭐ IgA protease: S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae, Neisseria.
  12. 12Sickle: Patients with sickle cell disease (functional asplenia from autosplenectomy) are highly susceptible to infection with encapsulated bacteria such as S. pneumoniae; vaccination and penicillin prophylaxis are required.⭐ Sickle cell: Streptococcus pneumoniae sepsis and osteomyelitis with Salmonella.
  13. 13Macrolide crow: Penicillin or amoxicillin is first-line for susceptible pneumococci; macrolides (azithromycin, clarithromycin) are alternatives, although macrolide resistance is increasing.
  14. 143 axes flag: For pneumococci with reduced penicillin susceptibility (and for meningitis) a third-generation cephalosporin such as ceftriaxone is used, often with vancomycin in meningitis. Penicillin resistance is due to altered penicillin-binding proteins.⭐ Pneumococcal meningitis empiric therapy: ceftriaxone + vancomycin.
  15. 15"Adults in the Mezzanine, Children on the Ground" vaccine sign: Two pneumococcal vaccines exist. PPSV23 (pure capsular polysaccharide, used in adults) is a T-cell-independent response producing short-lived IgM with little memory. PCV (conjugate vaccine, children and certain at-risk adults) links polysaccharide to a protein carrier, producing a T-cell-dependent response with IgG, memory and long-lived immunity. The CDC recommends PCV for all young children, at-risk persons and older adults.⭐ Conjugate vaccine = T-cell dependent, works in infants.
  16. 16Green jester: Viridans group streptococci ('viridis' = green) are gram-positive cocci that are alpha-hemolytic.
  17. 17No armor: Viridans streptococci are not encapsulated.
  18. 18Resistant chin: Viridans streptococci are optochin-resistant, which distinguishes them from S. pneumoniae.⭐ Optochin-resistant alpha-hemolytic strep = viridans.
  19. 19Protective bile boots: Viridans streptococci are bile-insoluble: their colonies persist after bile is added, unlike S. pneumoniae.
  20. 20Yellow teeth: Streptococcus mutans is a viridans streptococcus that is part of normal oral flora and plays a major role in dental caries by producing acid from sugars.⭐ S. mutans = dental caries.
  21. 21Damaged mitre hat: Viridans streptococci are a common cause of (subacute) infective endocarditis on previously damaged valves, such as in mitral valve prolapse or rheumatic heart disease; the mitre hat = mitral valve.⭐ Viridans group (S. sanguinis) = subacute endocarditis on damaged valves after dental work.
  22. 22Plate shield: Viridans streptococci make dextrans (insoluble glucans from sucrose by glucosyltransferase) that adhere to tooth enamel and form dental plaque.
  23. 23Deck of cards: The same dextrans let viridans streptococci stick to fibrin-platelet aggregates (the stacked cards = platelets/fibrin thrombi) on damaged heart valves, leading to vegetations and infective endocarditis after transient bacteremia (e.g., dental procedures).
Show the original legend picture

107. Streptococcus pyogenes

Exam snapshot
  • Pharyngitis (strep throat), impetigo, cellulitis/erysipelas, scarlet fever, necrotizing fasciitis, TSS; later rheumatic fever and PSGN
  • Gram-positive cocci in chains, beta-hemolytic, catalase-negative, group A (bacitracin-sensitive, PYR-positive); M protein, hyaluronic acid capsule
  • Diagnosis: rapid antigen test, throat culture, ASO/anti-DNase B titers for sequelae
  • Penicillin treats pharyngitis and prevents rheumatic fever (not PSGN); clindamycin added for toxin-mediated disease
  • Buzzwords: M protein, honey-crusted, strawberry tongue, sandpaper rash, Jones criteria, cola-colored urine, ASO
  1. 1Pie bakers: Streptococcus pyogenes (group A streptococcus, GAS), a gram-positive coccus; 'pie' = pyogenes.
  2. 2Purple stripes: Purple = gram-positive; the stripes/chain pattern reflects that streptococci grow as cocci in chains.
  3. 3"Hot Apple" pie + glass capsule: S. pyogenes has a hyaluronic acid capsule, which is poorly immunogenic (resembles host connective tissue) and helps it evade immune defenses.
  4. 4Beta light bulb: S. pyogenes is beta-hemolytic: complete lysis of red cells around colonies on blood agar (clear zone), mediated by streptolysins.⭐ Beta-hemolytic, catalase-negative, bacitracin-sensitive = GAS.
  5. 5Flaky pie crust: S. pyogenes can cause impetigo, a superficial skin infection with 'honey-crusted' (golden) lesions around the nose and mouth (S. aureus can also cause it).⭐ Honey-crusted lesions = impetigo.
  6. 6Red neckerchief on pie chef: The red neckerchief marks the throat: S. pyogenes is the most common bacterial cause of pharyngitis ('strep throat').⭐ Strep throat: no cough, tonsillar exudates, tender cervical nodes (Centor criteria).
  7. 7Red puffy gloves: S. pyogenes causes skin and soft tissue infections: impetigo, cellulitis, erysipelas (raised, sharply demarcated, painful), and necrotizing fasciitis.
  8. 8Licking strawberry: Scarlet fever, caused by pyrogenic exotoxin-producing S. pyogenes, features a strawberry tongue (red swollen tongue after the white coating peels, with prominent papillae). Here the strawberry represents the tongue finding.
  9. 9Red neckerchief on cookie chef: The neckerchief again marks pharyngitis: scarlet fever presents together with pharyngitis (sore, red throat) from the exotoxin-producing strain.
  10. 10Red frosting + not on hands and feet: The red frosting is the scarlet fever rash: a diffuse, finely papular erythematous 'sandpaper' rash that desquamates and typically spares the palms and soles (not on hands and feet). It starts on the neck/trunk, with circumoral pallor and accentuation in skin folds (Pastia lines).⭐ Scarlet fever rash = sandpaper, spares palms/soles.
  11. 11Superhero cape + bolt: Pyrogenic exotoxins (SpeA, SpeC) are superantigens: they bind MHC II and the T-cell receptor outside the antigen site and activate many T cells, causing a cytokine storm. This leads to streptococcal toxic shock syndrome (fever, rash, hypotension, multiorgan failure) and scarlet fever; the toxin gene is phage-encoded.⭐ Superantigens: GAS pyrogenic exotoxins and S. aureus TSST-1.
  12. 12"B" + necrotic cookie: Some strains produce SpeB, a cysteine protease that causes rapid tissue destruction in necrotizing fasciitis ('flesh-eating' infection).⭐ Necrotizing fasciitis: severe pain, rapid spread; needs surgical debridement.
  13. 13"M" on hat: The M protein is a surface virulence factor with several roles in pathogenesis; it is the basis for strain typing (M types).
  14. 14Inhibiting eating: The M protein confers resistance to phagocytosis (it binds factor H and blocks complement C3b deposition).⭐ M protein = antiphagocytic.
  15. 15Mitre hat: Antibodies to M protein cross-react with human cardiac and other tissue proteins (molecular mimicry), causing rheumatic heart disease. The mitre hat = mitral valve, the most commonly affected valve.⭐ Rheumatic fever is a type II hypersensitivity (molecular mimicry).
  16. 16Red neckerchief on master chef: Acute rheumatic fever is a complication of inadequately treated S. pyogenes pharyngitis (not skin infection), appearing 2-4 weeks later.
  17. 17"JONES" cupcakes: The Jones major criteria for acute rheumatic fever (JONES): J = Joints (migratory polyarthritis), O = heart (carditis; the heart shape/'O'), N = Nodules (subcutaneous), E = Erythema marginatum, S = Sydenham chorea. Minor criteria include fever, elevated ESR/CRP, arthralgia, prolonged PR interval.⭐ Diagnosis needs evidence of recent GAS infection plus criteria.
  18. 18"J" + elbow frosting: J = Joints: rheumatic fever typically causes migratory polyarthritis of large joints (knees, ankles, elbows, wrists) that is very responsive to NSAIDs/aspirin.⭐ Migratory arthritis of large joints.
  19. 19"O" (heart): O = the heart: acute rheumatic fever causes pancarditis (pericarditis, myocarditis, endocarditis), with the endocardial valvulitis typically affecting the mitral valve most (then aortic). Histology shows Aschoff bodies with Anitschkow (caterpillar) cells.⭐ Mitral regurgitation acutely; mitral stenosis chronically.
  20. 20"N" + nodular candies: N = Nodules: painless, firm subcutaneous nodules on extensor surfaces of joints (elbows, knees, wrists) and the spine.
  21. 21"E" + ring pattern: E = Erythema marginatum: a non-itchy, ring-shaped (serpiginous) pink rash with central clearing on the trunk and proximal limbs that spares the face.
  22. 22"S" cupcake falling: S = Sydenham chorea: sudden, involuntary, purposeless movements of the face, hands and feet, with emotional lability and weakness. It appears late (weeks to months, up to 6 months) after the infection.⭐ Sydenham chorea is a late manifestation (St. Vitus dance).
  23. 23Glomerular knot: Post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis (PSGN) results from immune-complex deposition in glomeruli (type III hypersensitivity) and presents with nephritic syndrome: hematuria, oliguria, hypertension, and edema.⭐ PSGN: subepithelial humps, lumpy-bumpy IF, low C3.
  24. 24Cola: Acute PSGN can present with gross hematuria, described as tea-colored, cola-colored or rust-colored urine (RBC casts on urinalysis).⭐ Cola-colored urine = nephritic syndrome.
  25. 25Later in the month: PSGN appears a few weeks (about 2-3 weeks after pharyngitis, 3-6 weeks after skin infection) after the strep infection.
  26. 26Crumbs on face + red neckerchief: Both pharyngitis (neckerchief) and skin infection (crumbs on the face = impetigo) from S. pyogenes can precede PSGN, unlike rheumatic fever which follows pharyngitis only.⭐ Rheumatic fever = pharyngitis only; PSGN = pharyngitis or skin.
  27. 27Purple pencil: Early penicillin treatment of S. pyogenes pharyngitis significantly reduces the risk of rheumatic fever. It does not reliably prevent PSGN, which can occur after pharyngitis or a skin infection even if treated.⭐ Penicillin prevents rheumatic fever but not PSGN.
  28. 28Lysed "O" doughnuts: Streptolysin O is a hemolytic toxin that lyses red cells and damages immune cells, helping the bacteria evade host defenses. It is oxygen-labile and immunogenic (antibodies form).⭐ Streptolysin O is the basis of the ASO titer.
  29. 29"P" cupcakes: Streptokinase (not a phosphate-adding kinase) is a protease-activating factor that converts plasminogen to plasmin, which degrades fibrin clots and helps the bacteria spread into deeper tissue.
  30. 30DNA twists: S. pyogenes produces DNases (streptodornase) that degrade host DNA in pus, reducing its viscosity and aiding immune evasion and spread.⭐ Anti-DNase B is useful after skin infection.
  31. 31Basset licking pastries: S. pyogenes is bacitracin-sensitive (basset hound = bacitracin) and PYR-positive, whereas S. agalactiae (group B) is bacitracin-resistant and PYR-negative.⭐ GAS: bacitracin-sensitive, PYR-positive; GBS: resistant.
  32. 32"O" doughnuts + antibody tongs: Antistreptolysin O (ASO) titers support the diagnosis of acute rheumatic fever or PSGN after pharyngeal infection. After skin infections the ASO response is blunted, so anti-DNase B titers are used to improve sensitivity.⭐ ASO + anti-DNase B document recent GAS infection.
Show the original legend picture

108. Tissue Nematodes (Dracunculus, Wuchereria, Onchocerca, Toxocara, Loa loa)

Exam snapshot
  • Clues: Dracunculus = painful leg ulcer with emerging worm; Onchocerca = river blindness plus itchy skin; Wuchereria = elephantiasis; Loa loa = Calabar swellings and worm in eye; Toxocara = visceral/ocular larva migrans.
  • All are tissue nematodes with eosinophilia; vectors: copepods (Dracunculus), black fly (Onchocerca), mosquito (Wuchereria), deer fly (Loa), dog/cat feces eggs (Toxocara).
  • Diagnosis: skin snip (Onchocerca), blood smear (Wuchereria, Loa), adult worm emergence (Dracunculus), serology/clinical (Toxocara).
  • Treatment: ivermectin (Onchocerca), diethylcarbamazine (Wuchereria, Loa), albendazole (Toxocara), supportive worm extraction (Dracunculus).
  • Buzzwords: Guinea worm, river blindness, leopard skin, elephantiasis, Calabar swelling, ocular larva migrans.
  1. 1Dracula: Dracunculus medinensis (Guinea worm), a tissue nematode.
  2. 2Water cooler: Infection occurs when humans drink water containing copepods (small crustaceans) that have ingested D. medinensis larvae.⭐ Prevention is by filtering drinking water.
  3. 3Small cups: Copepods serve as the intermediate hosts, harboring infective larvae.
  4. 4Shoelace emerging from foot: The adult female worm emerges through a painful skin ulcer, typically on the lower extremity, when the limb contacts water.⭐ Worm emerges from a blistering leg ulcer.
  5. 5Wrapped shoelace: Traditional treatment is slow extraction by winding the emerging worm around a stick over days to weeks.⭐ There is no effective drug; pulling too fast breaks the worm.
  6. 6Bilobed slingshot kid + pink granules by Dracula: Eosinophilia is a common finding in D. medinensis infection (bilobed eosinophils with bright red-orange granules), especially during larval migration.
  7. 7Fly guy: Onchocerca volvulus causes onchocerciasis (river blindness); an infected black fly (Simulium) bite injects larvae that mature into adults in the skin and release microfilariae.⭐ Black fly breeds near fast-flowing rivers.
  8. 8Covering eyes: Microfilariae in ocular tissues can cause visual impairment and ultimately blindness, a severe complication of onchocerciasis.⭐ Onchocerciasis is a leading infectious cause of blindness worldwide.
  9. 9Pink granules + fly guy: Eosinophilia is a common finding in Onchocerca volvulus infection.
  10. 10Light and dark spots: Onchodermatitis: inflammation from dying microfilariae causes pruritic dermatitis with hyperpigmented and hypopigmented patches ('leopard skin').
  11. 11Microscope: Diagnosis is by finding Onchocerca microfilariae in a skin snip (biopsy) under the microscope.⭐ Skin snip, not blood smear, is used for Onchocerca.
  12. 12No dumping, drains to river: Ivermectin is the treatment of choice for onchocerciasis, killing microfilariae.⭐ Ivermectin kills microfilariae, not adults, so repeated dosing is needed; DEC can precipitate severe reactions (Mazzotti).
  13. 13Witch: Wuchereria bancrofti, a tissue (filarial) nematode.
  14. 14Hammer pants: W. bancrofti causes lymphatic filariasis: adult worms block lymphatic vessels, causing impaired drainage, chronic inflammation, and fibrosis, leading to elephantiasis (massive lymphedema of limbs and genitals).
  15. 15Sleeves and collar ruffles: Infection can cause lymphangitis and painful lymphadenopathy from inflammation of lymphatic vessels and nodes.
  16. 16Witch coughing: Tropical pulmonary eosinophilia is a hypersensitivity reaction to microfilariae in the lungs, with nocturnal cough, wheezing, dyspnea, and marked eosinophilia.⭐ Nocturnal cough with high eosinophilia in an endemic area.
  17. 17Mosquitoes: A variety of mosquitoes (Culex, Aedes, Anopheles) transmit Wuchereria bancrofti.
  18. 18Blood smear + witch: Diagnosis is confirmed by finding microfilariae on a thick blood smear, ideally drawn at night.⭐ Microfilariae show nocturnal periodicity.
  19. 19Slingshot granules + witch: Eosinophilia is a common finding in W. bancrofti infection.
  20. 20"Diet and Carb" + witch: Diethylcarbamazine is effective against W. bancrofti, targeting microfilariae and adult worms.⭐ Doxycycline (against Wolbachia) is also used.
  21. 21Wolfman: Toxocara canis, a tissue nematode that causes toxocariasis (visceral and ocular larva migrans).
  22. 22Poop bag: Infection is acquired by ingesting food or soil contaminated with eggs from dog (or cat) feces.⭐ Think of a toddler eating dirt or a child with puppy exposure.
  23. 23Sleep mask: Ocular larva migrans results from larval migration to the eye, causing significant visual impairment or blindness.⭐ A retinal granuloma can mimic retinoblastoma.
  24. 24Slingshot granules + wolfman: Eosinophilia is a common finding in Toxocara infection, especially visceral larva migrans.
  25. 25Bent chair legs + wolfman: Albendazole treats visceral toxocariasis; ocular toxocariasis is difficult to treat and management aims to prevent further eye damage (e.g., corticosteroids).
  26. 26Lagoon swamp creature: Loa loa, a tissue (filarial) nematode (African eye worm).
  27. 27Lumps on arms and legs: Loa loa causes recurrent Calabar swellings: transient, soft, slightly red, itchy, migratory subcutaneous swellings, mainly on the extremities.⭐ Calabar swellings are transient and migratory.
  28. 28Worm in eye: Migration of the adult worm across the conjunctiva is a hallmark clinical feature.⭐ Adult worm visibly crossing the eye = Loa loa.
  29. 29Blood smear + swamp creature: Loa loa is diagnosed by microfilariae in peripheral blood smears or by adult worms seen in the conjunctiva.⭐ Microfilariae show diurnal periodicity.
  30. 30Flies: Deer flies (Chrysops) are the vectors of Loa loa.⭐ Chrysops is also called the mango fly.
  31. 31Pink granules + swamp creature: Eosinophilia is a common finding in Loa loa infection.
  32. 32"Diet and Carb" + swamp creature: Diethylcarbamazine is the treatment of choice for Loa loa, killing microfilariae and adults.⭐ Check for high microfilarial counts before treating to avoid encephalopathy.
  33. 33Bent chair legs + swamp creature: Albendazole is sometimes used when multiple courses of diethylcarbamazine fail to cure Loa loa.
Show the original legend picture

109. Toxoplasma gondii

Exam snapshot
  • Immunocompetent: asymptomatic or mononucleosis-like illness with lymphadenopathy; HIV (CD4<100): brain abscesses; congenital: triad
  • Intracellular protozoan with tachyzoites and bradyzoites in tissue cysts; definitive host is the cat
  • Diagnosis: serology (IgG/IgM), PCR; MRI/CT with multiple ring-enhancing lesions
  • Treatment: pyrimethamine + sulfadiazine + leucovorin; prophylaxis TMP-SMX for CD4<100 and IgG-positive; avoid cat litter and undercooked meat in pregnancy
  • Buzzwords: cat feces, undercooked meat, ring-enhancing lesions, chorioretinitis, hydrocephalus, intracranial calcifications
  1. 1Gandhi cat: Toxoplasma gondii is an intracellular protozoan that can infect almost any warm-blooded animal; cats are the definitive host (it can cause CNS disease in humans).
  2. 2Meaty cat food + cysts: Humans are infected by eating raw or undercooked meat containing tissue cysts (bradyzoites).
  3. 3Egg in cat litter box: Humans also become infected by ingesting oocysts shed in cat feces (e.g., changing cat litter or contaminated water/vegetables/soil).⭐ Pregnant women should avoid cat litter.
  4. 4Pregnant woman: Toxoplasma is transmitted vertically from an infected mother to the fetus across the placenta; the risk is greatest when the mother has primary infection during pregnancy.
  5. 5Torch: Toxoplasma is a TORCHeS infection: Toxoplasma, Other (VZV, parvovirus B19, Zika, HIV, HBV), Rubella, CMV, HSV and Syphilis; all are transmitted vertically.
  6. 6Immunocompromised cane: Immunocompromised patients, especially with HIV/AIDS, are at risk for severe toxoplasmosis (usually reactivation of latent cysts). Immunocompetent people are usually asymptomatic or have mild, self-limited flu-like illness with lymphadenopathy.
  7. 7Red turban: Cerebral toxoplasmosis (toxoplasmic encephalitis) occurs in severely immunocompromised patients (CD4 <100) with headache, fever, confusion, focal deficits, and imaging showing multiple ring-enhancing lesions on MRI or CT.⭐ Most common cause of brain mass lesions in AIDS.
  8. 8Multiple shiny ring-shaped lens on glasses: The ring-shaped lenses represent the multiple ring-enhancing brain lesions seen on MRI/CT in cerebral toxoplasmosis.
  9. 9Turban pin: A brain biopsy would distinguish cerebral toxoplasmosis from primary CNS lymphoma: necrotizing inflammation with tachyzoites and bradyzoites versus sheets of atypical B cells. In practice, positive Toxoplasma IgG supports the diagnosis and patients get an empiric treatment trial; biopsy is done if there is no response. CSF EBV PCR supports lymphoma.⭐ Treat empirically first; biopsy if no response in about 2 weeks.
  10. 10Cat carrying torch looking at area with cat with milk on head + water bowl on head + flash bulb: The classic triad of congenital toxoplasmosis: intracranial calcifications, hydrocephalus and chorioretinitis (milk = calcifications, water bowl = hydrocephalus, flash bulb = chorioretinitis).⭐ Triad: chorioretinitis, hydrocephalus, intracranial calcifications.
  11. 11Milk on head: Intracranial calcifications (scattered, diffuse) are deposits in brain tissue damaged by the parasite.
  12. 12Water bowl on head: Hydrocephalus occurs when inflammation obstructs CSF flow (e.g., at the aqueduct), enlarging the ventricles.
  13. 13Shaking head: Seizures can result from epileptogenic foci in damaged, calcified brain tissue and from raised intracranial pressure due to hydrocephalus.
  14. 14Flash bulb: Chorioretinitis is inflammation and necrosis of the retina and choroid, which can cause visual impairment or blindness; it is the most common manifestation of congenital toxoplasmosis.
  15. 15Deaf Beethoven: Congenital toxoplasmosis can also cause sensorineural hearing loss from direct parasitic invasion and inflammation of the cochlea and auditory pathways.
  16. 16Dyed sulfur eggs: Sulfadiazine (sulfur + 'dyed') is used with pyrimethamine as treatment for toxoplasmosis; it inhibits folate synthesis (dihydropteroate synthase).⭐ Treatment: sulfadiazine + pyrimethamine (+ leucovorin).
  17. 17Pyramid pattern: Pyrimethamine (pyramid), which inhibits dihydrofolate reductase, is the partner drug to sulfadiazine; leucovorin (folinic acid) is added to prevent bone marrow suppression.⭐ Add leucovorin to prevent pyrimethamine marrow toxicity.
  18. 18Sulphur egg by $100: The sulfur egg (sulfa drug) near the '$100' hints at TMP-SMX prophylaxis for HIV patients with a CD4 count below 100 cells/uL.⭐ TMP-SMX at CD4 <100 also covers PCP.
  19. 19"+" kite with IgG antibody key: The '+' means Toxoplasma IgG-positive. Prophylaxis with TMP-SMX is given to IgG-positive HIV patients with CD4 below 100 to prevent reactivation of latent infection (it also prevents Pneumocystis pneumonia).
  20. 20Immunocompromised cane + $100 bill: Immunocompromised patient (HIV) with a CD4 count below 100 cells/uL: start TMP-SMX prophylaxis against toxoplasmosis (if IgG positive).

110. Trematodes

Exam snapshot
  • Schistosoma: cercariae penetrate skin in freshwater; eggs with lateral spine (S. mansoni, S. japonicum) or terminal spine (S. haematobium).
  • S. mansoni/japonicum cause hepatosplenic disease, periportal fibrosis and portal hypertension; S. haematobium causes hematuria and bladder squamous cell carcinoma.
  • Clonorchis sinensis: raw freshwater fish in Asia; bile ducts, pigmented gallstones, cholangitis, cholangiocarcinoma.
  • Paragonimus westermani: raw crab/crayfish; lung fluke with cough and hemoptysis mimicking TB; operculated eggs in sputum or stool.
  • Treatment: praziquantel for all three groups (Clonorchis alternative: albendazole).
  1. 1San Franschisto Aquatic Park: Schistosoma spp. are freshwater blood flukes. Snail-released cercariae penetrate human skin during water exposure and cause schistosomiasis (intestinal, hepatic or urinary, depending on species).
  2. 2Swimmer: Cercariae are free-swimming larvae in contaminated freshwater lakes, rivers and ponds (Africa, Asia, South America), so swimming, wading or bathing puts people at risk. Skin penetration is followed by migration to the liver.
  3. 3Snails by fish: Freshwater snails are the intermediate hosts for Schistosoma and release the infectious cercariae. Snail control and avoiding contaminated water help prevent infection.
  4. 4Liver-shaped coral: After skin penetration, larvae enter the blood and mature into adult worms in the portal venous system of the liver. Acute infection may cause Katayama fever (fever, urticaria, eosinophilia).
  5. 5Blue fish swimming towards liver-shaped coral: Normal portal flow carries blood from the gut toward the liver. This sets up the direction the worms then reverse.
  6. 6Swimming opposite direction: Adult worms migrate against portal flow into the mesenteric (or vesical) venules, where they lay eggs. The eggs, not the worms, cause disease via granulomatous inflammation.
  7. 7Merman statue: Schistosoma mansoni (Africa, Caribbean, South America) lives in the mesenteric veins and causes intestinal and hepatosplenic disease.
  8. 8Lateral fin + merman statue: S. mansoni eggs have a prominent lateral spine, seen on stool microscopy.⭐ Mansoni = lateral spine. Eggs are seen in stool.
  9. 9Japanese tourist: Schistosoma japonicum (East Asia) lives in the mesenteric veins and causes intestinal and hepatosplenic disease.
  10. 10Round fish + japanese tourist: S. japonicum eggs are round with a tiny, inconspicuous lateral spine. Eggs are seen in stool.
  11. 11Aquarium swimmer: Swimmer's itch (cercarial dermatitis) is a transient allergic skin reaction to cercariae of bird schistosomes. The larvae die in the skin because humans are not their host, so there is no systemic disease.
  12. 12Porthole crack/degraded liver coral: Chronic egg deposition causes granulomas, periportal ('pipestem') fibrosis and cirrhosis, leading to presinusoidal portal hypertension with splenomegaly and varices. Liver function is often relatively preserved.⭐ Schisto = presinusoidal portal hypertension (hepatosplenomegaly, varices).
  13. 13Yellow coat: Hepatic schistosomiasis can cause jaundice and cholestasis because egg-induced periportal inflammation and fibrosis obstruct bile flow.
  14. 14Swordfish with terminal bill: Schistosoma haematobium (Africa, Middle East) lives in the vesical venous plexus around the bladder.
  15. 15Terminal bill: S. haematobium eggs have a large terminal spine and are found in urine, not stool.⭐ Haematobium = terminal spine, eggs in urine.
  16. 16Bleeding jellyfish: S. haematobium causes urinary schistosomiasis, often with terminal hematuria, dysuria and later obstructive uropathy.
  17. 17Crab + swordfish: Chronic S. haematobium infection is associated with squamous cell carcinoma of the bladder (not the usual urothelial carcinoma).⭐ S. haematobium = squamous cell carcinoma of the bladder.
  18. 18Pretzel + fish: Praziquantel is the treatment of choice for all Schistosoma species. It acts by increasing calcium permeability and paralyzing the worm.
  19. 19Orca: Clonorchis sinensis is the Chinese (Asian) liver fluke, found in East Asia.
  20. 20Snails + orca: Snails are the first intermediate host (releasing cercariae that encyst in freshwater fish). Humans are infected by eating undercooked or raw freshwater fish.
  21. 21Seagull: Adult flukes reside in the bile ducts. Eggs and worms cause chronic inflammation, ductal dilatation, mechanical obstruction and thickening of the biliary walls.
  22. 22Sclerosed rope: Chronic infection can lead to sclerosing cholangitis (recurrent pyogenic cholangitis), with inflammation and destruction of bile ducts.
  23. 23Crab + orca: Clonorchis is a known risk factor for cholangiocarcinoma, a malignancy of the biliary epithelium.⭐ Clonorchis = cholangiocarcinoma.
  24. 24Black rocks + seagulls: Infection can cause pigmented gallstones (bile stasis and bilirubin precipitation), which can lead to biliary obstruction and cholangitis.
  25. 25"Capped" seagull eggs: Clonorchis eggs are small and operculated (capped), seen on stool exam. Eggs have a lid and a shouldering rim.
  26. 26Pretzel + orca: Praziquantel is the treatment of choice, with albendazole as an alternative.
  27. 27Penguins: Paragonimus westermani is the (Japanese/Asian) lung fluke, endemic to Asia and parts of Africa and Latin America.
  28. 28Red speckled lung spots: Flukes encyst in the lung and cause chronic cough, hemoptysis (rusty sputum) and cystic lesions. It can mimic tuberculosis, so consider it in endemic-area patients with TB-like symptoms and a negative TB workup.⭐ Hemoptysis + eosinophilia in a patient from Asia: think Paragonimus, not TB.
  29. 29Snails + penguins: Snails are the first intermediate host, releasing cercariae that encyst in crustaceans. Humans are infected by eating them.
  30. 30Crab legs: Infection is acquired by eating raw, undercooked or pickled freshwater crabs or crayfish containing metacercariae.
  31. 31"Capped" penguin eggs: Paragonimus eggs are operculated (capped) and found in sputum or stool, because swallowed sputum passes into stool.
  32. 32Pretzel + penguins: Praziquantel is the drug of choice for Paragonimus westermani.
Show the original legend picture

111. Treponema pallidum

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: painless genital chancre (primary), palm and sole rash/condyloma lata (secondary), gummas, aortitis, tabes dorsalis, Argyll Robertson pupils (tertiary); stillborn/rhinitis/Hutchinson teeth (congenital)
  • Spirochete, too thin for Gram stain, not culturable; visualized by dark-field microscopy
  • Dx: nontreponemal screen (VDRL/RPR) then confirm with treponemal test (FTA-ABS, TP-PA); VDRL titer tracks treatment response
  • Treatment: IM benzathine penicillin G for all stages (IV penicillin G for neurosyphilis); desensitize if allergic, especially in pregnancy
  • Buzzwords: chancre, condyloma lata, tree-barking aorta, Argyll Robertson pupil, saber shins, Hutchinson teeth, Jarisch-Herxheimer
  1. 1Pale spiral galaxy: Treponema pallidum is a spirochete that causes the sexually transmitted infection syphilis.
  2. 2Spiral staircase: T. pallidum is a spiral-shaped spirochete that causes syphilis.
  3. 3"Darkfield galaxy": Dark-field microscopy allows direct visualization of T. pallidum, but it needs a sample taken directly from a lesion and is not commonly used.⭐ Used for chancre or condyloma lata fluid; not for routine screening.
  4. 4"VDRL" on screen: VDRL is a nontreponemal test that detects antibodies to cardiolipin-lecithin-cholesterol complexes; it is a convenient, nonspecific screening test that can give false positives. RPR is another nontreponemal test that detects the same antibodies and is used for screening and for monitoring treatment response.⭐ Nontreponemal titers fall with successful treatment.
  5. 5"MONO, RF, SLE, LEP, DRG": Conditions that can cause a false-positive VDRL include mononucleosis, rheumatic fever (RF), systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), leprosy and drugs (IV drug use).⭐ Mnemonic: MONO, RF, SLE, LEP, DRG (also pregnancy and other viral infections).
  6. 6"Field Telescope A-Ab": A positive VDRL/RPR is usually followed by a specific treponemal test, such as the FTA-ABS (fluorescent treponemal antibody absorption) test, to confirm syphilis.⭐ Treponemal tests (FTA-ABS) usually remain positive for life.
  7. 71st level: Syphilis is divided into early (primary and secondary), latent (asymptomatic) and late (tertiary) stages, each with distinct clinical manifestations; this level represents primary syphilis.
  8. 82nd level: Syphilis is divided into early (primary and secondary), latent and late (tertiary) stages; this level represents secondary syphilis.
  9. 9Late night outside: Syphilis is divided into early (primary and secondary), latent and late (tertiary) stages; the outside represents late (tertiary) syphilis.
  10. 10Poked in butt + 1st level: In primary syphilis, a painless chancre appears at the site of inoculation (genitals or anus); it heals spontaneously in about 3-6 weeks.⭐ Painless, indurated ulcer with clean base, unlike painful chancroid.
  11. 11Solar system model + 2nd level: Secondary syphilis is a systemic disease with rash, fever, lymphadenopathy and mucous membrane lesions.
  12. 12Red gloves and boots + 2nd level: A widespread maculopapular rash that often involves the palms and soles is characteristic of secondary syphilis.⭐ Rash on palms and soles: secondary syphilis, RMSF, coxsackie.
  13. 13Planet with a lot of bumps + 2nd level: Condyloma lata are flat-topped, moist, highly infectious lesions of secondary syphilis on the genitals, perineum, perianal region, axillae and inner thighs.⭐ Not to be confused with HPV condyloma acuminata.
  14. 14Looking at planet with a lot of bumps through telescopes: Live T. pallidum spirochetes can be seen within condyloma lata by dark-field microscopy.
  15. 15Cratered moon + outside: Gummas are noninfectious, soft tumor-like lesions with firm necrotic centers that form in tertiary syphilis from chronic granulomatous inflammation; they can occur in skin, bone, liver or other organs and cause local tissue destruction.
  16. 16Aortic tree + outside: Syphilitic aortitis in tertiary syphilis produces "tree-barking" of the aorta and can lead to an ascending aortic (aortic root) aneurysm.⭐ Aortic root dilation can lead to aortic regurgitation.
  17. 17Tree roots + outside: Tertiary syphilitic aortitis is caused by endarteritis and destruction of the vasa vasorum (small vessels supplying the aortic wall) by T. pallidum.
  18. 18Damaged posterior columns + outside: Tabes dorsalis, a form of late neurosyphilis, is degeneration of the dorsal columns and dorsal roots, causing impaired proprioception and vibration sense, progressive sensory ataxia, lancinating pains and loss of deep tendon reflexes.⭐ Wide-based gait, positive Romberg, and Charcot joints.
  19. 19Argyle sweater + light in eyes + outside: Argyll Robertson pupils accommodate but do not react to light ("prostitute's pupil"); they are a highly specific sign of neurosyphilis (tertiary), caused by midbrain (pretectal) damage that disrupts the light reflex while sparing accommodation.⭐ ARP: Accommodation Reflex Present, Pupillary Response Absent.
  20. 20Orion's constellation: Congenital syphilis is transmitted from an infected mother to the fetus and causes a constellation of findings: early (first 2 years, mainly systemic and mucocutaneous) and late (after 2 years, skeletal, dental and ocular stigmata).⭐ Can cause stillbirth and hydrops; screen all pregnant women.
  21. 21Sabre by shins + outside: Saber shins are anteriorly bowed tibias in late congenital syphilis, from periosteal inflammation and abnormal bone growth; usually bilateral.
  22. 22Saddle + outside: Saddle nose, collapse of the nasal bridge from cartilage destruction, is a feature of late congenital syphilis.⭐ Early congenital syphilis may present with snuffles (rhinitis).
  23. 23Chattering teeth + outside: Hutchinson teeth (blunted, notched incisors) and mulberry molars (abnormal enamel on molar cusps) are features of late congenital syphilis.
  24. 24Earmuffs + outside: Congenital syphilis can cause sensorineural hearing loss in both early (birth to 2 years) and late (after 2 years) stages.⭐ Hutchinson triad: notched incisors, interstitial keratitis, 8th nerve deafness.
  25. 25Purple pencil + 1st level: Intramuscular benzathine penicillin G is the preferred treatment for all stages of syphilis, including primary; in allergic patients, desensitize.⭐ Penicillin is the only treatment recommended in pregnancy.
  26. 26Purple pencil + 2nd level: Intramuscular benzathine penicillin G is the preferred treatment for secondary syphilis; in allergic patients, desensitize.
  27. 27Purple pencil + outside: Benzathine penicillin G is the preferred treatment for late (tertiary) syphilis, with desensitization in allergic patients; neurosyphilis requires IV aqueous penicillin G.
  28. 28"Jarisch-Herxheimer comet viewing": The Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction is triggered by rapid lysis of T. pallidum after starting antibiotics, with release of endotoxin-like products and cytokines.⭐ Most common with treatment of early syphilis.
  29. 29Fiery + icy comet: The Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction causes fever and chills, sometimes headache, myalgia and worsening of the rash, within hours of treatment.⭐ Supportive care and antipyretics; not a penicillin allergy.

112. Trichomonas vaginalis (Flagellated protozoan)

Exam snapshot
  • Clue: sexually active woman with foul-smelling, frothy yellow-green discharge, vulvovaginal itching and dysuria; strawberry cervix.
  • Flagellated protozoan with an undulating membrane; trophozoite only (no cyst); infects squamous epithelium.
  • Diagnosis: wet mount showing motile pear-shaped trophozoites with jerky motility; vaginal pH above 4.5; NAAT.
  • Treatment: metronidazole or tinidazole for patient and all sexual partners.
  • Buzzwords: strawberry cervix, frothy discharge, tumbling motility, pH greater than 4.5, no cyst.
  1. 1Magic trichs: Trichomonas vaginalis is a unicellular protozoan parasite.
  2. 2Magic scarves: It is a motile organism with four anterior flagella and an undulating membrane.
  3. 3Trophy: T. vaginalis exists only as a trophozoite; it has no cyst form (so it survives poorly outside the host).⭐ No cyst form means transmission is almost exclusively sexual.
  4. 4Couple kissing: Trichomonas vaginalis is a sexually transmitted infection of the urogenital tract.
  5. 5Cobblestone sidewalk: T. vaginalis has a tropism for squamous epithelium and causes infection and inflammation of the vulva, vagina, cervix, and urethra.
  6. 6Smiling delivery man: Asymptomatic carriage, primarily in men, contributes to spread; treating sexual partners reduces this risk.
  7. 7Vaginal flowers: T. vaginalis causes vulvovaginitis: inflammation of the vulva and vagina with erythema, burning, itching, and discharge.
  8. 8Burning trash can: Burning sensation (dysuria and vulvovaginal irritation) is a feature of the vulvovaginitis.
  9. 9Itching dog: Pruritus is a feature of the vulvovaginitis.
  10. 10Leaking fluid: Vaginal discharge is a feature of the vulvovaginitis.
  11. 11Frothy green puddles: Trichomonas causes a classically copious, yellow-green, frothy, malodorous vaginal discharge.⭐ Frothy, malodorous green discharge is the classic descriptor.
  12. 12Strawberry hat trick: Cervical inflammation with erythema, edema, and punctate hemorrhages gives the 'strawberry cervix' (colpitis macularis).⭐ Strawberry cervix is specific but seen in a minority of cases.
  13. 13$4.50 and up: Trichomonas raises vaginal pH (basic environment), so pH testing shows 4.5 or greater.⭐ pH above 4.5 is also seen in bacterial vaginosis; BV has clue cells and a positive whiff test.
  14. 14Leaking package: In men, infection is usually asymptomatic but may cause urethritis with dysuria and a thin white discharge.
  15. 15Pear puddle: Saline wet mount shows motile, pear-shaped trophozoites with a characteristic jerky, tumbling motility.⭐ Immediate wet mount is the fastest diagnostic test; NAAT is more sensitive.
  16. 16Metro: Metronidazole is effective against Trichomonas vaginalis.⭐ Warn about the disulfiram-like reaction with alcohol.
  17. 17Metro couple: All sexual partners must be treated to prevent reinfection and further transmission; therapy of choice is metronidazole (or tinidazole).
  18. 18Condom hat: Consistent barrier contraception (condoms) prevents re-transmission until treatment is complete.
Show the original legend picture

113. Trypanosoma brucei

Exam snapshot
  • African sleeping sickness: tsetse bite chancre, intermittent fever, lymphadenopathy, then somnolence and coma
  • Protozoan with single flagellum, kinetoplast and undulating membrane; variable surface glycoprotein (VSG) antigenic variation
  • Diagnosis: blood smear (trypomastigotes) and CSF exam via lumbar puncture for staging
  • Treatment depends on species and stage: pentamidine or suramin (stage 1); NECT or melarsoprol (stage 2); tsetse control
  • Buzzwords: tsetse fly, Winterbottom sign, antigenic variation, undulating fever, chancre, sleeping sickness
  1. 1Prince Bruce: Trypanosoma brucei is the protozoan that causes African sleeping sickness (African trypanosomiasis); 'Prince Bruce' = brucei.
  2. 2Sleeping princess: The sleeping princess marks the disease: African sleeping sickness, which has two human subspecies.
  3. 33 Feathers in Prince Bruce's cap: T. brucei has three subspecies: T. b. gambiense and T. b. rhodesiense infect humans; T. b. brucei does not infect humans and is used as a research model.
  4. 4Feather's stem: T. brucei is motile with a single flagellum arising near the kinetoplast and attached to the cell by an undulating membrane.
  5. 5Variable peacock cloak: T. brucei has a variable surface glycoprotein (VSG) coat that undergoes constant antigenic variation, allowing escape from antibody-mediated immunity and causing waves of parasitemia and fever. This is why vaccines are not available.⭐ VSG antigenic variation causes recurrent fever waves.
  6. 6Gambia spot on map: T. b. gambiense is carried mainly by humans (anthroponotic): transmitted person-to-person by the tsetse fly. It causes a slowly progressive disease lasting months to years.⭐ Gambiense = chronic, human reservoir.
  7. 7Rhodesiense spot on map: T. b. rhodesiense is zoonotic; antelope and cattle are the main reservoirs, and humans are infected by tsetse bites. It causes a more acute, rapidly progressive disease.⭐ Rhodesiense = acute, zoonotic.
  8. 8Tea fly: The vector is the tsetse fly (Glossina), which injects metacyclic trypomastigotes. In the blood they become replicating bloodstream trypomastigotes that multiply by binary fission in blood and lymph: the first (hemolymphatic) stage.⭐ Vector: tsetse fly (Glossina).
  9. 9Gambia stream: T. b. gambiense occurs in West and Central Africa, in riverine and forested areas where tsetse flies live near streams and vegetation.
  10. 10Trees: T. b. rhodesiense occurs in the savannas and woodlands of East (and Southern) Africa.
  11. 11Antelope mark: T. b. rhodesiense is zoonotic with wild game such as antelope as main reservoir (also cattle).
  12. 12Withering map near Gambiense: T. b. gambiense causes a slowly progressive chronic infection (withering), with CNS involvement often months to years later.
  13. 13Torn map near Rhodesiense: T. b. rhodesiense causes a severe, rapidly progressive, acutely fatal illness, with CNS involvement within weeks if untreated.
  14. 14Great lake sore: A trypanosomal chancre may form at the tsetse bite site: a red, raised, painful nodule or ulcer with regional lymphadenopathy (more common with rhodesiense).
  15. 15Dripping red ink: After inoculation, trypanosomes spread from the skin to the blood and lymphatics: the first (hemolymphatic) stage. It progresses slowly over months to years with gambiense and rapidly over days to weeks with rhodesiense.
  16. 16Flame headband: The first (hemolymphatic) stage is a nonspecific febrile illness with headache, malaise, weakness, pruritus and lymphadenopathy.
  17. 17Intermittent spikes on crown: Stage 1 patients have characteristic intermittent, undulating fever: waves of parasitemia caused by antigenic variation, separated by afebrile periods lasting days to weeks.⭐ Fever waves = antigenic variation of VSG.
  18. 18String of pearls around neck: Cervical lymphadenopathy, particularly enlarged posterior cervical nodes (Winterbottom sign), is typical of gambiense in the early stage; axillary nodes may also be enlarged.⭐ Winterbottom sign = posterior cervical lymphadenopathy.
  19. 19Sleep mask: The second (meningoencephalitic/neurologic) stage involves the CNS: daytime sleepiness with nocturnal insomnia (disrupted sleep-wake cycle), mood changes, psychomotor slowing, tremors, seizures and eventually coma and death.⭐ CNS stage = sleep-wake reversal, then coma.
  20. 20TrypomastiGOAT + blood spot: On blood smear, trypomastigotes are slender, slightly curved, with a central nucleus, a posterior kinetoplast, an undulating membrane and a flagellum. Serum IgM is elevated (and is also seen in the CSF).⭐ Diagnose by blood smear; IgM elevated.
  21. 21Spine splinter: Lumbar puncture and CSF analysis allow definitive staging: stage 1 has few WBCs and no organisms; stage 2 has a high WBC count (lymphocytes), elevated protein, organisms present, and morula cells of Mott (plasma cells with IgM inclusions).⭐ Stage by CSF analysis; drug choice depends on stage.
  22. 22Fogger made of jug of water and wicks: Control relies on reducing tsetse flies: insecticide spraying, habitat management and traps/foggers (here jugs of water with wicks releasing insecticide mist). Vaccines are not available.
  23. 23Pentagon around Gambia: Pentamidine treats first-stage T. b. gambiense. It does not cross the blood-brain barrier, so it is ineffective in the CNS stage.⭐ Pentamidine: does not cross the BBB.
  24. 24Serum: Suramin treats first-stage T. b. rhodesiense. It also does not cross the BBB, so it is used only before CNS involvement.⭐ Suramin = stage 1 rhodesiense.
  25. 25Hummingbird feeder filled with nectar near Gambia: NECT (nifurtimox-eflornithine combination therapy) treats second-stage T. b. gambiense; both drugs penetrate the CNS and clear parasites in the meningoencephalitic phase (the 'nectar' = NECT).⭐ NECT = stage 2 gambiense.
  26. 26Soap: Melarsoprol, an arsenic-based drug, is reserved for second-stage T. b. rhodesiense because it crosses the blood-brain barrier and treats the CNS stage.⭐ Melarsoprol = stage 2 rhodesiense (crosses BBB).
  27. 27Toxic soap: Melarsoprol is very toxic: it can cause a reactive encephalopathy (fatal in about 5-10%), so it is avoided when alternatives exist; thus NECT is preferred for gambiense stage 2.⭐ Melarsoprol causes reactive encephalopathy.
Show the original legend picture

114. Trypanosoma cruzi

Exam snapshot
  • Clinical clue: patient from Latin America with heart failure/arrhythmia or megacolon/megaesophagus, or acute periorbital swelling (Romana sign)
  • Protozoan, vector = triatomine (kissing, reduviid) bug defecating at bite site; also transfusion, congenital
  • Dx: acute - trypomastigotes on blood smear; chronic - serology (and PCR)
  • Treatment: benznidazole (first line) or nifurtimox
  • Buzzwords: Chagas disease, Romana sign, dilated cardiomyopathy, megacolon, megaesophagus, amastigotes in cardiac muscle
  1. 1PanAmericas Cruise Line: Trypanosoma cruzi is a protozoan parasite found in the Americas and causes Chagas disease.
  2. 2Coach's gas: Chagas disease (American trypanosomiasis) is a vector-borne parasitic infection caused by T. cruzi that can affect the heart, gastrointestinal tract and other organs.
  3. 3Peacock feather logo: T. cruzi appears long and thin on a blood smear, often C- or S-shaped, with an undulating membrane and a single flagellum with a large kinetoplast.
  4. 4TRI atom logo: The triatomine bug (reduviid or kissing bug) is the vector of T. cruzi.⭐ Bites near the face, often at night.
  5. 5A Master Goat yoga pose: Amastigotes are the replicative forms of T. cruzi.
  6. 6Stay WITHIN and the fun will begin: Amastigotes replicate intracellularly.⭐ A in amastigote = "A for Assimilated inside the cell".
  7. 7Triple Master Goat yoga pose: Trypomastigotes are the non-replicating, infective, extracellular forms of T. cruzi; they circulate in the blood, are detectable by microscopy, and are crescent-shaped and flagellated.
  8. 8Cruise destination map: T. cruzi is endemic in Latin America, with cases extending from the southern U.S. to Argentina and Chile.
  9. 9Substandard zone: Substandard housing (cracks in mud or thatch walls) raises the risk of vector exposure and transmission.
  10. 10Sipping red punch: T. cruzi can be transmitted by blood transfusion (and organ transplantation).
  11. 11Vertical slide: T. cruzi can be transmitted vertically (mother to fetus).
  12. 12Child on cruise: Many T. cruzi infections occur in childhood, including congenital Chagas disease, which is an acute-phase infection with high parasitemia.
  13. 13Pooping on Triple Master Goat: The triatomine bug bites to take a blood meal and then defecates near the bite; trypomastigotes in the feces enter through the wound, mucous membranes or conjunctiva when the bite is scratched or rubbed.⭐ The bite wound is not the source; the feces are.
  14. 14Muscle-bound A Master Goat yogi: Trypomastigotes enter host cells (especially muscle and nervous tissue) and transform into amastigotes that replicate intracellularly.
  15. 15Heart + crop top: T. cruzi has tropism for myocardium and the myenteric plexus of the gastrointestinal tract.
  16. 16Triple Master yogi's flame bandana: Acute infection is usually mild, with fever, malaise and nonspecific symptoms lasting about 8-12 weeks.⭐ Most acute infections are asymptomatic or nonspecific.
  17. 17A Master yogi's swollen eye: A chagoma is an inflammatory nodule at the parasite entry site; when T. cruzi enters through the conjunctiva, the unilateral painless periorbital edema is called the Romana sign (firm, red, swollen, may contain edema, monocytes and intracellular amastigotes).⭐ Romana sign = unilateral periorbital swelling; a classic Chagas buzzword.
  18. 18Reflected Triple Master Goat pose: Acute-phase Chagas disease is diagnosed by seeing trypomastigotes on a blood smear (also PCR).⭐ Chronic disease is diagnosed by serology, since parasitemia is low.
  19. 19Baby coach's gas: Congenital Chagas disease is part of the acute phase with high parasitemia; findings can include low birth weight, hepatosplenomegaly, jaundice or edema, though many infants are asymptomatic.
  20. 20Pallid shirt: Anemia can occur in congenital Chagas disease, likely from high parasitemia, inflammation and effects on red cell production.
  21. 21Liver and spleen pouch: Hepatosplenomegaly can occur in congenital Chagas disease, likely from high parasitemia and immune-mediated organ enlargement.
  22. 22Grandfather clock: The chronic indeterminate phase is a 10-30 year asymptomatic period during which patients are seropositive.⭐ About 20-30% progress to symptomatic chronic disease.
  23. 23Inflated heart pool + ECG tracing + clumped sticks: Chronic cardiac Chagas disease causes dilated cardiomyopathy with myocardial hypertrophy, heart failure, arrhythmias and thromboembolism (apical aneurysm with mural thrombus), from parasite-mediated injury and chronic inflammation.⭐ Chagas is a leading cause of dilated cardiomyopathy in Latin America; right bundle branch block is common.
  24. 24MEGA drainage pipe: Chronic T. cruzi infection can cause megacolon and megaesophagus with dysmotility, from destruction of myenteric plexus neurons (fibrosis).⭐ Megaesophagus resembles achalasia (dysphagia).
  25. 25Bug spraying: Insecticides are a key preventive measure to control triatomine (reduviid, kissing bug) populations and reduce transmission.
  26. 26Under renovation: Improving substandard housing is a key preventive measure to reduce triatomine infestation and Chagas transmission.
  27. 27TV screen: Vertical transmission is prevented by screening programs for women of childbearing age.
  28. 28Forward BEND pose: Benznidazole is used to treat acute or early chronic Chagas disease; for women identified through screening it can also help prevent congenital Chagas disease if given before a desired pregnancy.⭐ Benznidazole is contraindicated in pregnancy, so treat before conceiving.
  29. 29Spilled milk: During acute Chagas disease, women are advised to avoid breastfeeding because circulating parasites could potentially be transmitted to the infant.
  30. 30FUR MOCcasins: Nifurtimox is an approved treatment for Chagas disease (acute, reactivated, and children with chronic infection) but is second line to benznidazole.⭐ Nifurtimox is also used for sleeping sickness (with eflornithine) in West African disease.
